        ],
        notes = { definition = "Tailwind ships with a giant catalogue of utility classes, but most of them map to one CSS declaration. Once you learn to read a class name as the declaration it stands for, the catalogue becomes a list of labelled dials: turn `float-right` and the element pins right; turn `backface-hidden` and the back face of a 3D-rotated element disappears; turn `bg-cover` and the background image fills the box. The rest of this chapter is a reference for a cluster of those dials, plus the tooling that makes writing them bearable.  The first concept, the \"What's new in Tailwind CSS?\" YouTube series, is a 12-video walkthrough Tailwind published alongside early v1.x releases. It covers Grid, Transitions, Transforms, Space-between, Divide utilities, Color opacity, PurgeCSS, Animations, Reduced motion, Gradients, and `@apply` with complex classes. Treat the playlist as a video companion to the docs: watch the episode for the feature you need, then come back to the text reference here for the exact class names.", mental_model = "A Tailwind utility is a CSS declaration wearing a name tag. `bg-blue-500` is `background-color: rgb(59 130 246);`. `basis-1/2` is `flex-basis: 50%;`. `flex-col` is `flex-direction: column;`. You compose a design by stacking name tags on an element, and the browser applies the declarations they stand for.  Five dials, five declarations. The container is a flex row with a `1rem` gutter on a slate background. Each child takes half the main axis at minimum and grows to fill whatever space is left.", code_example = "<div className=\"flex flex-row gap-4 bg-slate-100 p-4\">\n  <span className=\"basis-1/2 grow\">Left</span>\n  <span className=\"basis-1/2 grow\">Right</span>\n</div>", pitfalls = "React warns in the console: `Warning: Invalid DOM property 'class'. Did you mean the className?` and ignores the class. The text never goes red. Every Tailwind snippet in the docs uses `class`; rename it to `className` in JSX.  `backdrop-hue-rotate-*` and `backdrop-invert-*` only do something visible when there is something *behind* the element. A `backdrop-invert` card on a plain white page inverts nothing because there is nothing to invert. The element needs a translucent background (`bg-white/30`) over an image, gradient, or coloured sibling, otherwise the effect is invisible.  `backface-hidden` is a no-op on its own. The 3D rotation has to be on a parent with a `perspective-*` utility (or arbitrary `perspective: ...px`) for the browser to draw the back face at all. Without perspective the element rotates in 2D and the back face never exists, so the utility is never triggered.  `h-screen` is `height: 100vh;` always. `block-screen` is `block-size: 100vh;`, which becomes `width: 100vh;` in a vertical writing mode. If your app ships in a vertical language, prefer `block-*` so the layout flips with the text direction.  `basis-1/2` only sets the *starting* size. With the default `flex-grow: 1` and a `flex-1` child elsewhere, the basis gets ignored when the container has spare space. Reach for `grow-0` on the item you want to keep at exactly half, or `basis-1/2` on a container whose children are not set to grow." }
      },

        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },

        ],
        notes = { definition = "A Tailwind utility is a class name that maps to exactly one CSS declaration. `float-left` sets `float: left`. `font-bold` sets `font-weight: 700`. `gap-4` sets a gutter. You do not write CSS; you compose class names on the element, and the browser applies the declarations they stand for.  This chapter is a working reference for a cluster of layout and typography utilities you reach for constantly: `float`, the four font utilities (`font-smoothing`, `font-style`, `font-weight`), and the grid gutter and placement utilities (`gap`, `grid-auto-columns`, `grid-auto-rows`, `grid-template-rows`, `grid-column`, `grid-row`). It closes with the install recipes for five non-Vite frameworks, so you can drop Tailwind into whatever project shows up next.", mental_model = "Think of a Tailwind class as a labelled dial. Turning the `float-right` dial pushes an element to the right edge of its container and lets the following text wrap around it. Turning the `font-bold` dial cranks the weight to 700. Each dial does one thing. You get a layout by turning several dials at once, all through the element's class list.  In HTML you would write `class=\"float-right\"`. In React you write `className=\"float-right\"`, because `class` is a reserved word in JavaScript and JSX renames the attribute. That single rename is the only translation you need: every Tailwind class name is identical between the docs and your JSX.", code_example = "// HTML from the Tailwind docs:      <p class=\"font-bold\">\n// The same thing in a React component:\n<p className=\"font-bold\">The quick brown fox.</p>", pitfalls = "React warns in the console: `Warning: Invalid DOM property 'class'. Did you mean 'className'?` The bold never applies. Every Tailwind snippet you copy from the docs uses `class`; rename it to `className` in JSX.  `col-span-2`, `row-span-2`, `auto-cols-max`, and friends only take effect when the parent has `display: grid`. Add `grid grid-cols-3` (or `flex` for `gap`) to the parent. Nothing errors; the layout just silently ignores the placement classes.  `gap` needs a flex or grid container. On a plain block container the children stack with no gutter and `gap-4` is inert. Add `flex` or `grid` to the parent first.  `grid-cols-3` defines three explicit columns; `auto-cols-max` sizes the *implicit* ones that appear when content overflows. If you set `auto-cols-max` but every item fits inside your explicit `grid-cols-*`, nothing changes, because no implicit tracks were created. Reach for `auto-cols-*` / `auto-rows-*` only with `grid-flow-col` / `grid-flow-row` or overflowing content.  A floated element is out of normal flow, so a short container can collapse to zero height around a tall float. In a component that means a parent `<div>` that looks empty. Modern layouts prefer flex or grid over floats for structure; keep `float` for its original job, wrapping text around an inline image." }
      },

    local_chapter_file = "003-install-tailwind-css-in-meteor-postcss-plugin.md",
    key_concepts = ["The mental model", "Install Tailwind in Meteor", "Install Tailwind in Next.js", "Predict the render", "Utility reference", "Common mistakes"],
    total_duration_seconds = 318,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzM6VjE6MDAzLWlu",
        raw_key = "S5:C3:V1",
        title = "Vibe Coding Common Web Patterns into Tailwind CSS",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/vibe-coding-common-web-patterns-into-tailwind-css/introduction-to-converting-common-web-patterns-25827004",
        duration_text = "03m00s",
        duration_seconds = 180,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Install Tailwind CSS in Meteor (PostCSS plugin), examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Vibe Coding Common Web Patterns into Tailwind CSS",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "Tailwind drops into a Meteor project the same way it drops into any PostCSS-based stack: install the package, register the plugin, import the CSS, start the server. There is no Meteor-specific wrapper. The recipe, plus a worked example installing the same plugin in a Next.js project, is the first half of this chapter. The second half is a working reference for a cluster of utility classes you reach for constantly: `line-clamp`, `max-w-*`, `min-h-*`, `min-w-*`, `object-fit`, `opacity`, `outline-color`, `outline-style`, `overflow`, `padding`, `position`, and `resize`.", mental_model = "A PostCSS plugin transforms a CSS file before the browser sees it. `@tailwindcss/postcss` is a transformer that takes a single `@import \"tailwindcss\";` line and emits the full preflight reset plus every utility class that the build can reach from your source files. Meteor's build pipeline already runs PostCSS on your CSS assets, so registering the plugin turns the CSS entry into a Tailwind entry with no extra tooling.  In React, every utility class is identical between the HTML docs and your JSX. The only translation is the attribute name: `class` in HTML becomes `className` in JSX because `class` is reserved.", code_example = "// HTML from the Tailwind docs:     <h1 class=\"text-3xl font-bold underline\">\n// The same thing in a React component:\n<h1 className=\"text-3xl font-bold underline\">Hello world!</h1>", pitfalls = "React warns in the console: `Warning: Invalid DOM property 'class'. Did you mean 'className'?`. The underline never applies. Every Tailwind snippet you copy from the docs uses `class`; rename it to `className` in JSX.  If the import is not in a file the build pulls in, none of the utility classes are emitted. The plugin has no source to scan. Symptom: every `className=` value is rendered, but the element looks unstyled. Fix: add the import to the entry CSS file the framework already loads.  Meteor and Next.js both look for the PostCSS config at the project root. A config inside `src/` or `app/` is ignored. The plugin registers, but PostCSS uses its default empty plugin list. Symptom: `tailwindcss` is installed but the build pipeline does not run it. Fix: move `postcss.config.mjs` to the root.  `object-fit` only applies to `<img>`, `<video>`, `<picture>`, and friends. To cover a non-replaced element, use `bg-cover` instead.  The browser draws a default focus ring unless you opt out. `outline-none` opts out and gives you nothing in return. Replace it with `outline-hidden` (which keeps a transparent outline for forced-colors) plus a custom ring, or with `focus-visible:outline-2 focus-visible:outline-blue-500`." }
      },

  {
    id = "05-004",
    number = 4,
    title = "scale utilities",
    local_chapter_file = "004-scale-utilities.md",
    key_concepts = ["`scale`: growing and shrinking", "`scroll-behavior`: instant or animated programmatic scrolling", "`skew`: slanting an element", "`stroke`: SVG outline colour", "Tailwind v4 browser targets", "Installing Tailwind in non-Vite stacks"],
    total_duration_seconds = 197,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzQ6VjE6MDA0LXNj",
        raw_key = "S5:C4:V1",
        title = "Meet Tailwind CSS",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/meet-tailwind-css",
        duration_text = "00m44s",
        duration_seconds = 44,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of scale utilities, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "A Tailwind utility is a class name that maps to exactly one CSS declaration. `scale-110` sets `transform: scale(1.10)`. `skew-y-3` sets `transform: skewY(3deg)`. `scroll-smooth` sets `scroll-behavior: smooth`. You do not write the CSS by hand; you compose class names on the element and the browser applies the declarations they stand for.  This chapter is a working reference for four transform-flavoured utilities you reach for when building interactive UI (`scale`, `scroll-behavior`, `skew`, `stroke`), plus the tooling that ships alongside them: Tailwind v4's browser targets, the install recipes for non-Vite stacks, the VS Code IntelliSense extension, the major version feature history (v1.9, v2, v3, v4), and the \"From Zero to Production\" screencast series.", mental_model = "These four utilities are the cheap-interactive-UI toolkit. A button that pops on hover is `hover:scale-110 transition-transform`. An anchor that animates instead of jumping is `scroll-smooth` on the container. An SVG icon that inherits the button's text colour is `stroke-current`. A \"next\" arrow that mirrors a previous one is `-scale-x-100`. Compose them on the same element and you get an interactive component without writing a single line of CSS.  The rest of the chapter (browser targets, install paths, IntelliSense, release history) is the meta layer that keeps the toolkit healthy. v4's modern-browser target is the reason these utilities feel fast: no autoprefixer lag, no legacy fallbacks, no polyfills. The IntelliSense extension is the reason the utilities feel fast to write: autocomplete, lint, and quick-fixes right inside the editor.", code_example = "// HTML from the Tailwind docs:      <button class=\"scale-105 hover:scale-110\">\n// The same thing in a React component:\n<button className=\"scale-105 hover:scale-110 transition-transform\">\n  Save\n</button>", pitfalls = "`hover:scale-110` writes `transform: scale(1.10)` on hover, with no transition. Add `transition` or `transition-transform` to animate it. The default transition duration is 150 ms and the default property list is the seven common transform / colour properties; `transition-transform` is the right shorthand for a transform-only animation.  `scale-z-150` only affects rendering when the element is in a 3D context (it has a parent with `transform-style: preserve-3d`, or its own `perspective-*` plus a 3D transform). On a flat element, `scale-z-150` produces no visible change. Pair it with `scale-3d` and a `perspective-*` utility on an ancestor if you want a real 3D effect.  `skew-y-6` pivots around the element's centre by default. If you wanted the parallelogram to lean on its bottom edge, add `origin-bottom` (or `origin-bottom-left` for a sharper tilt). The skew itself is correct; the visible result is off because the rotation pivot is wrong.  `scroll-smooth` only animates *programmatic* scrolling: anchor navigation, `scrollIntoView()`, `scrollTo()`, the URL hash jump. Mouse wheel, touch, and scroll bar drags are unaffected. If a user complaint is \"scrolling feels jerky,\" that is a scroll container issue, not a `scroll-behavior` issue.  Tailwind v4 expects to *be* the CSS preprocessor. If your project still runs Sass, the two engines will fight over `@import` resolution and source-map paths. Either migrate the Sass files to plain CSS (Tailwind v4 handles nesting via native CSS `@nest`) or downgrade to Tailwind v3." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzQ6VjI6MDA0LXNj",
        raw_key = "S5:C4:V2",
        title = "What is Tailwind CSS?",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/vibe-coding-common-web-patterns-into-tailwind-css/what-is-tailwind-css-25826008",
        duration_text = "02m33s",
        duration_seconds = 153,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of scale utilities, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Vibe Coding Common Web Patterns into Tailwind CSS",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "A Tailwind utility is a class name that maps to exactly one CSS declaration. `scale-110` sets `transform: scale(1.10)`. `skew-y-3` sets `transform: skewY(3deg)`. `scroll-smooth` sets `scroll-behavior: smooth`. You do not write the CSS by hand; you compose class names on the element and the browser applies the declarations they stand for.  This chapter is a working reference for four transform-flavoured utilities you reach for when building interactive UI (`scale`, `scroll-behavior`, `skew`, `stroke`), plus the tooling that ships alongside them: Tailwind v4's browser targets, the install recipes for non-Vite stacks, the VS Code IntelliSense extension, the major version feature history (v1.9, v2, v3, v4), and the \"From Zero to Production\" screencast series.", mental_model = "These four utilities are the cheap-interactive-UI toolkit. A button that pops on hover is `hover:scale-110 transition-transform`. An anchor that animates instead of jumping is `scroll-smooth` on the container. An SVG icon that inherits the button's text colour is `stroke-current`. A \"next\" arrow that mirrors a previous one is `-scale-x-100`. Compose them on the same element and you get an interactive component without writing a single line of CSS.  The rest of the chapter (browser targets, install paths, IntelliSense, release history) is the meta layer that keeps the toolkit healthy. v4's modern-browser target is the reason these utilities feel fast: no autoprefixer lag, no legacy fallbacks, no polyfills. The IntelliSense extension is the reason the utilities feel fast to write: autocomplete, lint, and quick-fixes right inside the editor.", code_example = "// HTML from the Tailwind docs:      <button class=\"scale-105 hover:scale-110\">\n// The same thing in a React component:\n<button className=\"scale-105 hover:scale-110 transition-transform\">\n  Save\n</button>", pitfalls = "`hover:scale-110` writes `transform: scale(1.10)` on hover, with no transition. Add `transition` or `transition-transform` to animate it. The default transition duration is 150 ms and the default property list is the seven common transform / colour properties; `transition-transform` is the right shorthand for a transform-only animation.  `scale-z-150` only affects rendering when the element is in a 3D context (it has a parent with `transform-style: preserve-3d`, or its own `perspective-*` plus a 3D transform). On a flat element, `scale-z-150` produces no visible change. Pair it with `scale-3d` and a `perspective-*` utility on an ancestor if you want a real 3D effect.  `skew-y-6` pivots around the element's centre by default. If you wanted the parallelogram to lean on its bottom edge, add `origin-bottom` (or `origin-bottom-left` for a sharper tilt). The skew itself is correct; the visible result is off because the rotation pivot is wrong.  `scroll-smooth` only animates *programmatic* scrolling: anchor navigation, `scrollIntoView()`, `scrollTo()`, the URL hash jump. Mouse wheel, touch, and scroll bar drags are unaffected. If a user complaint is \"scrolling feels jerky,\" that is a scroll container issue, not a `scroll-behavior` issue.  Tailwind v4 expects to *be* the CSS preprocessor. If your project still runs Sass, the two engines will fight over `@import` resolution and source-map paths. Either migrate the Sass files to plain CSS (Tailwind v4 handles nesting via native CSS `@nest`) or downgrade to Tailwind v3." }
      },

        references = [

        ],
        notes = { definition = "A Tailwind utility is a class name that maps to one CSS declaration. `h-12` sets `height: calc(var(--spacing) * 12)`. `size-6` sets both `width` and `height` to the same value. `leading-6` sets `line-height: calc(var(--spacing) * 6)`. You compose class names on the element and the browser applies the declarations they stand for.  This chapter is a reference for the height and size family (`h-*`, `size-*`, `inline-*`, `min-h-*`, `max-h-*`), the line-height utility, the `list-inside` and `list-outside` utilities, the isolation utility, and the four mask utilities (`mask-clip-*`, `mask-composite-*`, `mask-mode-*`, `mask-origin-*`). It closes with the Tailwind tooling roundup: the in-browser playground at `play.tailwindcss.com`, the Play CDN, the insiders build, the standalone CLI, and the Tailwind UI → Tailwind Plus rebrand.", mental_model = "Tailwind v4 derives every numeric utility from a single CSS variable: `--spacing`. The default value is `0.25rem`, so `h-1` is `0.25rem`, `h-4` is `1rem`, `h-96` is `24rem`. This is the \"spacing scale\" you already meet in `p-4`, `m-2`, `gap-6`, `w-12`. The `h-*`, `size-*`, `min-*`, and `max-*` families are the same dial in a different domain: vertical (or both-axes for `size-*`) sizing.  `size-<n>` is the shorthand for \"both width and height at the same value\". Most square UI elements (avatars, icon buttons, badges, the X icon on a modal) want both axes equal. `size-6` writes both `width: 1.5rem` and `height: 1.5rem` on the element. When the two axes need different values, fall back to `w-*` and `h-*` independently.", code_example = "// HTML from the Tailwind docs:     <div class=\"h-32 size-6\">\n// The same thing in a React component:\n<div className=\"h-32 size-6\">...</div>", pitfalls = "`h-screen` is `100vh`, the largest viewport height. On mobile the URL bar shrinks the visible viewport, so a `h-screen` element overflows when the bar is visible. Use `h-dvh` (dynamic) on mobile-first layouts. The browser engine adjusts the value as the chrome slides.  A modal with `z-50` does not stack above a dropdown with `z-50` if both share a stacking context with the same parent. The dropdown's `z-50` resolves first in the document order. Add `isolate` to the modal's overlay wrapper to force a new stacking context, and the modal becomes the unambiguous top of its sub-tree.  `size-12` sets both axes to `3rem`. If the avatar is a `size-12` circle, fine. If the layout wants a `w-12 h-24` rectangle, fall back to `w-*` and `h-*` independently. `size-*` is the symmetric shortcut, not a constraint to bend.  `h-full` is `height: 100%` of the parent. If the parent's height is `auto` (content-sized), `h-full` collapses to the content height. `min-h-full` is sometimes a better pick; the most reliable fix is to give the parent a fixed height (`h-screen`, `h-96`, or a flex parent's `h-*`) so the child has something to be 100% of.  The four mask utility groups only set the *behaviour* of a mask that already exists. Without a `mask-image` (via `mask-[url(...)]`, `mask-[linear-gradient(...)]`, or a `<mask>` SVG), the clip / mode / origin / composite properties have nothing to apply to. If you are seeing no visual effect, the missing piece is the mask image, not the bounding box." }
      },

    title = "Tailwind UI Ecommerce Kit, and the Utility-First Model Behind It",
    local_chapter_file = "006-tailwind-ui-ecommerce-kit.md",
    key_concepts = ["Hook: a class per decision", "The concept: utility-first styling", "What Tailwind sells on top of the model", "Typography utilities you will use constantly", "Predict-the-render", "Common mistakes"],
    total_duration_seconds = 423,
    videos = [

        id = "UzU6QzY6VjE6MDA2LXRh",

        title = "Understanding the utility-first approach",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/understanding-the-utility-first-approach",
        duration_text = "03m57s",
        duration_seconds = 237,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Tailwind UI Ecommerce Kit, and the Utility-First Model Behind It, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1 · **Prerequisites:** none (you can read this any time after you can write a JSX component)  You have been writing components. Now you need them to look like something. This chapter is about Tailwind CSS: the styling approach where you build a component's look by stacking small, single-purpose class names right in the `className`, instead of writing a separate stylesheet. We start from the model, then look at the pre-built pieces Tailwind sells on top of it (the Ecommerce component kit and the Next.js site templates), and finish with the typography utilities you will reach for constantly.", mental_model = "Utility-first styling is the default in a large share of modern React and Next.js codebases, including the Reddit-recommended toolchain this course follows. Once you can read a class string fluently, you can read almost any Tailwind component, drop in a pre-built kit component and understand it, and adjust a template without fighting a stylesheet you did not write. The typography utilities in particular show up in nearly every component you will build for the rest of the course.", code_example = "/* pure CSS, in a separate file */\n.card-title {\n  font-size: 1.25rem;\n  font-weight: 500;\n  color: black;\n}", pitfalls = "1. **Building class names at runtime.** `` className={`text-${size}`} `` can produce a class    Tailwind's scanner never saw at build time, so the CSS for it is never generated and the    style silently does nothing. Branch between complete strings instead:    `className={size === \"lg\" ? \"text-lg\" : \"text-sm\"}`. 2. **Expecting `capitalize` to normalize an all-caps string.** `capitalize` uppercases the    first letter of each word; it will not turn `\"SALE\"` into `\"Sale\"`. Fix the source text or    use `lowercase` first. 3. **Assuming utility order in the string sets priority.** The order of classes in    `className` does not decide which wins. When two utilities set the same property (say two    background colors), the winner is decided by the generated CSS, not by which you typed    last. Do not put conflicting utilities on one element and expect the \"last\" one to win. 4. **Treating the paid kits as required.** The Ecommerce kit and templates are optional,    paid starting points. The framework itself, the part every problem in this chapter uses,    is the free open-source utilities." }

  {
    id = "05-007",
    number = 7,
    title = "Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling",
    local_chapter_file = "007-tailwind-utility-first.md",
    key_concepts = ["Hook: composing decisions in the className", "The model: utility classes, build-time generation, and variants", "Text decoration: line, style, thickness, color, offset", "The Tailwind CLI: build and watch without a framework plugin", "Display, visibility, and `sr-only`", "`box-border` and `box-content`"],
    total_duration_seconds = 366,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6Qzc6VjE6MDA3LXRh",
        raw_key = "S5:C7:V1",
        title = "Prompts for Tailwind CSS code generation",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/vibe-coding-common-web-patterns-into-tailwind-css/prompts-for-tailwind-css-code-generation-25822009",
        duration_text = "02m23s",
        duration_seconds = 143,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Vibe Coding Common Web Patterns into Tailwind CSS",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1 · **Prerequisites:** Tailwind UI Ecommerce kit  The previous chapter framed Tailwind CSS as the styling layer for the rest of the course and showed a product card assembled from utility classes. This chapter turns that framing into a working vocabulary. You should be able to read a Tailwind `className` and know roughly what it renders, and reach for the right utility when a layout problem shows up.", mental_model = "The utility families in this chapter are the 30% of Tailwind you reach for 90% of the time. Layout (`flex`, `grid`, `block`, `hidden`, `sr-only`), spacing (`p-*`, `m-*`, `gap-*`, `space-*`), sizing (`h-*`, `w-*`, `size-*`, `h-dvh`), text decoration (`underline`, `decoration-*`, `underline-offset-*`), animation (`animate-*`), form-control resets (`appearance-none`), and the table family (`border-collapse`, `border-separate`, `caption-*`). The rest of the styling chapters in this section (typography, color, shadows, transforms) layer on top of the same model.  The Tailwind CLI is the bridge for non-JS stacks. If you ever build a server-rendered HTML app or a static site with a non-Node tool, the CLI is the build step. Inside the React + Vite projects in this course, the Vite plugin is the equivalent; the CLI is the same scanner and engine, just driven from a script.", code_example = "<article className=\"rounded-xl border p-6\">\n  <p className=\"text-sm uppercase tracking-widest\">3 in stock</p>\n  <h2 className=\"mt-2 text-2xl font-semibold underline decoration-wavy decoration-2 underline-offset-4\">\n    Nomad Tote\n  </h2>\n  <span className=\"mt-4 inline-flex size-12 animate-pulse rounded-full bg-emerald-500\" />\n  <table className=\"mt-6 w-full border-separate border border-gray-300 caption-top\">\n    <caption>Sizes</caption>\n    <thead>\n      <tr><th>S</th><th>M</th><th>L</th></tr>\n    </thead>\n  </table>\n</article>", pitfalls = "`` `text-${color}-500` `` produces a string Tailwind's scanner never saw, so the CSS is never generated and the style silently does nothing. Branch between complete strings instead:  // right className={color === \"red\" ? \"text-red-500\" : \"text-blue-500\"} ```  The same goes for `decoration-*`, `border-*`, `bg-*`, and any other family. If the full class name does not appear literally in a scanned file, the CSS is not in the output.  The order of utilities in `className` is irrelevant. The order in the *generated CSS file* is what matters, and that order is fixed by the build, not by what you typed. If two utilities set the same CSS property, the one that comes later in the generated CSS wins, not the one you wrote later. Do not stack `flex` and `grid` on the same element and expect the order of the string to decide.  `h-screen` is `100vh`, the largest possible viewport height. On mobile, the visible viewport shrinks when the URL bar shows and grows when it hides. A `h-screen` element overflows when the bar is visible. Use `h-dvh` (dynamic) for mobile-first layouts; the browser adjusts the value as the chrome slides." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6Qzc6VjI6MDA3LXRh",

        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1 · **Prerequisites:** Tailwind UI Ecommerce kit  The previous chapter framed Tailwind CSS as the styling layer for the rest of the course and showed a product card assembled from utility classes. This chapter turns that framing into a working vocabulary. You should be able to read a Tailwind `className` and know roughly what it renders, and reach for the right utility when a layout problem shows up.", mental_model = "The utility families in this chapter are the 30% of Tailwind you reach for 90% of the time. Layout (`flex`, `grid`, `block`, `hidden`, `sr-only`), spacing (`p-*`, `m-*`, `gap-*`, `space-*`), sizing (`h-*`, `w-*`, `size-*`, `h-dvh`), text decoration (`underline`, `decoration-*`, `underline-offset-*`), animation (`animate-*`), form-control resets (`appearance-none`), and the table family (`border-collapse`, `border-separate`, `caption-*`). The rest of the styling chapters in this section (typography, color, shadows, transforms) layer on top of the same model.  The Tailwind CLI is the bridge for non-JS stacks. If you ever build a server-rendered HTML app or a static site with a non-Node tool, the CLI is the build step. Inside the React + Vite projects in this course, the Vite plugin is the equivalent; the CLI is the same scanner and engine, just driven from a script.", code_example = "<article className=\"rounded-xl border p-6\">\n  <p className=\"text-sm uppercase tracking-widest\">3 in stock</p>\n  <h2 className=\"mt-2 text-2xl font-semibold underline decoration-wavy decoration-2 underline-offset-4\">\n    Nomad Tote\n  </h2>\n  <span className=\"mt-4 inline-flex size-12 animate-pulse rounded-full bg-emerald-500\" />\n  <table className=\"mt-6 w-full border-separate border border-gray-300 caption-top\">\n    <caption>Sizes</caption>\n    <thead>\n      <tr><th>S</th><th>M</th><th>L</th></tr>\n    </thead>\n  </table>\n</article>", pitfalls = "`` `text-${color}-500` `` produces a string Tailwind's scanner never saw, so the CSS is never generated and the style silently does nothing. Branch between complete strings instead:  // right className={color === \"red\" ? \"text-red-500\" : \"text-blue-500\"} ```  The same goes for `decoration-*`, `border-*`, `bg-*`, and any other family. If the full class name does not appear literally in a scanned file, the CSS is not in the output.  The order of utilities in `className` is irrelevant. The order in the *generated CSS file* is what matters, and that order is fixed by the build, not by what you typed. If two utilities set the same CSS property, the one that comes later in the generated CSS wins, not the one you wrote later. Do not stack `flex` and `grid` on the same element and expect the order of the string to decide.  `h-screen` is `100vh`, the largest possible viewport height. On mobile, the visible viewport shrinks when the URL bar shows and grows when it hides. A `h-screen` element overflows when the bar is visible. Use `h-dvh` (dynamic) for mobile-first layouts; the browser adjusts the value as the chrome slides." }
      },
    ]
  },
  {
    id = "05-008",
    number = 8,
    title = "Tailwind color palette",
    local_chapter_file = "008-tailwind-color-palette.md",
    key_concepts = ["The mental model: a name, a step, and a variable", "One palette, many properties", "Opacity: the slash modifier", "Dark mode: the `dark:` variant", "Referencing colors in CSS", "Customizing the palette with `@theme`"],
    total_duration_seconds = 717,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6Qzg6VjE6MDA4LXRh",
        raw_key = "S5:C8:V1",
        title = "Using colors",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/using-colors",
        duration_text = "04m36s",
        duration_seconds = 276,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Tailwind color palette, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "A color utility in Tailwind is a class name that resolves to one CSS variable. `bg-sky-500` sets `background-color: var(--color-sky-500)`. `text-gray-950` sets `color: var(--color-gray-950)`. `border-pink-300` sets `border-color: var(--color-pink-300)`. You never write a hex value on the element. You pick a color name and a step, and the class resolves to a design-token variable the framework already defined.  This chapter is the color layer of Tailwind plus a small group of interactivity and layout utilities that come up in the same components: the multi-step palette and its opacity, CSS variable, and dark-mode features; the `cursor-*`, `pointer-events-*`, and `resize-*` interactivity utilities; the `display` utilities; and two pieces of Tailwind ecosystem context (the IntelliSense editor extension and the Tailwind UI → Tailwind Plus rebrand).", mental_model = "Every color in the default palette has a name (`red`, `sky`, `slate`, `emerald`, …) and **11 steps**: `50`, `100`, `200`, `300`, `400`, `500`, `600`, `700`, `800`, `900`, `950`. Step `50` is the lightest, `950` is the darkest, and `500` sits in the middle as the \"base\" of the ramp. A color utility is the name and the step glued together behind a property prefix:  The steps are defined in the OKLCH color space, not RGB or HSL. OKLCH is perceptually uniform: stepping from `400` to `500` looks like the same jump in lightness whether the hue is yellow or blue, which is why the ramps look even across every color. You do not need to write OKLCH yourself. You pick the step; the framework holds the value.", code_example = "// HTML in the Tailwind docs:  <div class=\"bg-sky-500 text-white\">\n// The same thing in React:\n<div className=\"bg-sky-500 text-white\">...</div>", pitfalls = "Tailwind generates CSS by scanning your source for complete class strings. `\"bg-\" + color + \"-500\"` is never a complete string in the file, so `bg-sky-500` is never generated and the element renders unstyled. The fix is to write whole class names and switch between them, or to constrain the dynamic part to a small typed union and interpolate that (`bg-${tone}-500` where `tone` is `\"sky\" | \"rose\"`) so every possible result is spelled out somewhere the scanner can reach. When in doubt, map the input to a lookup of literal class strings.  `opacity-50` makes the whole element (background *and* its text and children) 50% transparent. The slash modifier `bg-sky-500/50` changes only the background color's alpha and leaves the text fully opaque. For a translucent card or scrim with readable content, you almost always want the slash, not `opacity-*`.  A \"saved!\" confirmation that toggles between `hidden` and `block` makes the layout jump every time it appears, because the element goes from taking no space to taking its full space. If the surrounding layout must stay stable, toggle `invisible` / `visible` instead: the space is always reserved and only the paint changes.  An icon absolutely-positioned over an input steals the click unless it opts out of hit testing. Add `pointer-events-none` to the icon so the click falls through and focuses the input. Conversely, if an interactive overlay is not responding, check that a parent did not set `pointer-events-none` and swallow the events.  `dark:bg-gray-800` is not a runtime conditional in your component. It compiles to a CSS rule that applies under the dark-mode selector. You do not read a \"theme\" value in the component to choose it; you write both branches as utilities and the browser resolves which one applies. Toggling the theme is a separate concern (a class on `<html>` or the OS setting), not something each component decides." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6Qzg6VjI6MDA4LXRh",
        raw_key = "S5:C8:V2",
        title = "Applying background colors and gradients",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/applying-background-colors-and-gradients",
        duration_text = "07m21s",
        duration_seconds = 441,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Tailwind color palette, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",

        ],
        notes = { definition = "A color utility in Tailwind is a class name that resolves to one CSS variable. `bg-sky-500` sets `background-color: var(--color-sky-500)`. `text-gray-950` sets `color: var(--color-gray-950)`. `border-pink-300` sets `border-color: var(--color-pink-300)`.   {
    id = "05-009",
    number = 9,
    title = "Width and height utilities",
    local_chapter_file = "009-width-and-height-utilities.md",
    key_concepts = ["The mental model: a spacing scale, plus a few kernel-level utilities", "`w-*`: width from the spacing scale", "`h-*`: height from the spacing scale", "`size-*`: shorthand for width and height", "`min-w-*`, `max-w-*`, `min-h-*`, `max-h-*`: bounding the box", "Automatic class detection and static class names"],
    total_duration_seconds = 356,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6Qzk6VjE6MDA5LXdp",
        raw_key = "S5:C9:V1",
        title = "Width, height, and display",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/width-height-and-display",
        duration_text = "03m07s",
        duration_seconds = 187,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Width and height utilities, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "CSS Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "A Tailwind utility is a class name that maps to one CSS declaration. `w-12` sets `width: calc(var(--spacing) * 12)`. `h-screen` sets `height: 100vh`. `size-6` sets both axes to the same value. You compose class names on the element and the browser applies the declarations they stand for.  This chapter is a reference roundup. The top of the chapter covers the width and height family (`w-*`, `min-w-*`, `max-w-*`, `h-*`, `min-h-*`, `max-h-*`, `size-*`). The middle covers three utility groups that often show up next to sizing: `break-before-*` / `break-inside-*` for print and columns, `clear-*` for float wrapping, and Tailwind's static-class detection rule that decides whether a runtime-built class name will appear in the generated CSS. The tail covers a small set of one-off utilities (font features, font stretch, grid auto flow, direction-aware text alignment, composable gradients) and a few Tailwind UI product primers (the single-page changelog template, framework install guides for non-React stacks, the design-engineering job description).", mental_model = "Tailwind v4 derives every numeric sizing utility from a single CSS variable: `--spacing`. The default value is `0.25rem`, so `w-1` is `0.25rem`, `w-4` is `1rem`, `w-96` is `24rem`. The `h-*`, `min-*`, `max-*`, and `size-*` families are the same dial in different domains: horizontal, vertical, bounded, and symmetric sizing.  The other utilities in this chapter are independent kernels: `break-*` is CSS paged-media, `clear-*` is float layout, `font-stretch` and `font-feature-settings` are OpenType selection, `grid-flow-*` is grid auto-placement, `text-start` / `text-end` are logical alignment, `bg-gradient-to-*` plus `from-*` / `via-*` / `to-*` plus `bg-clip-text` are layered backgrounds. Each is a small, complete API you can read independently.", code_example = "// HTML from the Tailwind docs:     <div class=\"w-32 h-12\">\n// The same thing in a React component:\n<div className=\"w-32 h-12\">...</div>", pitfalls = "Direct DOM mutation or impure side effects during the render phase cause hydration mismatches, duplicate executions in Strict Mode, and stale closures. Always declare full dependency arrays in useEffect and never read state during render from outside the component tree." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6Qzk6VjI6MDA5LXdp",
        raw_key = "S5:C9:V2",
        title = "Line-height and letter-spacing",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/line-height-and-letter-spacing",
        duration_text = "02m49s",
        duration_seconds = 169,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Width and height utilities, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",

        ],
        notes = { definition = "A Tailwind utility is a class name that maps to one CSS declaration. `w-12` sets `width: calc(var(--spacing) * 12)`. `h-screen` sets `height: 100vh`. `size-6` sets both axes to the same value.   {
    id = "05-010",
    number = 10,
    title = "Install Tailwind with Qwik",
    local_chapter_file = "010-install-tailwind-with-qwik.md",
    key_concepts = ["The mental model: three integration shapes", "The Vite-plugin frameworks", "The PostCSS-plugin frameworks", "Ruby on Rails: the generator does the wiring", "Utility reference", "Predict the render"],
    total_duration_seconds = 508,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzEwOlYxOjAxMC1p",
        raw_key = "S5:C10:V1",
        title = "Install and configure Tailwind CSS",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/master-react-19-build-ecommerce-solutions-and-prepare-for-interviews-with-typescript-next-js-and-remix/install-and-configure-tailwind-css",
        duration_text = "03m46s",
        duration_seconds = 226,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Install Tailwind with Qwik, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Master React 19, Build Ecommerce Solutions, and Prepare for Interviews with TypeScript, Next.js, and Remix",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "Tailwind v4 has exactly three integration shapes, and every framework in this chapter is one of them. Once you can name which shape a framework uses, the install is four moves: install the package, wire it into the build, import the CSS once, run the dev server. Qwik is the headline, but the same recipe covers SolidJS, SvelteKit, TanStack Start, AdonisJS, Astro, Rspack, Symfony, Angular, and Ruby on Rails.  The second half of the chapter is a working reference for five utilities that pair naturally with layout work: the two logical sizing utilities (`min-block-*` and `min-inline-*`), the blend-mode utilities (`mix-blend-*`), the flex/grid `order-*` utilities, and the `overflow-wrap` word-breaking utilities.", mental_model = "Every Tailwind v4 install is one of these:  The one constant across all three: a single CSS entry file contains the line", code_example = "@import \"tailwindcss\";", pitfalls = "Astro nests the plugin under a `vite` key; SvelteKit, SolidJS, Qwik, TanStack Start, and AdonisJS put it at the top-level `plugins` array of their own config. A `tailwindcss()` added to the top level of `astro.config.mjs` (outside `vite`) is silently ignored, and no utilities are emitted. Match the framework's config shape.  Rspack, Symfony, and Angular do not run Vite, so `@tailwindcss/vite` has nothing to hook into. They need `@tailwindcss/postcss` plus a PostCSS config. Installing the Vite plugin on an Angular project produces no error and no styles. Pick the package that matches the integration shape in the table at the top of the chapter.  SvelteKit, Astro, and AdonisJS all require you to *import* the CSS entry from a layout or page. The `@import \"tailwindcss\";` line does nothing if the file it lives in is never loaded. Symptom: every `class`/`className` renders, but the page is unstyled. TanStack Start is the sharp edge here. Its entry is registered as a `links` stylesheet with the `?url` query, not imported as a module, so importing `../styles.css` directly is the wrong move.  Adonis Edge templates and Symfony Twig templates are not JSX; Tailwind's automatic content detection can miss them. Adonis needs `@source \"../views\";`. Symfony needs `@source not \"../../public\";` for the opposite reason: to *exclude* its compiled output so the watcher does not loop. A class that appears only in a `.edge` or `.twig` file and never gets emitted is almost always a missing (or wrong) `@source`.  `order` is only meaningful on flex items and grid items. Applying `order-2` to a child of a plain block or inline container does nothing. If reordering has no effect, check that the parent is `flex` or `grid` first." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzEwOlYyOjAxMC1p",
        raw_key = "S5:C10:V2",
        title = "Project setup with Tailwind CSS and header creation",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/master-react-19-build-ecommerce-solutions-and-prepare-for-interviews-with-typescript-next-js-and-remix/project-setup-with-tailwind-css-and-header-creation",
        duration_text = "04m42s",
        duration_seconds = 282,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Install Tailwind with Qwik, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Master React 19, Build Ecommerce Solutions, and Prepare for Interviews with TypeScript, Next.js, and Remix",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "Tailwind v4 has exactly three integration shapes, and every framework in this chapter is one of them. Rspack, Symfony, and Angular do not run Vite, so `@tailwindcss/vite` has nothing to hook into. They need `@tailwindcss/postcss` plus a PostCSS config. Installing the Vite plugin on an Angular project produces no error and no styles. Pick the package that matches the integration shape in the table at the top of the chapter.  SvelteKit, Astro, and AdonisJS all require you to *import* the CSS entry from a layout or page. The `@import \"tailwindcss\";` line does nothing if the file it lives in is never loaded. Symptom: every `class`/`className` renders, but the page is unstyled. TanStack Start is the sharp edge here. Its entry is registered as a `links` stylesheet with the `?url` query, not imported as a module, so importing `../styles.css` directly is the wrong move.     id = "05-011",
    number = 11,
    title = "Overscroll-behavior utilities",
    local_chapter_file = "011-overscroll-behavior-utilities.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model: what reaching the edge means", "`overscroll-*` and `overscroll-x-*` / `overscroll-y-*`", "`pointer-events-*`: block or pass through pointer input", "Positioning: `static`, `relative`, `absolute`, `fixed`, `sticky`", "`scrollbar-*`: thickness and visibility", "The `@tailwindcss/typography` plugin and `prose`"],
    total_duration_seconds = 526,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzExOlYxOjAxMS1v",
        raw_key = "S5:C11:V1",

        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/creating-flexbox-layouts",

        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Overscroll-behavior utilities, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },

        notes = { definition = "When a scrollable element reaches its edge, three things can happen: it chains the scroll up to a parent container, it bounces within its own bounds (the iOS-style rubber-band effect), or it does nothing at all. Tailwind v4 exposes those three behaviours as `overscroll-auto`, `overscroll-contain`, and `overscroll-none`. The same property has axis variants (`overscroll-x-*`, `overscroll-y-*`) for the rare case where horizontal and vertical scrolling need different rules. Once you can name what each value does, a long-standing source of scroll bugs in modals, sidebars, and nested carousels turns into a one-line class.  The chapter is a reference roundup. The top half is the overscroll family and its neighbours that you reach for on the same scroll panel: `pointer-events-*`, the Tailwind positioning utilities (`static`, `relative`, `absolute`, `fixed`, `sticky`), and the `scrollbar-*` utilities (`auto`, `thin`, `none`). The second half covers three more groups that share the scroll / viewport mental model: the `@tailwindcss/typography` plugin's `prose` classes for long-form content, the scroll-snap family (`snap-*`, `snap-stop-*`, `scroll-m-*`, `scroll-p-*`), the `rotate-*` transform utilities, and the React-level rule for encapsulating repeated Tailwind combinations. The tail lists two Tailwind UI templates (Protocol, Radiant) as starting points rather than utilities, with a note that they are paid products and out of scope for a course on writing classes.", mental_model = "The browser's `overscroll-behavior` controls three independent choices at the boundary of a scrollable element:  The reason the same property fixes several common UX bugs:", code_example = "<div className=\"overscroll-contain h-64 overflow-y-auto\">…</div>", pitfalls = "`scroll-snap-align` on a child is a no-op without `scroll-snap-type` on a scrollable parent. The common symptom is \"I added `snap-start` to my images and the scroll does not snap.\" The fix: add `snap-x-mandatory` (or `snap-x-proximity`) on the scroll container.  `scroll-m-*` lives on the *child*; `scroll-p-*` lives on the *container*. Putting `scroll-m-4` on the container and `scroll-p-4` on the child reverses which axis offsets the snap. Pick by which element's geometry should be inset.  `overscroll-behavior` has no effect unless the element actually overflows. Wrap the contents with `overflow-y-auto` (or `overflow-x-auto` for horizontal) for the property to bite. The CSS is ignored otherwise.  `position: sticky` does not work when the nearest scrolling ancestor is also an `overflow: hidden` ancestor; the sticky element sticks relative to the wrong box and appears to not stick at all. If a sticky header does not stick, walk up the tree for an `overflow-hidden` parent and replace it with `overflow-clip` (which lets sticky work) or remove the overflow rule.  Tailwind's scanner reads source as plain text and generates CSS only for complete class-name strings. It cannot follow a template literal interpolation. Use a static lookup object (`{ sm: \"w-32\", md: \"w-64\" }`) or write the *complete* arbitrary value (`w-[var(--card-w)]`) so the class name is a single, scannable string." }
      },

    id = "05-012",
    number = 12,
    title = "Scrollbar-color utilities",
    local_chapter_file = "012-scrollbar-color-utilities.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model: two colours, one width, one gutter", "`scrollbar-width`: how wide the bar is", "`scrollbar-color`: thumb and track", "`scrollbar-gutter`: reserve the space", "State and selector variants", "Table layout utilities"],
    total_duration_seconds = 701,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzEyOlYxOjAxMi1z",
        raw_key = "S5:C12:V1",
        title = "Using display, position, and z-index utilities",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/using-display-position-and-z-index-utilities",
        duration_text = "07m24s",
        duration_seconds = 444,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Scrollbar-color utilities, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "A scrollbar has two paintable parts: the **thumb** you drag and the **track** it slides along. Tailwind exposes both as colour utilities, `scrollbar-thumb-<color>` and `scrollbar-track-<color>`, alongside a width family (`scrollbar-auto`, `scrollbar-thin`, `scrollbar-none`) and a gutter family (`scrollbar-gutter-auto`, `scrollbar-gutter-stable`, `scrollbar-gutter-both`). Once you can name what each one sets, the custom scrollbar on a chat panel or a code block stops being a hand-written block of `::-webkit-scrollbar` CSS and becomes three classes on the scroll container.  This chapter is a reference roundup. The core is the `scrollbar-*` family. Around it sit utilities you reach for on the same scroll panel or in the same design pass: state and selector variants (`hover:`, `md:`, and friends), the table layout utilities, `content-*` for aligning rows in a wrapped flex or grid, the border utilities (`rounded-*`, `border-*`, `divide-*`, `border-spacing-*`, `border-<style>`), and the composable filter utilities (`blur-*`, `grayscale`, `backdrop-blur`). The tail notes Tailwind's Studio template as a starting point rather than a utility, and where Tailwind sits as a CSS build tool.", mental_model = "A styled scrollbar is four independent decisions:  - **Width** picks a keyword: the browser default (`auto`), a slimmer bar   (`thin`), or no visible bar at all (`none`, still scrollable). - **Thumb and track colour** are the two values of the single CSS   `scrollbar-color` property. Tailwind splits them into two classes and   recombines them through CSS variables. - **Gutter** decides whether the browser reserves space for the bar even   when the content is not overflowing, which is how you stop layout from   shifting the moment a scrollbar appears.", code_example = "<div className=\"scrollbar-thin scrollbar-thumb-sky-500 scrollbar-track-slate-200 h-64 overflow-y-auto\">\n  …\n</div>", pitfalls = "1. **Expecting a visible bar to change with no scroll.** Colour and width    utilities only show up where a scrollbar actually renders. If the    content does not overflow, there is nothing to paint. Give the    container a fixed height and `overflow-y-auto`, or reserve space with    `scrollbar-gutter-stable`, before you judge the colours. 2. **Setting a thumb colour and expecting a visible track.** Set only    `scrollbar-thumb-*` and the track is `transparent`, not the browser    default grey. Add `scrollbar-track-*` when you want a painted track. 3. **Reaching for a pixel width.** `scrollbar-width` accepts only `auto`,    `thin`, and `none`. There is no arbitrary width; Tailwind cannot invent    one the CSS property does not support. 4. **`content-*` on a single row.** `align-content` needs multiple rows.    On a one-row flex container it does nothing; you almost certainly want    `items-*` (align-items) instead. 5. **`border-spacing-*` under `border-collapse`.** The spacing is silently    ignored. Switch the table to `border-separate` first." }
      },

  {
    id = "05-013",
    number = 13,
    title = "Tailwind CSS Just-In-Time engine",
    local_chapter_file = "013-tailwind-css-just-in-time-engine.md",
    key_concepts = ["Hook: build only what you used", "Scroll-snap: lock scrolling to discrete positions", "`scroll-snap-align` on the children", "`scroll-margin`: push the snap target inward", "`scroll-padding`: shrink the scroll port", "The release timeline: v2.0 to v4.3"],
    total_duration_seconds = 784,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzEzOlYxOjAxMy10",
        raw_key = "S5:C13:V1",
        title = "Performance considerations in Tailwind CSS",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/vibe-coding-common-web-patterns-into-tailwind-css/performance-considerations-in-tailwind-css-25822006",
        duration_text = "01m47s",
        duration_seconds = 107,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Tailwind CSS Just-In-Time engine, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Vibe Coding Common Web Patterns into Tailwind CSS",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2 · **Prerequisites:** Tailwind UI Ecommerce kit, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling  Tailwind CSS has gone through two engines. The first was the AOT (\"ahead-of-time\") engine that came with Tailwind v1 and v2: it scanned your source, generated every utility it could possibly need into a multi-megabyte CSS file, and let PurgeCSS trim the unused ones at the end of the build. The second is the JIT (\"just-in-time\") engine that landed as an opt-in preview in v2.1, became the default in v3.0, and now drives v4 in a Rust rewrite. The JIT engine flipped the workflow: instead of generating everything then purging, it generates only the classes the scanner actually finds, parses your source files into a token stream, and emits a small CSS file on every save. Sub-second rebuilds, every variant enabled by default, arbitrary values everywhere, and identical CSS in dev and prod.", mental_model = "The JIT engine is the engine; everything in this chapter is what the engine exposes. In real codebases you meet the families everywhere: a `snap-mandatory` carousel in the product gallery, a `scroll-pl-4` padding on the scroll port so the first card lands inside the gutter, a `:has(:checked)` toggle that swaps the gallery between detail and thumbnail modes, an `@theme` block that defines brand colors once and exposes them as `bg-brand-500`, `text-brand-700`, `border-brand-500/40`, `accent-brand-500`, `shadow-brand-700/20`, `ring-brand-500/30`. Each one is a single utility class with no custom CSS file.  The release timeline matters because the version on a project dictates which utilities are available. v3.0 utilities (`snap-*`, `aspect-*`, `accent-*`) work on any project since v3.0. v4-only utilities (`@theme`, `not-*`, `bg-linear-*`, `starting:`, `mask-*`) work only on v4. The v3.4 `has-[:checked]:` works on v3.4+ *and* on v4 (the same variant, same engine). When you copy a class from a Stack Overflow answer, check which Tailwind version it landed in before pasting it into your project.", code_example = "<button className=\"bg-rose-500 hover:bg-rose-700 px-4 py-2 rounded-md\">\n  Subscribe\n</button>", pitfalls = "`accent-brand-500` from the snippet above works only if the project has `--color-brand-500` in its `@theme` block. In a v3 project without the token, Tailwind's scanner does not see a literal `accent-brand-500` anywhere, so the CSS for it is never generated and the styling silently does nothing. The check is: does the utility name appear as a literal in a scanned source file? If not, the build does not know about it.  In v3.0, `snap-x` alone meant `scroll-snap-type: x mandatory`. In v4 the default for `snap-x` shifted to `scroll-snap-type: x var(--tw-scroll-snap-type)`; without a second utility or a custom property, the container falls back to `proximity`. To force strict snapping, write the pair explicitly: `snap-x snap-mandatory`. Otherwise the user can stop between snap targets and the strip stops behaving like a carousel.  `scroll-margin` is a child property; it shrinks the snap target of the element it is on. `scroll-padding` is the parent property; it shrinks the snap port of the container. Setting `scroll-mt-16` on the parent does nothing. Setting `scroll-pt-16` on the child does nothing. Pick the one that matches the role of the element.  A `snap-x` container only snaps if it is scrollable. A `<div className=\"snap-x flex gap-4\">` on a page wider than the children's sum has no overflow and no scrolling, so the snap behavior never engages. Add `overflow-x-auto` (or `overflow-scroll`) so the browser knows it can scroll, then the snap points take effect.  `columns-3` puts the children into three flowing newspaper columns. `flex` lays the children in a row or column and assigns main / cross axes. Putting both on the same element gives whichever CSS rule wins in the cascade, and the one you wanted often loses. The multi-column utilities are for flowing text content (long-form articles, gallery walls of thumbnails with wrap); the flex / grid utilities are for layouts you control axis by axis." }
      },

    id = "05-014",
    number = 14,
    title = "Tailwind focus indicator and outline utilities",
    local_chapter_file = "014-tailwind-focus-indicator-and-outline-utilities.md",
    key_concepts = ["Hook: `outline-none` does not mean \"no outline\"", "Color-agnostic focus rings: `outline-white` and `outline-black`", "`focus` vs `focus-visible`: only ring the keyboard user", "Positioning: `static`, `relative`, `absolute`, `fixed`, `sticky`", "Offsets: `inset-*` and `top` / `right` / `bottom` / `left`", "Stacking: `z-*`"],
    total_duration_seconds = 504,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzE0OlYxOjAxNC10",
        raw_key = "S5:C14:V1",
        title = "Sizing and reordering grid items",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/sizing-and-reordering-grid-items",
        duration_text = "04m26s",
        duration_seconds = 266,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Tailwind focus indicator and outline utilities, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1–2 · **Prerequisites:** Tailwind UI Ecommerce kit, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling  A focus ring is the box a browser draws around the control you can act on right now. Tab through a form and watch it hop from field to field: that outline is how a keyboard user knows where they are. When a component removes it to look tidy, the keyboard user is left blind. This chapter is about drawing that ring on purpose, in a way that survives Windows high-contrast mode, adapts to text direction, and respects a user who has asked the system to cut down on motion.", mental_model = "Focus and outline utilities are the difference between a component a keyboard user can operate and one they can't. In real component libraries every interactive element has an `outline-none focus-visible:outline-*` (or `focus-visible:ring-*`) pattern for exactly the reasons above: keep mouse clicks clean, keep keyboard focus visible, and survive forced-colors mode. Skip it and you ship a control that is invisible to a whole class of users.  The positioning, stacking, and isolation utilities are the substrate every overlay sits on: a dropdown (`absolute z-10`), a modal backdrop (`fixed inset-0`), a sticky header (`sticky top-0 z-30`), a badge (`absolute inset-e-2`). Getting the `relative`-parent and stacking-context rules right is what stops the \"my menu renders behind the next section\" bug. The build-side concepts (Preflight, source detection, Oxide) explain the two things that surprise newcomers most: why unstyled elements look flat (Preflight reset) and why a dynamically built class name produces no CSS (the scanner never saw the token). And the accessibility-minded utilities (logical offsets for RTL, `motion-safe` for reduced motion, `tabular-nums` for readable data) are the small touches that separate a component that works for one user from one that works for everyone.", code_example = "<button className=\"focus:shadow-outline focus:outline-none\">Subscribe</button>", pitfalls = "`outline-none` in Tailwind is `outline: 2px solid transparent`, not `outline: none`. That is intentional (forced-colors safety). If you genuinely need zero outline, that is almost always the wrong call for accessibility; pair `outline-none` with a `focus-visible:` ring instead so the keyboard user keeps an indicator.  `focus:outline-black` shows the ring even when a mouse user clicks the control, which many designers dislike and then \"fix\" by removing the outline entirely (mistake #1). Use `focus-visible:` so the ring appears for keyboard focus but not mouse clicks.  `z-index` is ignored on `static` elements. `<div className=\"z-50\">` does nothing on its own. Add `relative` (or another positioning utility) first, then the `z-50` takes effect.  If `z-[9999]` refuses to sit above something, a parent has created a stacking context that traps it (a `transform`, an `opacity` below 1, an `isolate`, or the parent's own `z-index`). The child can never escape its parent's stacking context. The fix is to raise or restructure the *parent*, or use `isolate` deliberately to control where contexts begin.  An `absolute` element positions against its nearest positioned ancestor. Without a `relative` (or other positioned) parent, it jumps up the tree, often all the way to the viewport. The corner badge ends up in the page corner. Add `relative` to the intended container." }
      },
      {

    id = "05-015",
    number = 15,
    title = "Tailwind theme variables",
    local_chapter_file = "015-tailwind-theme-variables.md",
    key_concepts = ["The `@theme` contract", "Theme variables are real CSS custom properties", "`@theme` vs `@theme inline`", "The v1.9 `presets` story (legacy)", "The v2.1 JIT engine promotion", "The v2.2 surface: pseudo-elements, typography, and slash opacity"],
    total_duration_seconds = 954,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzE1OlYxOjAxNS10",
        raw_key = "S5:C15:V1",
        title = "Changing the theme",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/changing-the-theme",
        duration_text = "09m34s",
        duration_seconds = 574,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Tailwind theme variables, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2 · **Prerequisites:** Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Tailwind UI Ecommerce kit  Theme variables are the design-token layer of Tailwind v4. A `@theme` block declares CSS custom properties such as `--color-mint-500`, `--font-display`, or `--breakpoint-3xl`. Each one does two jobs at once: it becomes a real CSS custom property on `:root` that any stylesheet can read with `var(...)`, and it tells the JIT engine which utilities and variants to generate. The takeaway is that there is no separate \"config\" step: declare the token, get the utilities.", mental_model = "Theme variables are the bridge between \"Tailwind as a utility catalogue\" and \"Tailwind as a design-system platform\". Once a team codifies its colours, fonts, spacings, and breakpoints in a shared `@theme` block (or publishes a package that exports one), every utility becomes a token reference. The class name names the design decision, and the design decision lives in one place.  You will meet the surrounding tooling everywhere in real codebases: the `@tailwindcss/vite` plugin in a fresh Vite app, the `@tailwindcss/postcss` plugin in a Webpack migration, the React framework guide pinning Tailwind to a particular bundler, the v2.2 `before:` / `selection:` / `/<opacity>` families surviving into v4 because they are load-bearing for any UI with quotes, bullets, or translucent overlays, the v1.9 `presets` key in a config from the v2 era that someone forgot to migrate. The release history is the reason a snippet you copy from a 2022 blog post still works on a 2026 codebase: the class names survived even when the engine underneath changed twice.", code_example = "@import \"tailwindcss\";\n\n@theme {\n  --color-mint-500: oklch(0.72 0.15 165);\n  --font-display: \"Inter\", \"system-ui\", \"sans-serif\";\n  --breakpoint-3xl: 1920px;\n}", pitfalls = "In v4 the source of truth is the `@theme` block. Grepping the config file for `--color-brand-500` returns nothing even when the brand colours render correctly. If a colour is unexplained, look at the CSS first.  Without the reset, Tailwind ships the full default palette (`slate`, `gray`, `red`, ...). Drop `--color-*: initial;` at the top of your `@theme` block to wipe the defaults and only emit the tokens you actually declare. Useful for strict design-system gates that forbid untokenised colours.  The `before:` and `after:` variants are designed for purely decorative glyphs (quote marks, icons, list markers). Writing a `<span>` for the same effect adds a DOM node a screen reader can reach, a `key`-collision source, and an extra element you must keep styling. Reach for `before:` first.  The hint costs memory per element. Apply it to the few elements that are about to animate, then remove the class when the animation finishes. A `will-change-*` blanket is a perf regression, not a fix.  The opposite of the fix you wanted. `touch-pan-x` claims horizontal pan only, leaving vertical pan free for the browser to interpret. For a vertical scroller that the browser keeps hijacking (overscroll, pull-to-refresh) use `touch-pan-y`. For full ownership of every gesture (a custom map, a drawing canvas, a game) use `touch-none`." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzE1OlYyOjAxNS10",
        raw_key = "S5:C15:V2",
        title = "Applying text and font styles",

        duration_text = "06m20s",
        duration_seconds = 380,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Tailwind theme variables, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [

          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2 · **Prerequisites:** Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Tailwind UI Ecommerce kit  Theme variables are the design-token layer of Tailwind v4. The release history is the reason a snippet you copy from a 2022 blog post still works on a 2026 codebase: the class names survived even when the engine underneath changed twice.", code_example = "@import \"tailwindcss\";\n\n@theme {\n  --color-mint-500: oklch(0.72 0.15 165);\n  --font-display: \"Inter\", \"system-ui\", \"sans-serif\";\n  --breakpoint-3xl: 1920px;\n}", pitfalls = "In v4 the source of truth is the `@theme` block. Grepping the config file for `--color-brand-500` returns nothing even when the brand colours render correctly. If a colour is unexplained, look at the CSS first.  Without the reset, Tailwind ships the full default palette (`slate`, `gray`, `red`, ...). Drop `--color-*: initial;` at the top of your `@theme` block to wipe the defaults and only emit the tokens you actually declare. Useful for strict design-system gates that forbid untokenised colours.  The `before:` and `after:` variants are designed for purely decorative glyphs (quote marks, icons, list markers). Writing a `<span>` for the same effect adds a DOM node a screen reader can reach, a `key`-collision source, and an extra element you must keep styling. Reach for `before:` first.
