  {
    id = "05-016",
    number = 16,
    title = "Custom color tokens",
    local_chapter_file = "016-custom-color-tokens.md",
    key_concepts = ["Hook: declaring a colour once, naming it everywhere", "The `@theme` block, restated for colours", "The default palette at a glance", "Slash opacity: tune alpha without leaving the className", "Border utilities: width, style, colour, radius, sides", "Divider utilities: borders between children, not around the parent"],
    total_duration_seconds = 1143,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzE2OlYxOjAxNi1j",
        raw_key = "S5:C16:V1",
        title = "Project: Borders, spacing, and color",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/project-borders-spacing-and-color",
        duration_text = "11m38s",
        duration_seconds = 698,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Custom color tokens, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "CSS Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1–2 · **Prerequisites:** Tailwind theme variables, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Tailwind UI Ecommerce kit  The previous chapter introduced `@theme` as a CSS-first config that emits CSS custom properties and the matching utility families in one declaration. This chapter zooms in on the most-used namespace, `--color-*`, and the family of utility classes it plumbs into. The vocabulary you build here is the one you will actually reach for on every styled component.", mental_model = "The `--color-*` namespace is the connective tissue between a Tailwind project and its brand. Every UI control that takes a colour (backgrounds, text, borders, rings, outlines, SVG fill and stroke, form-control accents, insertion cursors, text-decoration lines, shadow colour, scrollbar colour, dividers between children, gradient stops) reads from the same namespace. Designing a colour once, then letting that single declaration plumb through fourteen utility families, is the design-system guarantee the rest of the styling chapter builds on.  You will meet the surrounding utilities in every styled component from here on: text-decoration utilities paint brand underlines on links, the slash-opacity form appears in half the design-token rules, the `divide-*` family gives list views their visual rhythm, SVG utilities paint icon-colour-through-text-colour through the `fill-current` pair. The internalisation of \"the prefix names the property; the namespace names the family\" is what makes reading a Tailwind class list feel like reading CSS.", code_example = "@theme {\n  --color-brand-500: oklch(0.62 0.16 256);\n}", pitfalls = "`text-brand-500` paints `color`. `bg-brand-500` paints `background-color`. `border-brand-500` paints `border-color`. Forgetting which prefix you need is the single most common slip when a custom token lands. The rule: the prefix names the property you want to paint.  Without the reset, the default palette ships 22 families and the bundle keeps every utility, even the ones the design system forbids. `--color-*: initial;` at the top of `@theme` wipes the default and only emits the tokens you redeclare. The escape hatch for a one-off neutral is to redeclare `--color-white`, `--color-black`, or `--color-slate-*` explicitly.  CSS-in-JS muscle memory. The `text-brand-500` utility compiles to `color: var(--color-brand-500)` and gets the slash-opacity form (`/40`), the dark-mode variant (`dark:text-brand-500`), the responsive variant (`md:text-brand-500`), and the hover variant (`hover:text-brand-500`) for free. The inline style gives you none of those.  The arbitrary-value escape hatch is for one-offs and prototyping. A literal hex in a class list is not a token; it is a string the engine has no way to reuse. Promoting it to `--color-brand-cobalt: #abcdef;` in `@theme` makes the colour re-skinnable later.  `border-x-2` paints the inline edges of the parent. `divide-x-2` paints borders *between* children. On the same element, they do not conflict (they target different borders), but the visual effect is usually not what the author intended: the parent's inline edges plus the children's seam borders. Decide whether you want a parent border or inter-child dividers, then pick one family." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzE2OlYyOjAxNi1j",
        raw_key = "S5:C16:V2",
        title = "Adding borders and shadows",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/adding-borders-and-shadows",
        duration_text = "07m25s",
        duration_seconds = 445,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Custom color tokens, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1–2 · **Prerequisites:** Tailwind theme variables, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Tailwind UI Ecommerce kit  The previous chapter introduced `@theme` as a CSS-first config that emits CSS custom properties and the matching utility families in one declaration. Without the reset, the default palette ships 22 families and the bundle keeps every utility, even the ones the design system forbids. `--color-*: initial;` at the top of `@theme` wipes the default and only emits the tokens you redeclare.   {
    id = "05-017",
    number = 17,
    title = "Outline styles",
    local_chapter_file = "017-outline-styles.md",
    key_concepts = ["Hook: outline vs border, and why the difference matters", "Outline style: pick the line pattern", "Outline width: the width scale and the shorthand", "Position utilities: `top-*`, `right-*`, `bottom-*`, `left-*`, `inset-*`", "Margin utilities and `space-*`", "Padding utilities"],
    total_duration_seconds = 656,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzE3OlYxOjAxNy1v",
        raw_key = "S5:C17:V1",
        title = "Positioning with grid lines",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/positioning-with-grid-lines",
        duration_text = "03m31s",
        duration_seconds = 211,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Outline styles, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "CSS Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1–2 · **Prerequisites:** Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Tailwind color palette, Tailwind theme variables, Custom color tokens, Tailwind focus indicator and outline utilities  The chapter title picks up where the focus-ring chapter left off: an outline is the line drawn around an element, separate from its border, drawn outside the box, and invisible to layout. This chapter covers the full **outline style** family (`outline-solid`, `outline-dashed`, `outline-dotted`, `outline-double`, `outline-hidden`, `outline-none`), the **outline width** scale, the **position** utilities that decide *where* the outline goes (and where every other absolutely-positioned child sits), then sweeps the smaller utility families that fill out a styled component: margin, padding, typography, vertical alignment, tab size, text transform, text color, text indent, background, table layout, table collapse, and SVG fill/stroke. One chapter, many utility families, all already in the `theme` namespace you built earlier.", mental_model = "Outline styles are the safe accessibility surface (a focus ring that does not shift layout, that high-contrast mode can force to a system color). Position utilities turn a relative parent into a coordinate system so badges, modals, and tooltips pin to a corner instead of the viewport. Margin and padding are the two halves of \"outer space vs inner space\" and getting them right is most of the day-to-day layout work. Typography, vertical alignment, tab size, text transform, text color, and text indent are the small utilities you reach for on every paragraph, every label, every code block. Background utilities paint the area behind the content, including the gradients and patterns that show up in every modern landing page. Table layout and table collapse are the missing piece for any data grid that has a \"show/hide column\" toggle. SVG fill and stroke let icon libraries match the brand palette without per-component overrides.", code_example = "<button className=\"border-2 border-sky-500 outline outline-2 outline-offset-2 outline-dashed outline-rose-500\">\n  Save\n</button>", pitfalls = "1. **Assuming `outline-none` removes the outline.** It does not. Tailwind's `outline-none` renders a transparent outline that high-contrast mode can force to a system color. Use `outline-hidden` if you really mean \"no outline, ever.\" 2. **Putting `outline-2` on a button that does not also set `outline-solid` (or another style).** The default `outline-style` is `none`, so a button with `outline outline-2 outline-rose-500` and no style class has a 2px rose outline at rest and no outline on focus (because `outline-none` clears it). Add `outline-solid` (or `outline-dashed` / `outline-dotted`) so the outline is actually drawn. 3. **Using `top-2 right-2` on a `relative` parent that is itself inside an `overflow-hidden` ancestor.** The badge pins to the parent's box, not the visible region; if the parent is taller than its container the badge stays glued to the parent's edge and may scroll out of view. Test with long content. 4. **Interpreting `inset-0` as \"place at 0,0.\"** `inset-0` sets `top`, `right`, `bottom`, and `left` to `0`. Combined with `absolute`, that *stretches* the element to fill its containing block (because both edges are pinned). `inset-0` on a `relative` element does nothing visual. 5. **Writing `` `m-${size}` `` as a dynamic class.** The Tailwind source scanner only sees complete literal tokens. A template that interpolates the size never generates the CSS, and the layout collapses to whatever the browser default is. Use a lookup of complete literals (`m-2`, `m-4`, `m-8`) keyed by size. 6. **Swapping `margin` for `padding` to \"make the click target bigger.\"** Padding grows the element; margin grows the gap to its siblings. The click target grows in both cases, but the visual outcome differs: padding eats into the parent's space, margin pushes siblings. Pick by the visual you want. 7. **Using `space-y-<n>` on a flex container.** `space-y-*` only adds margins between plain siblings; in a flex container the margins compose with `gap-*`, often producing dou" }
      },

        id = "UzU6QzE3OlYyOjAxNy1v",
        raw_key = "S5:C17:V2",
        title = "Project: Grid line positioning",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/project-grid-line-positioning",

        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Outline styles, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "CSS Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1–2 · **Prerequisites:** Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Tailwind color palette, Tailwind theme variables, Custom color tokens, Tailwind focus indicator and outline utilities  The chapter title picks up where the focus-ring chapter left off: an outline is the line drawn around an element, separate from its border, drawn outside the box, and invisible to layout. This chapter covers the full **outline style** family (`outline-solid`, `outline-dashed`, `outline-dotted`, `outline-double`, `outline-hidden`, `outline-none`), the **outline width** scale, the **position** utilities that decide *where* the outline goes (and where every other absolutely-positioned child sits), then sweeps the smaller utility families that fill out a styled component: margin, padding, typography, vertical alignment, tab size, text transform, text color, text indent, background, table layout, table collapse, and SVG fill/stroke. One chapter, many utility families, all already in the `theme` namespace you built earlier.", mental_model = "Outline styles are the safe accessibility surface (a focus ring that does not shift layout, that high-contrast mode can force to a system color). Position utilities turn a relative parent into a coordinate system so badges, modals, and tooltips pin to a corner instead of the viewport. Margin and padding are the two halves of \"outer space vs inner space\" and getting them right is most of the day-to-day layout work. Typography, vertical alignment, tab size, text transform, text color, and text indent are the small utilities you reach for on every paragraph, every label, every code block. Background utilities paint the area behind the content, including the gradients and patterns that show up in every modern landing page. Table layout and table collapse are the missing piece for any data grid that has a \"show/hide column\" toggle. SVG fill and stroke let icon libraries match the brand palette without per-component overrides.", code_example = "<button className=\"border-2 border-sky-500 outline outline-2 outline-offset-2 outline-dashed outline-rose-500\">\n  Save\n</button>", pitfalls = "1. **Assuming `outline-none` removes the outline.** It does not. Tailwind's `outline-none` renders a transparent outline that high-contrast mode can force to a system color. Use `outline-hidden` if you really mean \"no outline, ever.\" 2. **Putting `outline-2` on a button that does not also set `outline-solid` (or another style).** The default `outline-style` is `none`, so a button with `outline outline-2 outline-rose-500` and no style class has a 2px rose outline at rest and no outline on focus (because `outline-none` clears it). Add `outline-solid` (or `outline-dashed` / `outline-dotted`) so the outline is actually drawn. 3. **Using `top-2 right-2` on a `relative` parent that is itself inside an `overflow-hidden` ancestor.** The badge pins to the parent's box, not the visible region; if the parent is taller than its container the badge stays glued to the parent's edge and may scroll out of view. Test with long content. 4. **Interpreting `inset-0` as \"place at 0,0.\"** `inset-0` sets `top`, `right`, `bottom`, and `left` to `0`. Combined with `absolute`, that *stretches* the element to fill its containing block (because both edges are pinned). `inset-0` on a `relative` element does nothing visual. 5. **Writing `` `m-${size}` `` as a dynamic class.** The Tailwind source scanner only sees complete literal tokens. A template that interpolates the size never generates the CSS, and the layout collapses to whatever the browser default is. Use a lookup of complete literals (`m-2`, `m-4`, `m-8`) keyed by size. 6. **Swapping `margin` for `padding` to \"make the click target bigger.\"** Padding grows the element; margin grows the gap to its siblings. The click target grows in both cases, but the visual outcome differs: padding eats into the parent's space, margin pushes siblings. Pick by the visual you want. 7. **Using `space-y-<n>` on a flex container.** `space-y-*` only adds margins between plain siblings; in a flex container the margins compose with `gap-*`, often producing dou" }

    title = "stroke",

    key_concepts = ["The `stroke-*` family", "`stroke-width` and the SVG attributes that stay on the element", "Text wrapping for the strings inside SVG `<text>`", "`overflow-wrap` for unbreakable strings", "Transforms: scale, rotate, translate, skew", "Variants: stacking prefixes and conditional rules"],
    total_duration_seconds = 888,
    videos = [

        title = "Project: Aligning elements with Flexbox",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/project-aligning-elements-with-flexbox",
        duration_text = "06m20s",
        duration_seconds = 380,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of stroke, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "CSS Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-2 · **Prerequisites:** Outline styles, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Tailwind theme variables, Custom color tokens  SVG icons live in nearly every React UI: a tiny check inside a button, a settings cog on a profile menu, a chart axis. The stroke is the line that draws the shape, and Tailwind gives you a `stroke-*` family that mirrors `text-*` and `bg-*` for color plus a `stroke-none` / `stroke-inherit` / `stroke-current` / `stroke-transparent` set that mirrors how every other color utility handles edge cases. Once you can read and write `stroke-*`, the rest of this chapter is the supporting vocabulary that tends to show up next to it: text wrapping and line clamping for the long strings inside SVG `<text>` elements, transforms for the icon hover animations, variant prefixes and state utilities for `hover:stroke-...`, arbitrary values for the one-off color from your brand tokens, and the dark-mode and pseudo-element patterns that finish the look.", mental_model = "SVG icons are the visible load-bearing element of a Tailwind UI. Once you know that `stroke-current` tracks the parent's text color and the rest of the `stroke-*` family mirrors `text-*` / `bg-*`, you can re-skin icons across a design system by changing the parent's `text-*` and nothing else. The text-wrapping family covers the long strings that show up in SVG `<text>` and in `<p>` elements next to the icon. The variant and state utilities (`hover:`, `focus-visible:`, `dark:`, `group-hover:`) are the conditional layer that makes every state transition a one-line edit. Transforms give you the hover animations without writing keyframes. Arbitrary values and arbitrary properties cover the one-off color from your brand tokens and the CSS property Tailwind does not yet own.  Together these utilities are the 60% of \"icon-and-text\" UI you reach for 90% of the time. The remaining chapters cover transitions, shadows, filters, and the layout primitives (`flex`, `grid`) that house them.", code_example = "<svg viewBox=\"0 0 24 24\" className=\"size-6 stroke-current text-sky-600\" fill=\"none\">\n  <path d=\"M5 12l4 4L19 6\" strokeWidth={2} strokeLinecap=\"round\" strokeLinejoin=\"round\" />\n</svg>", pitfalls = "`text-sky-500` on an `<svg>` element sets the `color` property, which is what `stroke-current` reads. It is a valid shortcut when the path itself is `stroke-current`, but it does not work if the path uses an explicit `stroke-*` color. Pick one mechanism per SVG: either every path uses `stroke-current` and the parent controls the color via `text-*`, or every path uses an explicit `stroke-<color>` and the parent's text color is irrelevant.  Many icon libraries ship paths with a default fill. If the icon renders as a solid blob instead of an outline, the path has a default fill that is painting over the stroke. Add `fill=\"none\"` to the `<svg>` (or the path) and the stroke shows through.  `text-balance` triggers a browser-native line-balancing algorithm on every render, and the cost scales with line count. The Tailwind docs note that browsers cap the algorithm at roughly six lines, after which the class becomes a no-op but the cost still applies on every layout pass. Use it on short headings only.  `content-['Save']` writes the literal \"Save\" into the DOM as pseudo-content, but screen readers announce it inconsistently across browsers and assistive tech, and the text is not selectable or copyable. If the text matters, use a real DOM element with text content. Pseudo-element content is for decorative marks (the arrow in the worked example, a required-field asterisk, a quote glyph).  By default `dark:*` utilities activate under `prefers-color-scheme: dark`. If you want an in-app toggle (a theme switcher in the header), you must configure Tailwind's dark mode strategy (`@custom-variant dark`) so the variant targets a class on the `<html>` instead of the OS preference. Otherwise your toggle does nothing and the dark styles only respond to the user's OS." }
      },
      {

        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-2 · **Prerequisites:** stroke, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Outline styles, Tailwind theme variables, Custom color tokens  The chapter title picks up a small but useful CSS trick: `content-[attr(name)]` reads a DOM attribute straight into a pseudo-element's generated text. Once you can do that, the rest of the chapter is the supporting vocabulary that tends to show up next to it: native form state variants (`required`, `invalid`, `checked`, `disabled`) so the browser does the conditional class work for you, perspective utilities for 3D transforms, responsive breakpoints and breakpoint configuration, accessible outline utilities, text color opacity modifiers, transform composition and reset, the `color-scheme` utilities, visual effects, white-space preservation, sizing and width, and the table-layout pair. One chapter, many small utility families, all already in the `theme` namespace you built earlier.", mental_model = "Generated content from attributes is the cheapest way to render a label without changing the markup. Form state variants keep the conditional class logic out of the template: the browser picks the rule, not the JSX. Perspective utilities, transform composition, and effects utility handle the 3D and visual polish. Text color opacity, white-space, sizing, and table layout are the small utilities you reach for on every paragraph, every label, every form field, every data grid. Once you can read and write them, the rest of the chapter's families are the same pattern: a short list of complete literal classes, one or two CSS keywords, and a worked example that combines them.", code_example = "<a\n  href=\"/docs/hooks\"\n  data-section=\"Hooks\"\n  className=\"relative no-underline hover:underline before:absolute before:-top-2 before:left-0 before:text-[10px] before:font-semibold before:uppercase before:tracking-widest before:text-rose-500 before:content-[attr(data-section)]\"\n>\n  useState\n</a>", pitfalls = "1. **Forgetting that `content-[attr(name)]` reads the attribute value literally, without CSS string interpolation.** The pseudo-element prints whatever the attribute value is, character for character. If the attribute is missing, the content is empty. There is no default fallback. 2. **Using `attr(name)` without a fallback for missing attributes.** If the attribute is not on the element, the pseudo-element renders nothing. Either declare the attribute on every element or pair `content-[attr(name)]` with a CSS-only fallback (uncommon, just always declare the attribute). 3. **Assuming `invalid:` only fires after form submission.** `:invalid` is live: it fires the moment the typed value fails the browser's constraint validation. That's the right behavior for a \"show the user what's wrong\" UI, but it also means the input is rose from the very first keystroke if the field is `required` and the value is empty. 4. **Applying `perspective-*` to the element being rotated.** The perspective belongs on the **parent** of the 3D-transformed element. Putting it on the rotated element itself does nothing for the transform, because the perspective reference for the child is the element's own box, which gets clipped by the rotation. 5. **Forgetting to add `transform-style: preserve-3d` to the rotated child.** Without `preserve-3d`, the child's own 3D children flatten to the parent's plane. There is no Tailwind utility for this; keep it as an inline `style={{ transformStyle: \"preserve-3d\" }}` or a custom class. 6. **Stacking `max-md:` with `md:` on the same utility.** The two variants are mutually exclusive: `max-md:` is the media range below 768px, `md:` is the range at 768px and up. The browser uses the last matching rule, so the order in the className matters and the visual can flip from one media query to the next. Pick one breakpoint system per utility. 7. **Using `outline-none` and thinking the focus ring is gone.** Tailwind's `outline-none` is a transparent outline that high-c" }
      },

  {
    id = "05-020",
    number = 20,
    title = "Combined width and height sizing",
    local_chapter_file = "020-combined-width-and-height-sizing.md",
    key_concepts = ["The mental model: size, bounds, and content-fit", "`min-w-*` and `max-w-*`", "`min-h-*` and `max-h-*`", "Logical-property sizing: `min-inline-size-*`, `max-inline-size-*`, `min-block-size-*`, `max-block-size-*`", "`size-*`: shorthand for width and height", "`aspect-*`: reserving a proportional box"],
    total_duration_seconds = 958,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzIwOlYxOjAyMC1j",
        raw_key = "S5:C20:V1",
        title = "Creating full-height layouts",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/creating-full-height-layouts",
        duration_text = "03m02s",
        duration_seconds = 182,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Combined width and height sizing, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "CSS Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "A Tailwind utility is a class name that maps to one CSS declaration. `size-12` sets both `width` and `height` to 3rem. `max-w-prose` caps `max-width` at 65ch. `aspect-video` reserves a 16:9 box. `object-cover` keeps a replaced image from distorting inside its box. `truncate` clips overflowing text with an ellipsis. You compose class names on the element and the browser applies the declarations they stand for.  This chapter is a reference roundup. The top covers the bounding utilities (`min-w-*`, `max-w-*`, `min-h-*`, `max-h-*`, plus their logical-property counterparts `min-inline-size-*`, `max-inline-size-*`, `min-block-size-*`, `max-block-size-*`). The middle covers the `size-*` shorthand and `aspect-*` for square and rectangular boxes. The tail covers replaced-content utilities (`object-fit`, `object-position`), overflow (`overflow-*`), and text-overflow truncation (`truncate`, `text-ellipsis`, `text-clip`).", mental_model = "A box on the page has three independent sizing decisions: how big it is by default (`size-*`, `w-*`, `h-*`), how it can stretch or shrink (`min-*`, `max-*`), and how its replaced content (an `<img>`, `<video>`, `<svg>`) fits inside the box it has been given (`object-fit`, `object-position`, `aspect-*`). Each layer composes with the next.  Logical-property utilities (`inline-size-*`, `block-size-*`) are the direction-aware replacements for the physical `width-*` / `height-*` family. They map to the inline axis (the direction text flows) and the block axis (the direction lines stack) respectively, so the same component lays out correctly in LTR and RTL documents without a conditional. For most React codebases that ship only in one direction, `w-*` and `h-*` are simpler and identical; reach for `inline-size-*` / `block-size-*` when a component needs to survive a locale change.", code_example = "// HTML from the Tailwind docs:     <div class=\"size-12 max-w-prose\">\n// The same thing in a React component:\n<div className=\"size-12 max-w-prose\">...</div>", pitfalls = "Direct DOM mutation or impure side effects during the render phase cause hydration mismatches, duplicate executions in Strict Mode, and stale closures. Always declare full dependency arrays in useEffect and never read state during render from outside the component tree." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzIwOlYyOjAyMC1j",
        raw_key = "S5:C20:V2",
        title = "CSS stylesheets: Global CSS and importing CSS",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/build-modern-web-apps-with-react-hooks-state-management-and-apis-using-vite-or-next-js/css-stylesheets-global-css-and-importing-css",
        duration_text = "12m56s",
        duration_seconds = 776,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Combined width and height sizing, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Build Modern Web Apps with React, Hooks, State Management, and APIs Using Vite or Next.js",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "A Tailwind utility is a class name that maps to one CSS declaration. `size-12` sets both `width` and `height` to 3rem. `max-w-prose` caps `max-width` at 65ch. `aspect-video` reserves a 16:9 box. `object-cover` keeps a replaced image from distorting inside its box. `truncate` clips overflowing text with an ellipsis. You compose class names on the element and the browser applies the declarations they stand for.  This chapter is a reference roundup. The top covers the bounding utilities (`min-w-*`, `max-w-*`, `min-h-*`, `max-h-*`, plus their logical-property counterparts `min-inline-size-*`, `max-inline-size-*`, `min-block-size-*`, `max-block-size-*`). The middle covers the `size-*` shorthand and `aspect-*` for square and rectangular boxes. The tail covers replaced-content utilities (`object-fit`, `object-position`), overflow (`overflow-*`), and text-overflow truncation (`truncate`, `text-ellipsis`, `text-clip`).", mental_model = "A box on the page has three independent sizing decisions: how big it is by default (`size-*`, `w-*`, `h-*`), how it can stretch or shrink (`min-*`, `max-*`), and how its replaced content (an `<img>`, `<video>`, `<svg>`) fits inside the box it has been given (`object-fit`, `object-position`, `aspect-*`). Each layer composes with the next.  Logical-property utilities (`inline-size-*`, `block-size-*`) are the direction-aware replacements for the physical `width-*` / `height-*` family. They map to the inline axis (the direction text flows) and the block axis (the direction lines stack) respectively, so the same component lays out correctly in LTR and RTL documents without a conditional. For most React codebases that ship only in one direction, `w-*` and `h-*` are simpler and identical; reach for `inline-size-*` / `block-size-*` when a component needs to survive a locale change.", code_example = "// HTML from the Tailwind docs:     <div class=\"size-12 max-w-prose\">\n// The same thing in a React component:\n<div className=\"size-12 max-w-prose\">...</div>", pitfalls = "Direct DOM mutation or impure side effects during the render phase cause hydration mismatches, duplicate executions in Strict Mode, and stale closures. Always declare full dependency arrays in useEffect and never read state during render from outside the component tree." }
      },
    ]
  },

        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2-3 · **Prerequisites:** Custom color tokens, Generated content from attributes, stroke, Combined width and height sizing, Tailwind theme variables  Backgrounds are where a UI leaves \"ordinary\" and starts showing texture. A solid color, an image that fills a card, a gradient that fades a hero, a parallax section that scrolls differently from its container, a marquee of icons that scrolls and snaps, a fully-painted text headline: each one of these is a property of the same CSS background machinery. Tailwind covers the whole family, and the chapters earlier in this section gave you the color tokens and the size primitives that the background utilities build on. This chapter is the bridge: it pulls those primitives together, names the families you reach for most, and pairs each one with a worked example.", mental_model = "Backgrounds are the CSS layer that runs above the box model but below the foreground. A `bg-*` family composes with the size, position, repeat, and origin utilities to express every visual relationship between an image and its container. Gradients, gradient text, and the slash-opacity modifier are what make modern card-based UI feasible: a single `bg-linear-to-r` block replaces a stylesheet of `linear-gradient(...)` declarations. The accessibility and environment variants (`motion-safe`, `pointer-fine`, `forced-colors`) close the loop by letting CSS adapt to the user's device and preferences without duplicating application logic.  Together with the color tokens from chapter 16, the stroke family from chapter 18, the sizing utilities from chapter 20, and the theme variables from chapter 15, this chapter completes the \"styling vocabulary\" you reach for in 80% of React UI work. The next chapters in this section move into filters, transitions, shadows, and the layout primitives that house them.", code_example = "<aside className=\"rounded-lg bg-sky-500/20 p-4 text-sky-900\n                  dark:bg-sky-500/10 dark:text-sky-100\">\n  Heads up: a sale ends tomorrow.\n</aside>", pitfalls = "The `bg-opacity-50` shorthand compiles to `background-color: rgb(... / 0.5)` in v3. In v4, that family is gone; the right pattern is `bg-sky-500/50`, which writes `color-mix(in oklab, ...)` and composes against the parent's background. Reaching for `bg-opacity-50` either fails to compile (no class generated) or, worse, compiles to a static color that does not respect the design tokens.  `bg-linear-to-r from-sky-500` writes a gradient with one stop. The CSS engine treats the other stop as the same color, so the result is a flat `bg-sky-500` rather than a gradient. Always include at least `from-*` and `to-*`; if you want a sharp band, use `from-sky-500 from-0% to-rose-500 to-0%` to put both stops at the boundary.  `bg-clip-text` clips the background to the text glyphs, but the default text color is opaque. The result is a colored background that is invisible because the text color sits on top of it. Always pair `bg-clip-text` with `text-transparent` so the text color is invisible and the gradient shows through.  `background-attachment: fixed` is a performance hazard on mobile. The browser has to repaint the entire background on every scroll, and many mobile browsers drop the rule entirely. The Tailwind pattern is `bg-fixed motion-reduce:bg-scroll` if you want the parallax, or `bg-scroll` plain if you do not need it. Never ship `bg-fixed` without testing on iOS Safari and Android Chrome.  `100vw` includes the scrollbar width on desktop browsers. A child with `w-screen` inside a fixed-width container can overflow horizontally because the scrollbar width is added on top of the container's width. The safe pattern is `w-full max-w-screen` or `w-dvw` for dynamic viewport sizing that does not include the scrollbar." }

  {
    id = "05-022",
    number = 22,
    title = "CSS transition utilities",
    local_chapter_file = "022-css-transition-utilities.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model: properties, transitions, and the GPU", "The `transition-*` family: property, duration, delay, behaviour", "`duration-*`, `delay-*`, `ease-*`: timing knobs", "`transition-discrete` and the `transition-behavior-*` utilities", "`forced-color-adjust` utilities: respect the user's high-contrast palette", "`appearance-none` and `appearance-auto`: paint your own form controls"],
    total_duration_seconds = 481,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzIyOlYxOjAyMi1j",
        raw_key = "S5:C22:V1",
        title = "Introduction to CSS Grid",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/intro-to-css-grid",
        duration_text = "02m49s",
        duration_seconds = 169,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of CSS transition utilities, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "CSS Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-3 · **Prerequisites:** stroke, Background color, image, size, and position utilities, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Tailwind theme variables, Generated content from attributes, Custom color tokens  The interactive parts of a Tailwind UI are a small vocabulary: properties transition from one value to another over a duration, transforms reposition elements in 2D or 3D space, animations run a keyframe loop, and a handful of accessibility utilities (`appearance`, `forced-colors`) keep the result usable when the operating system overrides your colours. This chapter is the working reference for that vocabulary.", mental_model = "CSS does three things to a value: it *computes* it (the layout), it *paints* it (the bitmap), and it *composites* it (the layer cake the browser shows on screen). A `transition` interpolates a value over time on each frame. A `transform` is a single property that the browser composes on a separate layer, so changing it rarely re-triggers layout. Animations are time-based value programs that run on the same pipeline. The families in this chapter are all parts of those three pipelines.  The GPU-acceleration utilities (`transform-gpu`, `transform-cpu`) and the `will-change`-style hints matter when a transform is animating frequently enough that the CPU form becomes the bottleneck. Reach for them when a transform is visibly janky, not as a default.", code_example = "<button className=\"bg-sky-500 text-white transition-colors hover:bg-sky-700\">\n  Save\n</button>\n<div className=\"transition-transform hover:scale-110 active:scale-95\" />\n<input className=\"transition focus:ring-2 focus:ring-sky-500\" />", pitfalls = "`hover:scale-110` writes `transform: scale(1.10)` on hover, with no transition. Add `transition` or `transition-transform` to animate it. The default duration is 150 ms and the default property list is the seven common colour / opacity properties; `transition-transform` is the right shorthand for a transform-only animation.  `transition-all` will animate the properties that *can* animate (opacity, in this case) but `display` is a discrete property that flips at frame zero. Add `transition-discrete` alongside `transition-all` (or `transition` + `transition-discrete`) so the discrete change waits for the transition window:  A 3D-flip card needs `backface-hidden` on each face, otherwise the back face's content shows through during the transition and the flip looks like a smear. Add `backface-hidden` (which writes `backface-visibility: hidden`) to every face of the flip.  `transform-gpu` forces the element onto a GPU compositor layer, which costs memory and upload time. Apply it only on elements that animate frequently or re-paint often. Static elements are fine on the CPU form.  `skew-y-6` pivots around the element's centre by default. If you wanted the parallelogram to lean on its bottom edge, add `origin-bottom` (or `origin-bottom-left` for a sharper tilt). The skew itself is correct; the visible result is off because the rotation pivot is wrong." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzIyOlYyOjAyMi1j",
        raw_key = "S5:C22:V2",
        title = "Setting up your environment",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/setting-up-your-environment",
        duration_text = "05m12s",
        duration_seconds = 312,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of CSS transition utilities, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [

          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-3 · **Prerequisites:** stroke, Background color, image, size, and position utilities, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Tailwind theme variables, Generated content from attributes, Custom color tokens  The interactive parts of a Tailwind UI are a small vocabulary: properties transition from one value to another over a duration, transforms reposition elements in 2D or 3D space, animations run a keyframe loop, and a handful of accessibility utilities (`appearance`, `forced-colors`) keep the result usable when the operating system overrides your colours. This chapter is the working reference for that vocabulary.", mental_model = "CSS does three things to a value: it *computes* it (the layout), it *paints* it (the bitmap), and it *composites* it (the layer cake the browser shows on screen). A `transition` interpolates a value over time on each frame. A `transform` is a single property that the browser composes on a separate layer, so changing it rarely re-triggers layout. Animations are time-based value programs that run on the same pipeline. The families in this chapter are all parts of those three pipelines.  The GPU-acceleration utilities (`transform-gpu`, `transform-cpu`) and the `will-change`-style hints matter when a transform is animating frequently enough that the CPU form becomes the bottleneck. Reach for them when a transform is visibly janky, not as a default.", code_example = "<button className=\"bg-sky-500 text-white transition-colors hover:bg-sky-700\">\n  Save\n</button>\n<div className=\"transition-transform hover:scale-110 active:scale-95\" />\n<input className=\"transition focus:ring-2 focus:ring-sky-500\" />", pitfalls = "`hover:scale-110` writes `transform: scale(1.10)` on hover, with no transition. Add `transition` or `transition-transform` to animate it. The default duration is 150 ms and the default property list is the seven common colour / opacity properties; `transition-transform` is the right shorthand for a transform-only animation.  `transition-all` will animate the properties that *can* animate (opacity, in this case) but `display` is a discrete property that flips at frame zero. Add `transition-discrete` alongside `transition-all` (or `transition` + `transition-discrete`) so the discrete change waits for the transition window:  A 3D-flip card needs `backface-hidden` on each face, otherwise the back face's content shows through during the transition and the flip looks like a smear. Add `backface-hidden` (which writes `backface-visibility: hidden`) to every face of the flip.  `transform-gpu` forces the element onto a GPU compositor layer, which costs memory and upload time. Apply it only on elements that animate frequently or re-paint often. Static elements are fine on the CPU form.  `skew-y-6` pivots around the element's centre by default. The skew itself is correct; the visible result is off because the rotation pivot is wrong." }

  {
    id = "05-023",
    number = 23,
    title = "Z-axis translation",
    local_chapter_file = "023-z-axis-translation.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model: Z translation is just one coordinate on a 3D scene", "Reference roundup", "Putting it together: a Z-lifted feature spotlight", "Common mistakes", "Why this matters", "Recall check"],
    total_duration_seconds = 308,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzIzOlYxOjAyMy16",
        raw_key = "S5:C23:V1",
        title = "Extracting common styles",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/extracting-common-styles",
        duration_text = "03m26s",
        duration_seconds = 206,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Z-axis translation, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-3 · **Prerequisites:** CSS transition utilities, stroke, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Background color, image, size, and position utilities, Custom color tokens, Generated content from attributes, Tailwind theme variables  The headline concept is `translate-z-*`, the utility that pushes an element toward or away from the viewer along the Z axis. Everything else in this chapter is a Tailwind v4 reference roundup for the small vocabulary that surrounds it: arbitrary values, arbitrary variants, direct-child and descendant selectors, container queries, breakpoints, the `container` utility, mobile-first composition, `background-blend-mode` utilities, `transform-gpu` acceleration, grid and spacing utilities, CSS-first configuration, custom styles, and theme overrides. Each is a one-paragraph reference with a one-line example. A worked section at the end shows how the pieces combine on a real \"feature spotlight\" card with a Z-axis lift.", mental_model = "A 2D transform lives on the page plane (X and Y). A 3D transform adds depth (Z). `translate-z-12` writes `transform: translate3d(0, 0, 12px)` (or the equivalent matrix call), pushing the element 12 pixels toward the viewer. Without a perspective on the ancestor, the value is a flat translate; the element moves \"toward the camera\" only visually. With `transform-3d` (which writes `transform-style: preserve-3d`) on an ancestor that has `perspective` set, the move reads as actual depth.  The Z translation only renders visibly when there is a `perspective` somewhere up the tree. The most common source of \"nothing happened\" is a missing `perspective-[value]` on a grandparent. Tailwind ships `perspective-near` (small depth), `perspective-far` (large depth), `perspective-<number>` (a numeric scale), and the arbitrary/pixel forms.", code_example = "<div className=\"perspective-[800px]\">\n  <div className=\"transform-3d transition-transform duration-300 ease-out\n                  hover:translate-z-12 hover:rotate-x-6\">\n    Spotlight\n  </div>\n</div>", pitfalls = "1. **Z translation without `perspective`.** A `translate-z-12` on a parent with no perspective looks like a flat 2D translate. Add `perspective-[<value>]` to a grandparent, or use `transform-3d` plus a perspective utility on the element itself. 2. **Z translation without `transform-3d`.** The parent of the lifted element needs `transform-style: preserve-3d` (Tailwind's `transform-3d`) so the Z translation is preserved on the move rather than collapsed. Without it, the rendered result is a flat translate. 3. **Negative Z without expected \"behind\" siblings.** A `translate-z-(-12)` pulls an element away from the viewer; it does not put it behind other elements. Use `z-*` (z-index utilities) for stacking. Z translation and z-index are orthogonal. 4. **`transform-gpu` on every element.** Promoting every element to a compositor layer fills the GPU memory budget and slows paint. Use it on elements that animate transforms; leave it off the rest. 5. **`*:p-2` followed by a child `p-2`.** The wildcard rule applies to direct children; a child's own `p-2` has equal specificity but sits later in the cascade, so the child's value wins. If you want the child *forced* to inherit a value, use a more specific selector (`[&>h2]:p-2` on the parent is the same effect, but a child cannot override an inline `style`). 6. **`max-md:` as if it were a min-width.** `max-md:hidden` matches screens strictly below `md`; it does not add to the `md` rule. For \"from `md` upward\", use `md:hidden` or `md:flex` (a clear positive statement is easier to reason about than a negative)." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzIzOlYyOjAyMy16",
        raw_key = "S5:C23:V2",
        title = "Common web patterns",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/vibe-coding-common-web-patterns-into-tailwind-css/common-web-patterns-25824012",
        duration_text = "01m42s",
        duration_seconds = 102,

          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-3 · **Prerequisites:** CSS transition utilities, stroke, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Background color, image, size, and position utilities, Custom color tokens, Generated content from attributes, Tailwind theme variables  The headline concept is `translate-z-*`, the utility that pushes an element toward or away from the viewer along the Z axis. A 3D transform adds depth (Z). `translate-z-12` writes `transform: translate3d(0, 0, 12px)` (or the equivalent matrix call), pushing the element 12 pixels toward the viewer. Without a perspective on the ancestor, the value is a flat translate; the element moves \"toward the camera\" only visually. With `transform-3d` (which writes `transform-style: preserve-3d`) on an ancestor that has `perspective` set, the move reads as actual depth.  The Z translation only renders visibly when there is a `perspective` somewhere up the tree. The most common source of \"nothing happened\" is a missing `perspective-[value]` on a grandparent. Tailwind ships `perspective-near` (small depth), `perspective-far` (large depth), `perspective-<number>` (a numeric scale), and the arbitrary/pixel forms.", code_example = "<div className=\"perspective-[800px]\">\n  <div className=\"transform-3d transition-transform duration-300 ease-out\n                  hover:translate-z-12 hover:rotate-x-6\">\n    Spotlight\n  </div>\n</div>", pitfalls = "1. **Z translation without `perspective`.** A `translate-z-12` on a parent with no perspective looks like a flat 2D translate. Add `perspective-[<value>]` to a grandparent, or use `transform-3d` plus a perspective utility on the element itself. 2. **Z translation without `transform-3d`.** The parent of the lifted element needs `transform-style: preserve-3d` (Tailwind's `transform-3d`) so the Z translation is preserved on the move rather than collapsed. Without it, the rendered result is a flat translate. 3. **Negative Z without expected \"behind\" siblings.** A `translate-z-(-12)` pulls an element away from the viewer; it does not put it behind other elements. Use `z-*` (z-index utilities) for stacking. Z translation and z-index are orthogonal. 4. **`transform-gpu` on every element.** Promoting every element to a compositor layer fills the GPU memory budget and slows paint. Use it on elements that animate transforms; leave it off the rest. 5. **`*:p-2` followed by a child `p-2`.** The wildcard rule applies to direct children; a child's own `p-2` has equal specificity but sits later in the cascade, so the child's value wins. If you want the child *forced* to inherit a value, use a more specific selector (`[&>h2]:p-2` on the parent is the same effect, but a child cannot override an inline `style`). 6. **`max-md:` as if it were a min-width.** `max-md:hidden` matches screens strictly below `md`; it does not add to the `md` rule. For \"from `md` upward\", use `md:hidden` or `md:flex` (a clear positive statement is easier to reason about than a negative)." }
      },
    ]
  },

        notes = { definition = "Tailwind's flex and grid utilities are a thin vocabulary over the CSS box model. Each class name maps to one CSS declaration: `justify-center` is `justify-content: center`, `items-start` is `align-items: flex-start`, `order-2` is `order: 2`, `space-x-4` is `margin-left: 1rem` on every child after the first. You compose class names on the element and the browser applies the declarations they stand for.  This chapter is a reference roundup. The first half covers the flex/grid alignment families in two pairs: container-side (`justify-content`, `align-items`, `justify-items`) and item-side (`justify-self`, `align-self`, `order`). The middle covers spacing siblings (`space-x-*`, `space-y-*`), the flex sizing primitives (`flex`, `flex-1`, `grow`, `shrink`), and grid template tracks (`grid-cols-*`, `grid-rows-*`). The tail covers multi-column flow (`columns-*`) and the `break-*` family that controls whether pages or columns may split an element.", mental_model = "CSS flex and grid layout both describe how items sit inside a container. The container has a main axis (the direction the items are laid out) and a cross axis (the perpendicular direction). For each axis you have two kinds of choices: a container-side rule that applies to every item, and an item-side rule that overrides the container for one item.  Tailwind gives each of these slots a utility family. The container-side families end in a plural or a generic verb (`justify-*`, `items-*`, `justify-items-*`). The item-side families end in `self-*` (`justify-self-*`, `self-*`). The `order-*` family sits outside the axis model: it changes the visual order of items without touching the source order in the DOM.", code_example = "// HTML from the Tailwind docs: <div class=\"flex items-center justify-between\">\n// The same thing in a React component:\n<div className=\"flex items-center justify-between\">...</div>", pitfalls = "`break-inside-*` controls whether a page or column may split the contents of an element. `break-after-*` controls whether a page or column break is created immediately after an element. The values: `auto` (default), `avoid` (no break), `avoid-page`, `avoid-column`, `page`, `column`.  `break-inside-avoid` on a figure inside a `columns-3` article keeps the figure intact in one column, then resumes the text flow in the next. Without it, the browser may split the figure between two columns." }

    id = "05-025",
    number = 25,
    title = "Break before an element",
    local_chapter_file = "025-break-before-an-element.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model: breaks and two-axis shortcuts", "`break-before-*` and `break-after-*`: force a break, then resume", "`place-content-*`: two-axis content distribution for the container", "`place-items-*`: set per-cell alignment once on the container", "`place-self-*`: override container alignment for one item", "`space-x-*` / `space-y-*` vs `gap`: when each one is right"],
    total_duration_seconds = 375,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzI1OlYxOjAyNS1i",
        raw_key = "S5:C25:V1",
        title = "What are container components?",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/react-design-patterns-25656257/what-are-container-components",
        duration_text = "01m50s",
        duration_seconds = 110,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Break before an element, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "React: Design Patterns",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2-3 · **Prerequisites:** Tailwind justify-items utilities, Z-axis translation, Generated content from attributes, scale utilities, Tailwind UI Ecommerce kit, Tailwind theme variables, stroke  The headline concept is `break-before-*`, the family of utilities that ask the browser to start the next page, column, or region on a fresh break right before an element. Everything else in this chapter is a Tailwind v4 reference roundup: `break-after-*` complements it, and a block of two-axis alignment shortcuts (`place-content-*`, `place-items-*`, `place-self-*`) replaces the verbose `justify-items-*` plus `align-items-*` boilerplate. A short note at the end covers `space-*` versus `gap` so you stop reaching for `space-y-4` on grids, then four framework installation guides (Gatsby, Laravel + Vite, Meteor, Ember.js), a note on layer-aware purge, the `@plugin` directive for legacy Tailwind v3 plugins, and the relationship-bound state variants (`group-*`, `peer-*`, `has-*`, `aria-*`, `data-*`).", mental_model = "A break (`break-before-*` / `break-after-*` / `break-inside-*`) is an instruction the layout engine obeys when it is splitting content across pages, columns, or regions. `columns-3` from chapter 024 turns an article into a three-column newspaper flow; `break-before-column` on the next `<h2>` forces that heading to start a new column. `place-content-*` and friends are vocabulary shortcuts: each one writes two CSS declarations, mirroring the relationship between `justify-content` and `align-content` on a container, or between `justify-self` and `align-self` on an item.  Every entry in this chapter is a thin layer over an existing CSS declaration. The value is not new power, it is fewer characters to type and a consistent vocabulary across the team.", code_example = "<article className=\"columns-3 gap-8\">\n  <p>...</p>\n  <h2 className=\"break-before-column text-xl font-semibold mt-6\">Section A</h2>\n  <p>...</p>\n  <h2 className=\"break-before-column text-xl font-semibold mt-6\">Section B</h2>\n</article>", pitfalls = "Direct DOM mutation or impure side effects during the render phase cause hydration mismatches, duplicate executions in Strict Mode, and stale closures. Always declare full dependency arrays in useEffect and never read state during render from outside the component tree." }
      },

    id = "05-026",
    number = 26,
    title = "Outline width",
    local_chapter_file = "026-outline-width.md",
    key_concepts = ["Hook: three independent knobs on one line", "Outline width: the number is pixels", "Outline color: the same palette you already use", "Outline offset: push the line out (or in)", "Ring: a focus indicator built from box-shadow", "Scale: resize visually without touching layout"],
    total_duration_seconds = 451,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzI2OlYxOjAyNi1v",
        raw_key = "S5:C26:V1",
        title = "Project: Grid layout",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/project-grid-layout",
        duration_text = "04m36s",
        duration_seconds = 276,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Outline width, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "CSS Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1 · **Prerequisites:** Outline styles, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Custom color tokens, Tailwind theme variables, CSS transition utilities, Break before an element  The outline-styles chapter taught you that an outline is a line drawn *outside* the box, invisible to layout, and the safe surface for a keyboard focus ring. This chapter zooms in on the three utilities that decide how that line looks (`outline-<width>`, `outline-<color>`, `outline-offset-<n>`), the `ring-*` alternative built from box-shadow, the `scale-*` transform, the `underline` / `line-through` text-decoration family, and the four transition utilities that animate all of it: `transition-<group>`, `duration-<n>`, `delay-<n>`, and `ease-*`. Every one of these is difficulty 1. The value is in the combinations: a focus ring that fades in over 200ms, a card that scales up on hover, a \"done\" item that strikes through with a coloured line.", mental_model = "Outline width, colour, and offset are the accessibility surface: a focus ring that a keyboard user can see, that high-contrast mode can force to a system colour, and that never shifts layout when it appears. The ring utilities give you the same indicator when you want it to hug rounded corners. Scale is the hover/press affordance on every interactive card and button in a modern UI. Text-decoration lines carry meaning (a struck-through price, a completed task) that colour alone should not carry. And the four transition families are what separate a UI that snaps harshly from one that feels considered, all without a single line of JavaScript animation code. You will apply these together on nearly every button, link, and card you build for the rest of the course.", code_example = "<button className=\"outline-2 outline-sky-500 outline-offset-2\">Save</button>", pitfalls = "1. **`outline-2` with no colour on a light background.** The width is set, but `outline-color` defaults to the element's `currentColor` (its text colour). On a white button with dark text you get a dark ring you did not design. Always pair a width with an explicit `outline-<color>` (or `ring-<color>`). 2. **Expecting `outline-offset` to animate the box.** It animates the *gap*, not the box size, which is exactly why it is safe. If you meant to grow the element, that is `scale-*` or a size utility, not offset. 3. **Reaching for `transition-all` by default.** It animates every property, including layout properties like `height` and `width` that reflow. A hover that changes `height` under `transition-all` will visibly lurch. Use the narrowest group: `transition-colors`, `transition-transform`, `transition-shadow`. 4. **Setting `duration-*` or `ease-*` without a `transition-*` group.** `duration-300` alone does nothing; there is no transition to time. The group class (`transition`, `transition-colors`, …) is what turns animation on. Duration, easing, and delay only modify an already-declared transition. 5. **Animating a `ring` with `transition-colors`.** A ring is a box-shadow, not a colour property. To animate a ring appearing, use `transition-shadow` (or `transition`, which includes shadow). `transition-colors` will animate `ring-color` changes but not the ring *width* growing from 0. 6. **Interpolating a dynamic class like `` `outline-${n}` ``.** Tailwind's scanner only sees complete literal tokens in your source. A template-built class name never generates the CSS and the outline silently disappears. Use a lookup of full literals (`outline-2`, `outline-4`) keyed by the variable. 7. **Using `-scale-x-100` and wondering why text reads backwards.** A negative scale mirrors the element, including its text and children. Mirror only decorative elements (an icon), not content." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzI2OlYyOjAyNi1v",
        raw_key = "S5:C26:V2",
        title = "Testing responsive layouts",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/testing-responsive-layouts",

        duration_seconds = 175,

    id = "05-027",
    number = 27,
    title = "Transition property utilities",
    local_chapter_file = "027-transition-property-utilities.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model: an accessibility-first motion vocabulary", "`transition-property-(*)` and `transition-[<list>]`: named property families", "`transition-discrete` and `transition-normal`: discrete-property behaviour", "`motion-reduce:` and `motion-safe:` variants", "`--ease-*` theme variables: custom easing as a design token", "`zoom-*` utilities: the CSS `zoom` property"],
    total_duration_seconds = 565,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzI3OlYxOjAyNy10",
        raw_key = "S5:C27:V1",
        title = "The color property and value",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/the-color-property-and-value",
        duration_text = "03m55s",
        duration_seconds = 235,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Transition property utilities, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "CSS Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-3 · **Prerequisites:** CSS transition utilities, Break before an element, Outline styles, Outline width, Background color, image, size, and position utilities, Tailwind theme variables, stroke  This chapter is the wider Tailwind vocabulary that surrounds `transition-*` and the transform families. It collects the property-list extensions, the accessibility-aware variants, the interactivity utilities, the motion-preference variants, and the few transform-adjacent families that did not fit in the CSS transition utilities chapter. Each section is short on prose and dense on tables.", mental_model = "A Tailwind UI is a contract with the user as much as it is a set of classes. Each interactive utility answers a question: *what animates*, *who gets it*, *when does it fire*, *does the user know it's moving*. The contracts layer on top of each other:  Read the class string once per layer. The first layer says what animates. The second layer says which user states it applies to. The third layer says how it degrades for users who opted out.", code_example = "@import \"tailwindcss\";\n\n@theme {\n  --card-properties: width, height, color, background-color, box-shadow;\n}", pitfalls = "// right: branch between complete strings. className={kind === \"color\" ? \"transition-colors\" : \"transition-transform\"} ```  The full class name is never a literal in a scanned file, so the scanner does not generate CSS for it. The style silently does nothing. Branch between complete strings or build a `Record<Kind, string>` of complete class strings.  A user who has asked the OS for less motion still sees the lift. The WCAG-recommended fix is to pair every animated utility with `motion-reduce:`:  Forgetting the `motion-reduce:` on a *single* utility breaks the user's accessibility setting without warning. Audit class strings for any `transition-*`, `duration-*`, `delay-*`, `animate-*`, `scale-*`, `rotate-*`, or `translate-*`; each is a candidate.  The `focus:outline-none` removes the browser's native focus ring without providing a replacement. A keyboard user has no way to tell where focus is. The right pattern is a focus-visible rule with a visible replacement:" }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzI3OlYyOjAyNy10",
        raw_key = "S5:C27:V2",
        title = "The background property",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/the-background-property",
        duration_text = "05m30s",
        duration_seconds = 330,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Transition property utilities, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "CSS Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-3 · **Prerequisites:** CSS transition utilities, Break before an element, Outline styles, Outline width, Background color, image, size, and position utilities, Tailwind theme variables, stroke  This chapter is the wider Tailwind vocabulary that surrounds `transition-*` and the transform families. It collects the property-list extensions, the accessibility-aware variants, the interactivity utilities, the motion-preference variants, and the few transform-adjacent families that did not fit in the CSS transition utilities chapter. Each section is short on prose and dense on tables.", mental_model = "A Tailwind UI is a contract with the user as much as it is a set of classes. Each interactive utility answers a question: *what animates*, *who gets it*, *when does it fire*, *does the user know it's moving*. The contracts layer on top of each other:  Read the class string once per layer. The first layer says what animates. The second layer says which user states it applies to. The third layer says how it degrades for users who opted out.", code_example = "@import \"tailwindcss\";\n\n@theme {\n  --card-properties: width, height, color, background-color, box-shadow;\n}", pitfalls = "// right: branch between complete strings. className={kind === \"color\" ? \"transition-colors\" : \"transition-transform\"} ```  The full class name is never a literal in a scanned file, so the scanner does not generate CSS for it. The style silently does nothing. Branch between complete strings or build a `Record<Kind, string>` of complete class strings.  A user who has asked the OS for less motion still sees the lift. The WCAG-recommended fix is to pair every animated utility with `motion-reduce:`:  Forgetting the `motion-reduce:` on a *single* utility breaks the user's accessibility setting without warning. Audit class strings for any `transition-*`, `duration-*`, `delay-*`, `animate-*`, `scale-*`, `rotate-*`, or `translate-*`; each is a candidate.  The `focus:outline-none` removes the browser's native focus ring without providing a replacement. A keyboard user has no way to tell where focus is. The right pattern is a focus-visible rule with a visible replacement:" }
      },
    ]
  },

        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-3 · **Prerequisites:** Transition property utilities, Tailwind theme variables, stroke, Z-axis translation, Width and height utilities, Break before an element, overscroll-behavior utilities, Custom color tokens, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Tailwind UI Ecommerce kit, Generated content from attributes  This is a reference-heavy Tailwind chapter. Its main subject is `perspective-origin`, with a compact reference roundup for related Tailwind v4 configuration and interaction utilities used in the exercises.", mental_model = "Imagine a small card floating in front of a camera. `perspective-*` chooses how close the camera is. `perspective-origin-*` moves the camera's vanishing point across the card. The card can use the same `rotate-y-12` transform in both cases, yet the tilt appears to lean toward a different corner.  The origin is a coordinate on the element being projected. It does not move the element by itself. A 3D transform must be present before changing the origin can produce a visible difference.", code_example = "export function OriginPreview() {\n  return (\n    <div className=\"grid grid-cols-2 gap-6\">\n      <div className=\"perspective-near\">\n        <div className=\"perspective-origin-top-left transform-3d rotate-y-12 rounded-lg bg-sky-500 p-6 text-white\">\n          top left\n        </div>\n      </div>\n      <div className=\"perspective-near\">\n        <div className=\"perspective-origin-bottom-right transform-3d rotate-y-12 rounded-lg bg-indigo-500 p-6 text-white\">\n          bottom right\n        </div>\n      </div>\n    </div>\n  );\n}", pitfalls = "This deliberately broken TypeScript does not compile:  function Origin({ value }: { value: string }) {   const className: string = origins[value as keyof typeof origins];   return <div className={className} />; } ```  A stricter and safer version accepts the union at the component boundary:  function Origin({ value }: { value: Origin }) {   return <div className={origins[value]} />; } ```  If you remove the cast in the broken version, TypeScript reports `Element implicitly has an 'any' type because expression of type 'string' can't be used to index type ...`. The cast can hide an invalid value, so a prop union is the better boundary." }
      },

  {
    id = "05-029",
    number = 29,
    title = "Shadows, opacity, blending, and isolation",
    local_chapter_file = "029-shadows-opacity-blending-and-isolation.md",
    key_concepts = ["Box shadow: elevation tokens and inset shadows", "Text shadow: `text-shadow-*` and shadow colour", "Opacity: `opacity-*` and how it differs from slash-opacity", "Filter family: blur, brightness, and composition", "Mix-blend-mode: how an element combines with its backdrop", "Isolation: `isolate`"],
    total_duration_seconds = 431,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzI5OlYxOjAyOS1z",
        raw_key = "S5:C29:V1",
        title = "Working with font-weight and font-style",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/working-with-font-weight-and-font-style",
        duration_text = "01m48s",
        duration_seconds = 108,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Shadows, opacity, blending, and isolation, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "CSS Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-3 · **Prerequisites:** Custom color tokens, perspective-origin, Outline styles, Break before an element, stroke  Effects are the surface layer of a UI. They make cards lift, text punch through a busy hero, and a frosted modal read like glass instead of cardboard. Tailwind groups all of these under one roof, calling them *effects*. Most are zero-runtime utility wrappers around a CSS property: `shadow-*` wraps `box-shadow`, `opacity-*` wraps `opacity`, `mix-blend-*` wraps `mix-blend-mode`, `mask-*` wraps the mask family, and `backdrop-*` wraps the backdrop-filter family.", mental_model = "Every modern UI pulls from this toolkit: a hero headline drops a text shadow, a card lifts on hover, a modal blurs its backdrop, a price tag multiplies into the card. Treating the effect utilities as a vocabulary (a `box-shadow` story, a `text-shadow` story, an `opacity` story, a `filter` story, a `mix-blend-mode` story, a `mask` story, a `prose` story) keeps every component pulled from the same palette. The classes live in the same `className` strings as the layout utilities from earlier chapters, so a fully styled component is one string per element.", code_example = "<button\n  type=\"button\"\n  className=\"rounded-md bg-white px-4 py-2 text-slate-900\n             shadow-md hover:shadow-lg\n             focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-sky-500\n             dark:bg-slate-800 dark:text-slate-100\"\n>\n  Save\n</button>", pitfalls = "Tailwind's scanner does not see the assembled string, so the utility is missing from the generated CSS. The element renders with no shadow at all, and the bug surfaces only in production builds where unused utilities are stripped. Map values to complete strings instead.  `opacity` is multiplied across every rendered pixel of the element and its children. The title and the body fade with the surface. If the goal is a translucent surface with crisp text, reach for `bg-sky-500/30` instead and leave `text-*` fully opaque.  Without an isolating ancestor, `mix-blend-mode` composites against everything behind the element in the stacking context, including the page background. The multiply can produce unexpected colours when the layout shifts. Wrap the card in `isolate` (or any `position: relative` ancestor without `z-index: auto`); inside Tailwind, prefer the explicit `isolate` utility.  `transition-all` covers everything, including unintended properties (a `border-radius` change, a `padding` change). `transition-shadow` is the explicit subset that matches `box-shadow`. Pair the geometry utility with the matching transition utility.  `backdrop-filter` operates on the pixels behind the element. If the background is opaque (`bg-white`), the backdrop filter has nothing to operate on and visually does nothing. Always pair backdrop filters with translucent backgrounds (`bg-white/30`, `bg-sky-500/40`)." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzI5OlYyOjAyOS1z",
        raw_key = "S5:C29:V2",

        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-3 · **Prerequisites:** Custom color tokens, perspective-origin, Outline styles, Break before an element, stroke  Effects are the surface layer of a UI.
