The multiply can produce unexpected colours when the layout shifts. Wrap the card in `isolate` (or any `position: relative` ancestor without `z-index: auto`); inside Tailwind, prefer the explicit `isolate` utility.  `transition-all` covers everything, including unintended properties (a `border-radius` change, a `padding` change). `transition-shadow` is the explicit subset that matches `box-shadow`.   {
    id = "05-030",
    number = 30,
    title = "Tailwind contrast filter",
    local_chapter_file = "030-tailwind-contrast-filter.md",
    key_concepts = ["`contrast-*`", "`grayscale`", "`hue-rotate-*`", "`invert`", "`saturate-*`", "`sepia`"],
    total_duration_seconds = 318,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzMwOlYxOjAzMC10",
        raw_key = "S5:C30:V1",
        title = "Challenge: Style an event page section",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/challenge-style-an-event-page-section",
        duration_text = "01m19s",
        duration_seconds = 79,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Tailwind contrast filter, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-2 · **Prerequisites:** Shadows, opacity, blending, and isolation  CSS `filter` accepts a chain of effects: `contrast(150%) grayscale(50%) hue-rotate(45deg)`. Tailwind splits the chain into single-purpose utilities that compose on the element. `backdrop-filter` is the same idea applied to whatever is behind the element, which is only visible when the element itself is translucent. `drop-shadow` is the filter-family version of `box-shadow`, and it follows the rendered silhouette instead of the box.", mental_model = "Filters show up the moment a UI stops being a stack of rectangles: a hero photo that needs more punch, a logo that needs to flip in dark mode, a frosted modal scrim, a coloured drop-shadow on an SVG icon. Tailwind's filter families are the canonical place to reach for them; CSS exposes the same primitives, but the utility form lets you keep all of styling in `className` so the design tokens and the component logic live in the same file.  - `filter-*` on the element, `backdrop-*` on what is behind. They compose independently. - `drop-shadow-*` follows the silhouette; `shadow-*` follows the box. For SVG, PNG-with-transparency, or text, `drop-shadow` is correct; for cards, modals, and other rectangles, `shadow` is correct.", code_example = "<img className=\"contrast-50 ...\" src=\"/photo.jpg\" alt=\"Low contrast\" />\n<img className=\"contrast-100 ...\" src=\"/photo.jpg\" alt=\"Identity\" />\n<img className=\"contrast-150 hover:contrast-200 ...\" src=\"/photo.jpg\" alt=\"Boosted\" />", pitfalls = "`drop-shadow-lg` on a rectangular div produces the same result as `shadow-lg`. The cost is higher: `drop-shadow` runs through the silhouette pass even though the silhouette is the same as the box. For rectangles, `shadow-lg` is the right tool. Reach for `drop-shadow` only when the silhouette is non-rectangular: SVG icons, PNG logos with transparency, text glyphs.  `bg-white` is opaque; the backdrop blur has nothing to blur. The element looks like a plain white card. Pair every `backdrop-*` utility with a `/<opacity>` colour modifier (`bg-white/30`, `bg-slate-900/40`).  These are two different effects. `saturate-150` recolours the image pixels. `backdrop-saturate-150` recolours the pixels behind the `bg-slate-900` element. They look similar in a screenshot, but the underlying filter declarations are different and the cost profile differs.  Hue rotation works on the entire hue wheel, including greys and near-greys. A logo that mixes brand orange with a near-grey support colour will have its orange shifted but its grey left roughly where it was. For \"swap the brand colour only\", reach for an explicit recolour via CSS variables or export a second variant. Hue rotation is a quick recolour, not a brand re-skin.  The hover state should use the named `hue-rotate-0` utility if one exists, or the bare `hue-rotate-0`. The arbitrary `[0deg]` form works but is the more verbose choice. The named utilities exist precisely for these \"turn it back off\" moments: `grayscale-0`, `invert-0`, `sepia-0`, `hue-rotate-0`, `contrast-100`, `saturate-100`, `brightness-100`." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzMwOlYyOjAzMC10",
        raw_key = "S5:C30:V2",
        title = "Solution: Style an event page section",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/solution-style-an-event-page-section",
        duration_text = "03m59s",
        duration_seconds = 239,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Tailwind contrast filter, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-2 · **Prerequisites:** Shadows, opacity, blending, and isolation  CSS `filter` accepts a chain of effects: `contrast(150%) grayscale(50%) hue-rotate(45deg)`.         notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2-3 · **Prerequisites:** Perspective origin, Tailwind theme variables, Custom color tokens, Tailwind UI Ecommerce kit, Tailwind CSS Just-In-Time engine, Static asset URL import  Tailwind generates one CSS file that contains every utility your content scan touched. Two utilities that target the same property (`bg-blue-500` and `bg-red-500`) end up as two rules with the same specificity. The browser applies whichever rule appears later in the source order; the order of class names on the element is irrelevant. The same applies when Tailwind sits next to legacy CSS that uses IDs or `!important`: Tailwind's specificity is class-only, so legacy rules win unless Tailwind is forced to be `!important` globally.", mental_model = "The cascade is the part of Tailwind most projects get wrong without knowing it. A designer asks \"why does this button not turn blue on hover?\", a developer reorders class names, nothing happens, the developer adds `!important` to the rule that should lose, the page becomes unmaintainable. None of those fixes address the actual cause: class-name order is not the cascade.  The features in this chapter (the `!` modifier, the global `important` mode, the `@apply` directive, the `--alpha()` function, the `@layer` ordering, the `peer-*` and `group-*` variants, the `type:` hint) are the tools that make the cascade do what you want without `!important` on every rule. The version notes are a paper trail: if you inherit a v3 project and need to know whether `flex-shrink` or `shrink` is the right class, the v4 changelog is the answer.", code_example = "<button className=\"bg-red-500 hover:bg-blue-500\">Save</button>", pitfalls = "The two are equivalent. The class attribute is not a stylesheet; the browser applies both rules, and the one that appears later in the *generated* CSS wins. Reordering class names to \"make the override win\" is a no-op.  The `!` makes the red `!important`. The hover fires, the blue wins because the blue rule is the only one matching the hover state, and the red rule (even though important) does not match the hover state. The button is blue on hover. The `!` is not a \"make this state win\", it is a \"make this declaration unkillable by non-important rules\".  Without `@layer components` (or any `@layer`), the rule sits in the author's layer, which is the lowest specificity in the cascade. A `<button className=\"bg-red-500\">` inside the rule would lose, even though utilities are supposed to override components. Wrap component classes in `@layer components` so they sit in the right slot.  This makes every utility important. Use it only when the entire stylesheet sits next to high-specificity legacy CSS that wins every tie. For a single override, the per-utility `!` modifier or a higher-specificity selector is the right tool.  `peer-checked:` reads from the *previous sibling* that has the `peer` class. Without `<input className=\"peer\" />`, there is no peer to read from, and the rule never fires." }
      },

    id = "05-032",
    number = 32,
    title = "Tailwind v4 default style changes",
    local_chapter_file = "032-tailwind-v4-default-style-changes.md",
    key_concepts = ["Hook: read the release notes before you upgrade", "Default style changes", "Individual transform properties", "Removed and renamed utilities", "Variant behavior", "`will-change` utilities"],
    total_duration_seconds = 867,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzMyOlYxOjAzMi10",
        raw_key = "S5:C32:V1",
        title = "Using responsive modifiers",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/tailwind-css-4-essential-training/using-responsive-modifiers",
        duration_text = "05m18s",
        duration_seconds = 318,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Tailwind v4 default style changes, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 3 · **Prerequisites:** Global important mode and utility conflicts, perspective-origin, CSS transition utilities, Outline width, Break before an element, Transition property utilities, Background color, image, size, and position utilities, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Currying event-handler factories, Tailwind theme variables, Custom color tokens, stroke  Tailwind v4 is a rewrite, not a rename. The utility names you already know mostly survived, so a `bg-rose-500` from a v3 codebase still works in v4. What changed is the ground under those names: the Preflight defaults, the CSS properties the transform utilities emit, the order stacked variants apply, and the syntax for arbitrary values. A migration is mostly an audit: walk every place you relied on a v3 default and decide whether the new default is what you want or whether you have to restate it.", mental_model = "Tailwind v4 keeps the utility names that made Tailwind popular and rebuilds the engine underneath. The className strings you write are mostly unchanged; the CSS those strings compile to has shifted in ways that affect borders, rings, placeholders, buttons, dialogs, transforms, and the variant order. A migration that does not audit these defaults ships subtle visual regressions: a border that paints in `currentColor` instead of gray-200, a focus ring that is 1px instead of 3px, a placeholder that is solid instead of 60% opacity, a button cursor that is `default` instead of `pointer`.  You will meet this in real codebases in three places. First, a v3 project you inherit gets the v4 upgrade and the audit checklist is your first task. Second, a v4 design system you build has to choose whether to accept the new defaults or restate the old ones; the `@theme` block is the right place to set the project-wide preferences. Third, a v4 dependency you consume (a UI kit, a design-system package) may rely on `@theme inline` or `theme(static)`, and you have to know the difference to debug \"why is my variable empty in production?\" bugs.", code_example = "/* v4 output */\n.border { border-color: currentColor; }\n.border-2 { border-color: currentColor; border-width: 2px; }", pitfalls = "In v4 it paints `currentColor`. A card with `className=\"border p-4\"` whose parent is dark-mode-on will have a white border on a dark background. If you wanted gray-200 (the v3 default), write `border-gray-200`.  v4 ignores `bg-opacity-50`. Replace with `bg-red-500/50`.  v4 renamed them. `shrink-0` and `grow-1` are the v4 spellings. A v3 codebase migrating to v4 needs every `flex-shrink-*` and `flex-grow-*` swapped.  `overflow-ellipsis` is removed; `text-ellipsis` is the v4 spelling. A global find-and-replace handles most migrations.  `rounded-sm` in v4 is a smaller radius than v3. If your design used v3 `rounded-sm`, you want `rounded-xs` in v4. The same shift applies to `shadow-sm` → `shadow-xs` and `blur-sm` → `blur-xs`." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzMyOlYyOjAzMi10",
        raw_key = "S5:C32:V2",
        title = "Styling Next.js applications with Tailwind CSS",

        duration_text = "09m09s",

        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Tailwind v4 default style changes, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",

        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 3 · **Prerequisites:** Global important mode and utility conflicts, perspective-origin, CSS transition utilities, Outline width, Break before an element, Transition property utilities, Background color, image, size, and position utilities, Tailwind CSS: Utility-First Styling, Currying event-handler factories, Tailwind theme variables, Custom color tokens, stroke  Tailwind v4 is a rewrite, not a rename. If you wanted gray-200 (the v3 default), write `border-gray-200`.  v4 ignores `bg-opacity-50`. Replace with `bg-red-500/50`.  v4 renamed them. `shrink-0` and `grow-1` are the v4 spellings.         duration_text = "02m55s",

        references = [

        ],
        notes = { definition = "A component library is like a box of tested building parts. You assemble buttons, alerts, navigation, and forms instead of cutting every piece from raw CSS. The tradeoff is that the parts arrive with an API and a visual opinion.", mental_model = "Component libraries shorten routine UI work and make interaction patterns consistent. Their props also become architectural boundaries: application state enters through typed props, events leave through callbacks, and theme values coordinate the screen. Knowing where the library stops prevents accessibility regressions, invalid prop values, and state hidden inside visual components.", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled", pitfalls = "Installing only `@mui/material` can produce:  Install `@emotion/react` and `@emotion/styled` with MUI's core package.  This is deliberately broken:  Use `variant=\"contained\"`, or augment MUI's variant types when your theme defines a real custom variant.  `<Checkbox indeterminate />` draws a mixed state, but the native input does not submit an `indeterminate` value. Store the domain state yourself and map it to `checked` and `indeterminate`." }
      },

It is the smallest interactive thing Material UI ships, and it borrows from three of the bigger components:  - `Avatar` for the leading ornament (`avatar` prop), - `Icon` for the leading or trailing glyph (`icon` prop, or the delete-icon from `onDelete`), - `ButtonBase` for the underlying click affordance (when `onClick` or `onDelete` is set, the chip is a button in the tab order).", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled\nbun add @mui/icons-material", pitfalls = "Direct DOM mutation or impure side effects during the render phase cause hydration mismatches, duplicate executions in Strict Mode, and stale closures.     id = "05-035",
    number = 35,
    title = "Material UI deep imports for bundle size",
    local_chapter_file = "035-material-ui-deep-imports-for-bundle-size.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model", "The deep-import rule", "Minimal example", "Enforce the rule with ESLint", "Predict the render", "Common mistakes"],
    total_duration_seconds = 240,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzM1OlYxOjAzNS1t",
        raw_key = "S5:C35:V1",
        title = "The rules of JSX",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/master-react-19-build-ecommerce-solutions-and-prepare-for-interviews-with-typescript-next-js-and-remix/the-rules-of-jsx",
        duration_text = "02m37s",
        duration_seconds = 157,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Material UI deep imports for bundle size, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Master React 19, Build Ecommerce Solutions, and Prepare for Interviews with TypeScript, Next.js, and Remix",
        references = [
          { label = "Material UI Official Guide: Getting Started", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/getting-started/", description = "Production-ready component library implementing Material Design." },
          { label = "Material UI: Customization & Theming", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/customization/theming/", description = "Palette, typography, and createTheme configuration." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1 · **Prerequisites:** Chip, Component Libraries: Material UI  This chapter covers two things. First, the deep-import rule that keeps Material UI's dev server fast (barrel imports do not change the production bundle, but they do slow startup and rebuild). Second, a reference roundup of nine neighbouring MUI primitives you will reach for next to `Chip` and `Button`: `Divider`, `Fab`, `Link`, `Paper`, `Progress` indicators, `RadioGroup`, `Skeleton`, the `createTheme` / `ThemeProvider` pair, and the responsive viewport meta tag. The two exercises at the end combine most of these into one UI slice and then re-type it under strict TypeScript.", mental_model = "Material UI's package layout is a directory of files with one default export per file, plus one barrel file (`index.js`) that re-exports them all. Your bundler can resolve either path:  In production, both paths ship the same tree-shaken result. In development, the barrel forces the dev server to parse every named export to learn which symbols exist before it can throw a `ReferenceError` when you mistype one. That parse takes a long time. The `@mui/icons-material` package is worse: it ships 2,100+ components, and the barrel parse takes long enough that you can watch it happen.", code_example = "import Button from '@mui/material/Button';\nimport DeleteIcon from '@mui/icons-material/Delete';\n\nexport default function ConfirmDelete() {\n  return (\n    <Button\n      variant=\"contained\"\n      color=\"error\"\n      startIcon={<DeleteIcon />}\n      onClick={() => console.log('confirmed')}\n    >\n      Delete account\n    </Button>\n  );\n}", pitfalls = "ESLint with the `no-restricted-imports` pattern from above reports:  Replace with `import Button from '@mui/material/Button';`. Add `@mui/icons-material/[IconName]` for icons.  VS Code by default adds barrel-import suggestions. Stop that for MUI with a project-scoped setting:  `@mui/icons-material` depends on `@mui/material`, which depends on Emotion. Installing the icon package without the core produces runtime errors when the first icon mounts:  The core trio + icons is the minimum. Add `@fontsource/roboto` if you want the default font." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzM1OlYyOjAzNS1t",
        raw_key = "S5:C35:V2",
        title = "IQ: The rules of JSX",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/master-react-19-build-ecommerce-solutions-and-prepare-for-interviews-with-typescript-next-js-and-remix/iq-the-rules-of-jsx",
        duration_text = "01m23s",
        duration_seconds = 83,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Material UI deep imports for bundle size, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Master React 19, Build Ecommerce Solutions, and Prepare for Interviews with TypeScript, Next.js, and Remix",
        references = [
          { label = "Material UI Official Guide: Getting Started", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/getting-started/", description = "Production-ready component library implementing Material Design." },
          { label = "Material UI: Customization & Theming", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/customization/theming/", description = "Palette, typography, and createTheme configuration." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1 · **Prerequisites:** Chip, Component Libraries: Material UI  This chapter covers two things. First, the deep-import rule that keeps Material UI's dev server fast (barrel imports do not change the production bundle, but they do slow startup and rebuild). Second, a reference roundup of nine neighbouring MUI primitives you will reach for next to `Chip` and `Button`: `Divider`, `Fab`, `Link`, `Paper`, `Progress` indicators, `RadioGroup`, `Skeleton`, the `createTheme` / `ThemeProvider` pair, and the responsive viewport meta tag. The two exercises at the end combine most of these into one UI slice and then re-type it under strict TypeScript.", mental_model = "Material UI's package layout is a directory of files with one default export per file, plus one barrel file (`index.js`) that re-exports them all. Your bundler can resolve either path:  In production, both paths ship the same tree-shaken result. In development, the barrel forces the dev server to parse every named export to learn which symbols exist before it can throw a `ReferenceError` when you mistype one. That parse takes a long time. The `@mui/icons-material` package is worse: it ships 2,100+ components, and the barrel parse takes long enough that you can watch it happen.", code_example = "import Button from '@mui/material/Button';\nimport DeleteIcon from '@mui/icons-material/Delete';\n\nexport default function ConfirmDelete() {\n  return (\n    <Button\n      variant=\"contained\"\n      color=\"error\"\n      startIcon={<DeleteIcon />}\n      onClick={() => console.log('confirmed')}\n    >\n      Delete account\n    </Button>\n  );\n}", pitfalls = "ESLint with the `no-restricted-imports` pattern from above reports:  Replace with `import Button from '@mui/material/Button';`. Add `@mui/icons-material/[IconName]` for icons.  VS Code by default adds barrel-import suggestions. Stop that for MUI with a project-scoped setting:  `@mui/icons-material` depends on `@mui/material`, which depends on Emotion. Installing the icon package without the core produces runtime errors when the first icon mounts:  The core trio + icons is the minimum. Add `@fontsource/roboto` if you want the default font." }
      },
    ]
  },

        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1 · **Prerequisites:** Chip, Component Libraries: Material UI  This chapter is a reference roundup. The slug's namesake, **MUI Supported Components**, frames Material UI's scope: the library ships the practical building blocks of Material Design but does not promise every component or every guideline feature. Around that, the page documents **Supported Platforms** (which browsers, runtimes, and bundlers are first-class), the **System props to `sx`** codemod for the v6/v9 migration, the **TextareaAutosize** utility that auto-grows a `<textarea>`, the **Timeline** lab component for chronological content, the **Versioning Strategy** (semantic versioning with deprecation windows), the **Changelog Policy** (`CHANGELOG.md` for the current major, `CHANGELOG.old.md` for archives), the **Long-Term Support** policy (security and regression fixes for the predecessor major), and the four core building blocks the rest of the section leans on: **Paper** (surface + elevation), **Card** (paper with composable parts), **Alert** (transient feedback with severity), **Progress** (LinearProgress and CircularProgress), and **Radio** + **RadioGroup** (roving-tabindex single-select).", mental_model = "Material UI's stance is explicit: it provides the practical building blocks developers need to build compelling user interfaces, omitting incidental or rarely-used components. The library does not aim to implement every Material Design component or every guideline feature. If a component exists in the Material Design specification but has no clear use in real apps, it does not ship in `@mui/material`. If you need it, you either build it yourself or reach for a community extension in `@mui/x` or a related project.  The practical effect of this scope:", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled\nbun add @mui/icons-material", pitfalls = "Direct DOM mutation or impure side effects during the render phase cause hydration mismatches, duplicate executions in Strict Mode, and stale closures. Always declare full dependency arrays in useEffect and never read state during render from outside the component tree." }

The headline topic is Material UI's color architecture (palettes, shades, and the `theme.spacing` scale), but the page also covers the supporting surfaces and patterns you reach for when you wire a theme into a real app: `CssBaseline` for normalization, `Paper` for elevation, `Skeleton` for loading placeholders, the `default-theme` viewer for inspection, the Figma design kit for designer-developer parity, locale text, the `theme.zIndex` scale, the `slots` / `slotProps` / `component` composition contract, the boolean-versus-enum prop convention, the tree-shaking rule, and the experimental API stability contract.", mental_model = "A Material UI color object is a colour dictionary in the same shape as Material Design's design tokens. Each colour has a `main` shade, a `light` and `dark` companion, and a `contrastText` shade that picks readable text on top of `main`. The palette tokens you address in components (`primary`, `secondary`, `error`, `warning`, `info`, `success`) are slots on a `palette` object that each reference a colour object.  You can either hand-build a color object (good for full control) or import a prebuilt hue from `@mui/material/colors` and pass its `main` shade into `createTheme` (good for a quick branded seed). Components read the `palette.primary.main` token through React context, so the same colour shows up everywhere without prop drilling.", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled\nbun add @mui/icons-material", pitfalls = "The locale is not a `ThemeProvider` prop. The compiler does not always catch this because both accept a string-shaped prop. Pass the locale to `createTheme`:  If a `Snackbar` or `Tooltip` mounts at the same time, your backdrop sits behind them. Read `theme.zIndex.drawer + 1` (or whichever neighbour you need to be above) and let the theme own the layering scale.  The import works. The next minor release can rename or remove it. Add a comment at the call site so the next maintainer knows the surface is unstable and can plan a migration.  `component` swaps the root element; the `variant` styles then target a `<div>` instead of a `<button>`. The result looks the same visually but loses the button semantics (no form submission, no native click activation). Use `component` for router links and similar semantic swaps, not for visual overrides.  In dark mode, `Paper elevation={0}` is visually identical to `elevation={1}` because the dark-mode gradient at elevation `0` is the same as the page background. The fix is to test in dark mode and pick the elevation that produces visible separation, or use `variant=\"outlined\"` for a deterministic border." }

Around that, the page documents the neighbouring MUI primitives and APIs that this chapter covers: **Accordion** (a collapsible region with `AccordionSummary` / `AccordionDetails`), **AppBar** (top-of-page chrome), **BottomNavigation** (the mobile top-level dest switcher), the **Breakpoints** API (`up` / `down` / `only` / `between`), **ClickAwayListener** (close-on-outside-click utility), **CSS Theme Variables** (token-based theming via custom properties), the **Customization Strategy Ladder** (sx -> styled -> theme overrides -> global CSS), the **Figma design kit** vocabulary, the **Grid v1 -> Grid v2** migration, the **Icon APIs** (packaged SVG, custom `SvgIcon`, fonts through `Icon`), **ImageList** (the modernised grid list), **MUI Lab** (the `@mui/lab` incubator package), **List** (the semantic list primitive), and **Masonry** (variable-height columns).", mental_model = "Keyboard navigation and focus is a contract between every interactive MUI primitive and the user agent: the component owns the keyboard behaviour, the ARIA roles, and the focus management. You write JSX with `value`, `onChange`, and content; MUI ships the arrow-key roving tabindex on `Tabs`, the focus trap on `Dialog`, and the type-ahead buffer on `Autocomplete`. The job you do as a developer is to make sure the props you pass carry an accessible name and the layout you build does not break the focus order.  Each primitive in the roundup below carries its own keyboard contract; the rest of the chapter is reference material grouped around that idea, the same way Material UI component catalog groups primitives by interface job.", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled\nbun add @mui/icons-material", pitfalls = "Direct DOM mutation or impure side effects during the render phase cause hydration mismatches, duplicate executions in Strict Mode, and stale closures. The slug's namesake is the **Material UI MCP documentation server**: a local process that hands your AI client the official Material UI docs and published component code over standard I/O, so answers cite real pages instead of guessing at APIs. Around that, the page documents the MUI primitives and theme APIs this chapter owns: the **native color** opt-in (`cssVariables.nativeColor`), **Pagination**, **Portal**, **Rating**, **Select**, the theme **shape** tokens, **Slider**, **Snackbar**, the **slots / slotProps** structure model and its migration API, **StyledEngineProvider**, **style library interoperability**, **Material UI Sync for Figma**, and the theme **palette**.", mental_model = "The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard that connects an AI client to trusted sources of documentation and code. You add it to a client with one command.     title = "Material UI theme provider",
    local_chapter_file = "043-material-ui-theme-provider.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model", "createTheme and ThemeProvider", "Theme scoping with THEME_ID", "theme.transitions.create and getAutoHeightDuration", "Themed components (default props, slot overrides, variants)", "Transfer List pattern"],
    total_duration_seconds = 838,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzQzOlYxOjA0My1t",
        raw_key = "S5:C43:V1",
        title = "Theme switcher and header component",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/build-modern-web-apps-with-react-hooks-state-management-and-apis-using-vite-or-next-js/theme-switcher-and-header-component",
        duration_text = "06m26s",
        duration_seconds = 386,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Material UI theme provider, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Build Modern Web Apps with React, Hooks, State Management, and APIs Using Vite or Next.js",
        references = [
          { label = "Material UI Official Guide: Getting Started", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/getting-started/", description = "Production-ready component library implementing Material Design." },
          { label = "Material UI: Customization & Theming", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/customization/theming/", description = "Palette, typography, and createTheme configuration." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2 · **Prerequisites:** Material UI component catalog, Material UI Color System, Component Libraries: Material UI, Chip, Material UI deep imports for bundle size, Material UI MCP documentation server  This chapter is a reference roundup. The headline topic is the `ThemeProvider` and the `createTheme` factory, but the page also covers the surfaces and patterns you reach for when you wire a theme into a real app: scoping MUI alongside another styling system with `THEME_ID`, the `theme.transitions.create` and `getAutoHeightDuration` motion helpers, the theme's `components` section for default-props and slot-level overrides, the `TransferList` shuttle composition pattern, the five transition primitives (`Collapse`, `Fade`, `Grow`, `Slide`, `Zoom`), the migration paths from v0 to v1 and v3 to v4, the v5 component-level and style-level breaking changes, the v7 `enableCssLayer` and standardized slots update, the `theme.zIndex` scale, the minimal-bug-reproduction rule for filing MUI issues, the bundle-size minimization story, and the `Modal` primitive that `Dialog`, `Drawer`, `Menu`, and `Popover` all build on.", mental_model = "The `ThemeProvider` is a React context provider whose value is a fully built `Theme` object. `createTheme(options)` walks the options, fills every unset token from a default, and returns a complete, deeply-typed `Theme`. The provider hands that theme down the tree, and every MUI component (and every `styled` / `sx` / `useTheme` call) reads it from context. Move the provider, change the theme; the visible app re-themes without a single prop-drilled colour.  When two styling systems coexist in the same tree (MUI plus Theme UI, MUI plus Chakra, MUI plus a custom design-token context), the providers must agree on which context key carries which theme. MUI exposes `THEME_ID` for exactly that negotiation: a string constant you put on a non-default context, so MUI's `styled`, `sx`, and `useTheme` look up the right value while other libraries keep using the default key.", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled", pitfalls = "`useTheme` from `@mui/material/styles` looks up the value under the `THEME_ID` constant, not under `myCustomKey`. The button will render with the default theme or throw depending on the MUI version. Use `[THEME_ID]: materialTheme`.  The property is `theme.transitions.duration.standard`. `theme.duration` is undefined. A rename of the namespace would be a breaking change, so the chain stays as is.  The button's icon slot is `startIcon` and `endIcon`, not `icon`. The wrong name compiles because the slot map is open-ended, but the override never applies. The fix is the slot name printed in the component's `slots` table.  A `Snackbar` at `zIndex.tooltip` or a `Dialog` at `zIndex.modal` can stack above the hand-picked number. Use `theme.zIndex.drawer + 1` (or whichever neighbour the backdrop needs to exceed) and let the theme own the scale.  Production tree-shaking still works, but the dev server parses the entire barrel before resolving the named exports. Deep imports keep dev fast. Enforce the rule with the `no-restricted-imports` ESLint pattern." }
      },

        id = "UzU6QzQzOlYyOjA0My1t",

    title = "Dialog",
    local_chapter_file = "044-dialog.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model", "Dialog", "Drawer and SwipeableDrawer", "Popover", "Modal scroll lock and fixed elements", "Accordion"],
    total_duration_seconds = 482,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzQ0OlYxOjA0NC1k",
        raw_key = "S5:C44:V1",
        title = "Apply custom scrollbar styles using a utility class",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/master-react-19-and-next-js-16-with-hands-on-projects-and-real-world-applications/apply-custom-scrollbar-styles-using-a-utility-class",
        duration_text = "04m40s",
        duration_seconds = 280,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Dialog, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Master React 19 and Next.js 16 with Hands-On Projects and Real-World Applications",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2 · **Prerequisites:** Material UI theme provider, Component Libraries: Material UI, Material UI component catalog  This chapter is a reference roundup. The headline topic is the `Dialog` family and the small constellation of surfaces that share MUI's `Modal` primitive, but the page also covers the panels that flank a screen (`Drawer`, `SwipeableDrawer`, `Popover`), the scroll lock behind every modal, the disclosure and message components that sit alongside a dialog (`Accordion`, `Alert`), the top-of-screen surface (`AppBar`, `Toolbar`), and the responsive helpers (`breakpoints`, `useMediaQuery`, `Grid`, theme `Typography`, MUI's API design rules) that switch a dialog between desktop and full-screen mobile.", mental_model = "A `Dialog` is a paper panel that floats above the page, traps focus, and blocks the rest of the screen until dismissed. Under the hood it is `Dialog` content composed inside `Modal`. The same `Modal` primitive powers `Drawer`, `Popover`, `Menu`, and the dropdown half of `Select`. Reach for one of those higher-level components whenever the surface matches its job; reach for `Modal` directly only when you need a custom positioner (a video lightbox, a centered tooltip).  The `Modal` primitive is the seam: backdrop, focus trap, scroll lock, portal. Every higher-level surface inherits those behaviours and adds one more concern (a paper panel for `Dialog`, an anchor position for `Drawer`/`Popover`, an item list for `Menu`).", code_example = "import { useState } from 'react';\nimport Button from '@mui/material/Button';\nimport Dialog from '@mui/material/Dialog';\nimport DialogTitle from '@mui/material/DialogTitle';\nimport DialogContent from '@mui/material/DialogContent';\nimport DialogContentText from '@mui/material/DialogContentText';\nimport DialogActions from '@mui/material/DialogActions';\nimport Slide from '@mui/material/Slide';\nimport { createTheme, ThemeProvider, type Theme } from '@mui/material/styles';\n\nconst theme: Theme = createTheme({\n  palette: { primary: { main: '#0052cc' } },\n  shape: { borderRadius: 8 },\n});\n\nexport function App(): JSX.Element {\n  const [open, setOpen] = useState(false);\n  return (\n    <ThemeProvider theme={theme}>\n      <Button variant=\"contained\" color=\"primary\" onClick={() => setOpen(true)}>\n        Delete item\n      </Button>\n      <Dialog\n        open={open}\n        onClose={() => setOpen(false)}\n        TransitionComponent={Slide}\n        keepMounted={false}\n        aria-labelledby=\"confirm-title\"\n      >\n        <DialogTitle id=\"confirm-title\">Delete this item?</DialogTitle>\n        <DialogContent>\n          <DialogContentText>\n            This action cannot be undone.\n          </DialogContentText>\n        </DialogContent>\n        <DialogActions>\n          <Button onClick={() => setOpen(false)}>Cancel</Button>\n          <Button variant=\"contained\" color=\"primary\" onClick={() => setOpen(false)}>\n            Delete\n          </Button>\n        </DialogActions>\n      </Dialog>\n    </ThemeProvider>\n  );\n}", pitfalls = "A screen reader announces a dialog with no name (failures of the same kind as a button without an accessible name). Pair the title's `id` with the dialog's `aria-labelledby`:  Mobile and desktop want different drawer behaviour. The wrong answer is to render `<Drawer variant=\"temporary\">` everywhere and toggle `open` everywhere; the user on desktop still has to click the menu icon to see the sidebar. The right answer is the responsive swap with `useMediaQuery`.  Without `open` and `onClose`, the popover never renders. Both props are required for a controlled popover. Same `open` / `onClose` pair as `Dialog` and `Drawer`.  When a `Dialog` opens, the body scrollbar disappears. The banner, which ignores the scrollbar, visibly shifts left. Add the `mui-fixed` class to the banner root and MUI compensates the offset automatically.  `Alert` does not steal focus, does not block the rest of the screen, and does not trap anything. For an interrupting destructive confirmation, use a `Dialog` with `role=\"alertdialog\"`. `Alert` is for non-interrupting feedback only." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzQ0OlYyOjA0NC1k",
        raw_key = "S5:C44:V2",

        duration_seconds = 202,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Dialog, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "CSS Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2 · **Prerequisites:** Material UI theme provider, Component Libraries: Material UI, Material UI component catalog  This chapter is a reference roundup. Every higher-level surface inherits those behaviours and adds one more concern (a paper panel for `Dialog`, an anchor position for `Drawer`/`Popover`, an item list for `Menu`).", code_example = "import { useState } from 'react';\nimport Button from '@mui/material/Button';\nimport Dialog from '@mui/material/Dialog';\nimport DialogTitle from '@mui/material/DialogTitle';\nimport DialogContent from '@mui/material/DialogContent';\nimport DialogContentText from '@mui/material/DialogContentText';\nimport DialogActions from '@mui/material/DialogActions';\nimport Slide from '@mui/material/Slide';\nimport { createTheme, ThemeProvider, type Theme } from '@mui/material/styles';\n\nconst theme: Theme = createTheme({\n  palette: { primary: { main: '#0052cc' } },\n  shape: { borderRadius: 8 },\n});\n\nexport function App(): JSX.Element {\n  const [open, setOpen] = useState(false);\n  return (\n    <ThemeProvider theme={theme}>\n      <Button variant=\"contained\" color=\"primary\" onClick={() => setOpen(true)}>\n        Delete item\n      </Button>\n      <Dialog\n        open={open}\n        onClose={() => setOpen(false)}\n        TransitionComponent={Slide}\n        keepMounted={false}\n        aria-labelledby=\"confirm-title\"\n      >\n        <DialogTitle id=\"confirm-title\">Delete this item?</DialogTitle>\n        <DialogContent>\n          <DialogContentText>\n            This action cannot be undone.\n          </DialogContentText>\n        </DialogContent>\n        <DialogActions>\n          <Button onClick={() => setOpen(false)}>Cancel</Button>\n          <Button variant=\"contained\" color=\"primary\" onClick={() => setOpen(false)}>\n            Delete\n          </Button>\n        </DialogActions>\n      </Dialog>\n    </ThemeProvider>\n  );\n}", pitfalls = "A screen reader announces a dialog with no name (failures of the same kind as a button without an accessible name).   {
    id = "05-045",
    number = 45,
    title = "MUI Bundle Size Optimization",
    local_chapter_file = "045-mui-bundle-size-optimization.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model", "Deep imports", "Enforce the rule with ESLint", "Stop VS Code from auto-importing the barrel", "Next.js `optimizePackageImports`", "Parcel and package exports"],
    total_duration_seconds = 474,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzQ1OlYxOjA0NS1t",
        raw_key = "S5:C45:V1",
        title = "Summary of Redux code and mock data from previous section",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/building-ui-components-with-storybook-master-react-components-and-ui-design-with-storybook-for-better-efficien/summary-of-redux-code-and-mock-data-from-previous-section",
        duration_text = "04m54s",
        duration_seconds = 294,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of MUI Bundle Size Optimization, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Building UI Components with Storybook: Master React Components and UI Design with Storybook for Better Efficiency",
        references = [
          { label = "Material UI Official Guide: Getting Started", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/getting-started/", description = "Production-ready component library implementing Material Design." },
          { label = "Material UI: Customization & Theming", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/customization/theming/", description = "Palette, typography, and createTheme configuration." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2 · **Prerequisites:** Chip, Material UI component catalog, Component Libraries: Material UI, Pagination Query Parameters, MUI Supported Components, JSX and Rendering, Dialog, Material UI deep imports for bundle size  This chapter is a reference roundup. The headline topic is the bundle-size story for Material UI: why `@mui/material` and `@mui/icons-material` barrel imports are slow on the developer machine, how to enforce deep imports with ESLint, and how to keep your IDE from re-introducing barrel imports through auto-complete. The page then summarises the neighbouring MUI primitives the rest of the roundup covers: `ButtonGroup`, `Card` (and its composition parts), `CircularProgress`, `ClickAwayListener`, `theme.containerQueries`, `CssBaseline` (and `ScopedCssBaseline`), MUI's deprecation lifecycle, dialog and snackbar feedback, the FAQ practices that come up most, `Fab`, and the SVG / `SvgIcon` / `createSvgIcon` icon path. The two exercises at the end combine most of these into one UI slice and then re-type the slice under strict TypeScript.", mental_model = "Material UI's package layout is a directory of files, one default export per file, plus one barrel file (`index.js`) that re-exports them all. Modern bundlers tree-shake the production bundle, so the size on a deployed CDN is the same either way. The cost shows up on the developer machine, where the bundler has to walk every named export of the barrel to learn which symbols exist before it can throw a `ReferenceError` when you mistype one. `@mui/icons-material` is worse than `@mui/material`: it ships 2,100+ icons in the barrel, and the parse takes long enough to watch.  Importing each component by its own path side-steps the barrel. The dev server sees the one file you need and skips the rest. ESLint stops the rule from being silently broken, the IDE auto-import exclusion stops VS Code from re-introducing the barrel, and the codemod migrates existing barrel imports in bulk.", code_example = "import Button from '@mui/material/Button';\nimport DeleteIcon from '@mui/icons-material/Delete';\n\nexport default function ConfirmDelete() {\n  return (\n    <Button\n      variant=\"contained\"\n      color=\"error\"\n      startIcon={<DeleteIcon />}\n      onClick={() => console.log('confirmed')}\n    >\n      Delete account\n    </Button>\n  );\n}", pitfalls = "ESLint with the `no-restricted-imports` pattern from above reports:  Replace with `import Button from '@mui/material/Button';`. Add `@mui/icons-material/[IconName]` for icons.  VS Code by default offers `Button` from `@mui/material` first. Add `typescript.preferences.autoImportSpecifierExcludeRegexes` from above, or accept the suggestion, then let ESLint flag it.  `@mui/icons-material` depends on `@mui/material`, which depends on Emotion. Installing the icon package without the core produces runtime errors when the first icon mounts:  The core trio plus icons is the minimum. Add `@fontsource/roboto` if you want the default font." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzQ1OlYyOjA0NS1t",

        duration_text = "03m00s",
        duration_seconds = 180,

        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2 · **Prerequisites:** Chip, Material UI component catalog, Component Libraries: Material UI, Pagination Query Parameters, MUI Supported Components, JSX and Rendering, Dialog, Material UI deep imports for bundle size  This chapter is a reference roundup. The cost shows up on the developer machine, where the bundler has to walk every named export of the barrel to learn which symbols exist before it can throw a `ReferenceError` when you mistype one. `@mui/icons-material` is worse than `@mui/material`: it ships 2,100+ icons in the barrel, and the parse takes long enough to watch.  Importing each component by its own path side-steps the barrel. The dev server sees the one file you need and skips the rest. ESLint stops the rule from being silently broken, the IDE auto-import exclusion stops VS Code from re-introducing the barrel, and the codemod migrates existing barrel imports in bulk.", code_example = "import Button from '@mui/material/Button';\nimport DeleteIcon from '@mui/icons-material/Delete';\n\nexport default function ConfirmDelete() {\n  return (\n    <Button\n      variant=\"contained\"\n      color=\"error\"\n      startIcon={<DeleteIcon />}\n      onClick={() => console.log('confirmed')}\n    >\n      Delete account\n    </Button>\n  );\n}", pitfalls = "ESLint with the `no-restricted-imports` pattern from above reports:  Replace with `import Button from '@mui/material/Button';`. Add `@mui/icons-material/[IconName]` for icons.  VS Code by default offers `Button` from `@mui/material` first.     id = "05-046",
    number = 46,
    title = "MUI Material Icons Package (and a roundup: ImageList, layout primitives, LinearProgress, List, Rating, SpeedDial, NumberField, localization, RTL, the `classes` and `slots` APIs, and the MCP server)",
    local_chapter_file = "046-mui-material-icons-package.md",
    key_concepts = ["The icon package", "`ImageList`", "Layout primitives: `Box`, `Stack`, `Grid`", "`LinearProgress`", "The `List` family", "`Rating`"],
    total_duration_seconds = 1024,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzQ2OlYxOjA0Ni1t",
        raw_key = "S5:C46:V1",
        title = "Building the skills and experience section layout",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/master-react-19-and-next-js-16-with-hands-on-projects-and-real-world-applications/building-the-skills-experience-section-layout",
        duration_text = "04m27s",
        duration_seconds = 267,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of MUI Material Icons Package (and a roundup: ImageList, layout primitives, LinearProgress, List, Rating, SpeedDial, NumberField, localization, RTL, the `classes` and `slots` APIs, and the MCP server), examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Master React 19 and Next.js 16 with Hands-On Projects and Real-World Applications",
        references = [
          { label = "Material UI Official Guide: Getting Started", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/getting-started/", description = "Production-ready component library implementing Material Design." },
          { label = "Material UI: Customization & Theming", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/customization/theming/", description = "Palette, typography, and createTheme configuration." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-2 · **Prerequisites:** Component Libraries: Material UI, Chip, MUI Avatar, MUI Supported Components, Material UI component catalog, MUI Bundle Size Optimization, Pagination Query Parameters  This is a reference roundup built around the icon package that most Material UI screens depend on. The namesake is `@mui/icons-material`, the package that ships the official Material Icons as React components. Around it sits the set of components you reach for in the same breath: the `ImageList` grid, the layout primitives (`Box`, `Stack`, `Grid`), the `LinearProgress` bar, the `List` family, the `Rating` and `SpeedDial` interactive surfaces, the Base-UI-composed `NumberField`, and the two customization APIs (`classes` and `slots` / `slotProps`) that every one of these components exposes. Localization, right-to-left layout, and the documentation MCP server close it out.", mental_model = "`@mui/icons-material` converts the 2,100+ official Material Icons into `SvgIcon`-based React components. Each icon is a named export, and each name follows a predictable scheme: the icon's Material name in PascalCase, with an optional theme suffix.  The five themes are `Filled` (the default, no suffix), `Outlined`, `Rounded`, `TwoTone`, and `Sharp`. An icon whose Material name starts with a digit is prefixed so it stays a valid identifier: the \"3D Rotation\" icon is `ThreeDRotation`.", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled\nbun add @mui/icons-material", pitfalls = "Direct DOM mutation or impure side effects during the render phase cause hydration mismatches, duplicate executions in Strict Mode, and stale closures. Always declare full dependency arrays in useEffect and never read state during render from outside the component tree." }
      },

  {
    id = "05-047",
    number = 47,
    title = "MUI Stepper (and a roundup: SvgIcon and `@mui/icons-material`, `sx` and style overrides, the `Table` family, `Tabs`, `TextField` and `NumberField`, `ToggleButton`, nested themes, and the polymorphic `component` prop)",
    local_chapter_file = "047-mui-stepper.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model", "`Stepper` and `Step` basics", "`MobileStepper`", "`SvgIcon` and `@mui/icons-material`", "Customizing `Stepper` with `sx` and theme overrides", "`Table` family for a wizard's review step"],
    total_duration_seconds = 420,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzQ3OlYxOjA0Ny1t",
        raw_key = "S5:C47:V1",
        title = "Build the settings component and define reInitialiseGame in the process",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/master-react-19-and-next-js-16-with-hands-on-projects-and-real-world-applications/build-the-settings-component-and-define-reinitialisegame-in-the-process",
        duration_text = "04m22s",
        duration_seconds = 262,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of MUI Stepper (and a roundup: SvgIcon and `@mui/icons-material`, `sx` and style overrides, the `Table` family, `Tabs`, `TextField` and `NumberField`, `ToggleButton`, nested themes, and the polymorphic `component` prop), examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Master React 19 and Next.js 16 with Hands-On Projects and Real-World Applications",
        references = [
          { label = "Material UI Official Guide: Getting Started", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/getting-started/", description = "Production-ready component library implementing Material Design." },
          { label = "Material UI: Customization & Theming", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/customization/theming/", description = "Palette, typography, and createTheme configuration." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-2 · **Prerequisites:** Component Libraries: Material UI, Pagination Query Parameters, Material UI component catalog  This chapter is a reference roundup built around the `Stepper` family, the wizard-like workflow surface that comes up in every multi-step form (checkout, onboarding, settings with validation steps, KYC, signup). Around the `Stepper` sits the constellation of MUI parts a stepper naturally composes with: the `SvgIcon` wrapper and the `@mui/icons-material` package that put a check mark on completed steps, the `sx` prop and the wider customization strategy that retunes a step's look, the `Table` family for the \"summary\" final step in many wizards, `Tabs` for the variant where the steps are peers rather than a sequence, the `TextField` and `NumberField` inputs that fill each step's body, `ToggleButton` for binary choices in a step, nested `ThemeProvider`s to give the wizard its own palette, and the polymorphic `component` prop that swaps the step root for a router link when the wizard drives a URL.", mental_model = "A `Stepper` is a numbered, sequenced indicator. You feed it the steps (an array of labels) and tell it which one is active; it paints the icons, the connectors, and the labels in the correct state. The pattern is \"active / completed / disabled / error\" on every step, with a connector rule between each pair.  The `Stepper` only paints the indicator. The body of each step lives outside the indicator; you keep a `currentStep` index in your component and render a different `<Box>` (or `StepContent`) based on that index. The component gives you the chrome, you own the body and the navigation.", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled\nbun add @mui/icons-material", pitfalls = "Direct DOM mutation or impure side effects during the render phase cause hydration mismatches, duplicate executions in Strict Mode, and stale closures. Always declare full dependency arrays in useEffect and never read state during render from outside the component tree." }

        id = "UzU6QzQ3OlYyOjA0Ny1t",
        raw_key = "S5:C47:V2",

        duration_text = "02m38s",

        description = "In-depth walkthrough of MUI Stepper (and a roundup: SvgIcon and `@mui/icons-material`, `sx` and style overrides, the `Table` family, `Tabs`, `TextField` and `NumberField`, `ToggleButton`, nested themes, and the polymorphic `component` prop), examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Master React 19, Build Ecommerce Solutions, and Prepare for Interviews with TypeScript, Next.js, and Remix",
        references = [
          { label = "Material UI Official Guide: Getting Started", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/getting-started/", description = "Production-ready component library implementing Material Design." },
          { label = "Material UI: Customization & Theming", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/customization/theming/", description = "Palette, typography, and createTheme configuration." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-2 · **Prerequisites:** Component Libraries: Material UI, Pagination Query Parameters, Material UI component catalog  This chapter is a reference roundup built around the `Stepper` family, the wizard-like workflow surface that comes up in every multi-step form (checkout, onboarding, settings with validation steps, KYC, signup). Around the `Stepper` sits the constellation of MUI parts a stepper naturally composes with: the `SvgIcon` wrapper and the `@mui/icons-material` package that put a check mark on completed steps, the `sx` prop and the wider customization strategy that retunes a step's look, the `Table` family for the \"summary\" final step in many wizards, `Tabs` for the variant where the steps are peers rather than a sequence, the `TextField` and `NumberField` inputs that fill each step's body, `ToggleButton` for binary choices in a step, nested `ThemeProvider`s to give the wizard its own palette, and the polymorphic `component` prop that swaps the step root for a router link when the wizard drives a URL.", mental_model = "A `Stepper` is a numbered, sequenced indicator. You feed it the steps (an array of labels) and tell it which one is active; it paints the icons, the connectors, and the labels in the correct state. The pattern is \"active / completed / disabled / error\" on every step, with a connector rule between each pair.  The `Stepper` only paints the indicator. The body of each step lives outside the indicator; you keep a `currentStep` index in your component and render a different `<Box>` (or `StepContent`) based on that index. The component gives you the chrome, you own the body and the navigation.", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled\nbun add @mui/icons-material", pitfalls = "Direct DOM mutation or impure side effects during the render phase cause hydration mismatches, duplicate executions in Strict Mode, and stale closures. Always declare full dependency arrays in useEffect and never read state during render from outside the component tree." }
      },

    id = "05-048",

    title = "Popper (and friends: Menu, Autocomplete, Tabs, Box, Stack, Paper, Icons, RTL, prop forwarding, styled engine)",
    local_chapter_file = "048-popper.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model", "`Popper`", "Common mistakes", "Why this matters", "Reference: composite positioning components", "Reference: layout primitives"],
    total_duration_seconds = 752,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzQ4OlYxOjA0OC1w",
        raw_key = "S5:C48:V1",
        title = "Sizing with flex properties",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/css-essential-training-26985268/sizing-with-flex-properties",
        duration_text = "02m27s",
        duration_seconds = 147,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Popper (and friends: Menu, Autocomplete, Tabs, Box, Stack, Paper, Icons, RTL, prop forwarding, styled engine), examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "CSS Essential Training",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-2 · **Prerequisites:** Component Libraries: Material UI, Material UI component catalog, Chip  This chapter opens the positioning layer of Material UI. `Popper` is the low-level primitive that places any floating content next to an anchor, and three of the most-used composite components (`Tooltip`, `Menu`, `Autocomplete`) are built on top of it. The reference sections after `Popper` cover the rest of the components in the brief: `Box`, `Stack`, `Paper`, `Tabs`, the `@mui/icons-material` package, right-to-left support, root and nested prop forwarding, and the Emotion vs `@mui/styled-engine-sc` choice. Each section is what the component is for, the props you reach for, and one minimal JSX. The two exercises at the end build a real `Autocomplete` and a Tabs-driven settings panel.", mental_model = "A `Popper` is a positioning engine, not a UI element. You give it an `anchorEl` (the thing you want to float content next to) and a child, and the child lands where the browser viewport and Popper's placement logic say it should land. The component itself draws no border, no background, and no shadow. It is the anchor + the child + the math that connects them.  1. `Popper` renders into a `Portal` at `document.body` by default. The floating content escapes any `overflow: hidden` or `transform: 3d` ancestor on the trigger. This is why tooltips survive inside scrolling drawers. 2. `Popper` does not block scrolling, does not close on click-away, and does not manage focus. Those are jobs for `Popover` and `Dialog`, which wrap `Popper` and add the modal behaviour.", code_example = "import { useRef, useState } from \"react\";\nimport Popper from \"@mui/material/Popper\";\nimport Box from \"@mui/material/Box\";\n\nexport default function HoverHint() {\n  const [anchor, setAnchor] = useState<HTMLElement | null>(null);\n  return (\n    <>\n      <button\n        ref={useRef(null)}\n        onMouseEnter={(event) => {\n          setAnchor(event.currentTarget);\n        }}\n        onMouseLeave={() => {\n          setAnchor(null);\n        }}\n      >\n        Hover me\n      </button>\n      <Popper open={Boolean(anchor)} anchorEl={anchor} placement=\"top\">\n        <Box sx={{ p: 1, bgcolor: \"background.paper\", boxShadow: 3 }}>\n          Floating hint\n        </Box>\n      </Popper>\n    </>\n  );\n}", pitfalls = "`Popover` is a `Popper` plus click-away handling, scroll lock, focus management, and a `Paper` surface. If you want modal behaviour (the user must dismiss the floating content), reach for `Popover` first and only fall back to `Popper` when you need raw positioning without the modal layer.  `Popper` opens when `open` is true and `anchorEl` is non-null. If you call `setAnchor(null)` to close, the popper unmounts. If you keep `anchorEl` set and just flip `open` to `false`, the popper unmounts as well, but the next render starts with no target to measure against, which produces a one-frame flash if you re-mount quickly. Clear both on close.  Click-away handling is not built in. You need a `ClickAwayListener` from `@mui/material/ClickAwayListener` around the trigger (not the popper) and a manual close handler. That is exactly what `Popover`, `Menu`, and `Select` already do for you.  `anchorEl` is the resolved DOM element or a virtual element (`{ getBoundingClientRect: () => DOMRect }`). A React `ref` object is neither. Read `ref.current` first or use `event.currentTarget` from the click handler." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzQ4OlYyOjA0OC1w",

        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-2 · **Prerequisites:** Component Libraries: Material UI, Material UI component catalog, Chip  This chapter opens the positioning layer of Material UI. `Popper` is the low-level primitive that places any floating content next to an anchor, and three of the most-used composite components (`Tooltip`, `Menu`, `Autocomplete`) are built on top of it.         notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-2 · **Prerequisites:** Component Libraries: Material UI, Material UI component catalog, Material UI Color System, Dialog, Popper  This chapter is a reference roundup built around the `Timeline` component, the chronological list surface that comes up in changelogs, order histories, audit logs, and activity feeds. Around the `Timeline` sits the larger MUI layout and theming toolkit a `Timeline` composes with: the `theme.breakpoints` API (the five screen-width keys and the `up` / `down` / `only` / `between` helpers) that lets the layout collapse from alternating to single-column on small screens, `Container` for horizontally centered page content, `Grid` (MUI 6 layout grid) for the responsive two-column variant of the `Timeline`, the `theme.components` overrides system that tunes every `TimelineDot` in the app at once, density props that tighten the `Timeline` on dense surfaces, `defaultProps` merging for class-name and style defaults, routing integration for the optional click-through timeline, palette tokens for the dot colour, `theme.spacing()` for the row gap, and `Stack` for the inner content column.", mental_model = "A `Timeline` is a chronological list. Each entry is a `TimelineItem` whose visual structure is a `TimelineSeparator` (the line and the dot) sitting next to a `TimelineContent` (the title and body). The `TimelineContent` is yours to fill: a typography node, a card, a button, a `Stack` of chips. The component is open enough to render anything inside the content slot but opinionated enough to keep the visual rhythm consistent.", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled", pitfalls = "Direct DOM mutation or impure side effects during the render phase cause hydration mismatches, duplicate executions in Strict Mode, and stale closures.   {
    id = "05-050",
    number = 50,
    title = "Material UI Grid v2 migration (and a roundup: `theme.typography` and the `Typography`/`Link` components, `theme.transitions`, reduced motion, `ImageList`, `Tooltip`, the `ClassNameGenerator` build-time hook)",
    local_chapter_file = "050-material-ui-grid-v2-migration.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model", "Grid v2 migration", "`theme.typography` and the `Typography` component", "`Link`", "`theme.transitions` and reduced motion", "`ImageList`"],
    total_duration_seconds = 798,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzUwOlYxOjA1MC1t",
        raw_key = "S5:C50:V1",
        title = "Thesis: Storybook saves valuable time and resources for projects",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/building-ui-components-with-storybook-master-react-components-and-ui-design-with-storybook-for-better-efficien/thesis-storybook-saves-valuable-time-and-resources-for-projects",
        duration_text = "03m15s",
        duration_seconds = 195,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Material UI Grid v2 migration (and a roundup: `theme.typography` and the `Typography`/`Link` components, `theme.transitions`, reduced motion, `ImageList`, `Tooltip`, the `ClassNameGenerator` build-time hook), examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Building UI Components with Storybook: Master React Components and UI Design with Storybook for Better Efficiency",
        references = [
          { label = "Material UI Official Guide: Getting Started", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/getting-started/", description = "Production-ready component library implementing Material Design." },
          { label = "Material UI: Customization & Theming", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/customization/theming/", description = "Palette, typography, and createTheme configuration." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-3 · **Prerequisites:** Component Libraries: Material UI, Chip, Material UI component catalog, MUI Timeline component, Popper  This chapter opens with the Grid v2 migration, which renames the legacy grid (`@mui/material/Grid` in v6, `@mui/material/GridLegacy` in v7) and lands the new layout grid at `@mui/material/Grid` (v7) or `@mui/material/Grid2` (v6), with collapsed `size={{ xs, sm, md, ... }}` props and a removed `item` prop. Around the migration sits the rest of the MUI vocabulary the new grid composes with: `theme.typography` and the `Typography` and `Link` components that style every text node, the `Typography` `variantMapping` that decouples visual variant from semantic HTML, `responsiveFontSizes()` for media-query-driven type scales, the `theme.transitions.create()` and `getAutoHeightDuration()` helpers plus `theme.motion.reducedMotion` that keep motion consistent across the UI, `ImageList` (and its quilted / woven / masonry variants) for repeated image grids, `Tooltip` for accessible labels on hover / focus / long-press, and the `unstable_ClassNameGenerator` build-time hook that rewrites every MUI component class prefix across the app before any `@mui/*` import is evaluated.", mental_model = "The new layout grid (Grid v2, also called the modern Grid) is the same flexbox idea as before: a `container` with `size`-driven children that break onto multiple lines at the theme breakpoints. What changed is the API surface. Per-breakpoint props collapsed into a single `size` object, the `item` flag disappeared, the `offset` feature replaced `zeroMinWidth`, and the grid now uses CSS variables instead of nested negative margins (so it no longer overflows the container). The migration is a one-time rewrite of an import path and a few prop names, with a codemod that does most of the rename for you.  subgraph v6 [MUI v6]     V6Import[\"import Grid from '@mui/material/Grid2'\"]     V6Props[\"size={{ xs: 12, sm: 6 }}\"]     V6Import --> V6Grid[\"Modern Grid (Grid2 import)\"]     V6Props --> V6Grid   end", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled\nbun add @mui/icons-material", pitfalls = "The codemod is keyed on the import path. Run it after the rename or it skips the file.  Either un-wrap the inner `<Grid>` (rename one of them), or do the prop rename by hand. The codemod's source-matcher only catches direct `<Grid>` JSX.  Missing weights fall back to browser synthetic bold / light, which never matches real Roboto. The MUI docs flag the four weights explicitly.  `hyphens-auto` is language-aware and depends on a `lang` attribute somewhere up the tree (the standard place is on `<html>` or the section root). A `<Typography lang=\"en\">` works for that node, but if you put `lang` only on the `Typography` and not on the surrounding `ImageList` or `Box`, the rest of the tree still uses the document language.  The Tailwind-style \"inverse variant\" trap carries over to the MUI transition components. `disablePrefersReducedMotion` is on every transition component; you set it on the single component you want, and `theme.motion.reducedMotion` handles the rest. Building a custom CSS rule that starts unconditional then patches the inverse is fragile; build the rule with `motion.reducedMotion === 'system'` and the `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)` wrapper from the start." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzUwOlYyOjA1MC1t",
        raw_key = "S5:C50:V2",
        title = "What are actions in Storybook and the synthetic base event object?",

        duration_text = "10m03s",
        duration_seconds = 603,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Material UI Grid v2 migration (and a roundup: `theme.typography` and the `Typography`/`Link` components, `theme.transitions`, reduced motion, `ImageList`, `Tooltip`, the `ClassNameGenerator` build-time hook), examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Building UI Components with Storybook: Master React Components and UI Design with Storybook for Better Efficiency",
        references = [
          { label = "Material UI Official Guide: Getting Started", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/getting-started/", description = "Production-ready component library implementing Material Design." },
          { label = "Material UI: Customization & Theming", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/customization/theming/", description = "Palette, typography, and createTheme configuration." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-3 · **Prerequisites:** Component Libraries: Material UI, Chip, Material UI component catalog, MUI Timeline component, Popper  This chapter opens with the Grid v2 migration, which renames the legacy grid (`@mui/material/Grid` in v6, `@mui/material/GridLegacy` in v7) and lands the new layout grid at `@mui/material/Grid` (v7) or `@mui/material/Grid2` (v6), with collapsed `size={{ xs, sm, md, ... }}` props and a removed `item` prop. Around the migration sits the rest of the MUI vocabulary the new grid composes with: `theme.typography` and the `Typography` and `Link` components that style every text node, the `Typography` `variantMapping` that decouples visual variant from semantic HTML, `responsiveFontSizes()` for media-query-driven type scales, the `theme.transitions.create()` and `getAutoHeightDuration()` helpers plus `theme.motion.reducedMotion` that keep motion consistent across the UI, `ImageList` (and its quilted / woven / masonry variants) for repeated image grids, `Tooltip` for accessible labels on hover / focus / long-press, and the `unstable_ClassNameGenerator` build-time hook that rewrites every MUI component class prefix across the app before any `@mui/*` import is evaluated.", mental_model = "The new layout grid (Grid v2, also called the modern Grid) is the same flexbox idea as before: a `container` with `size`-driven children that break onto multiple lines at the theme breakpoints. What changed is the API surface. Per-breakpoint props collapsed into a single `size` object, the `item` flag disappeared, the `offset` feature replaced `zeroMinWidth`, and the grid now uses CSS variables instead of nested negative margins (so it no longer overflows the container). The migration is a one-time rewrite of an import path and a few prop names, with a codemod that does most of the rename for you.  subgraph v6 [MUI v6]     V6Import[\"import Grid from '@mui/material/Grid2'\"]     V6Props[\"size={{ xs: 12, sm: 6 }}\"]     V6Import --> V6Grid[\"Modern Grid (Grid2 import)\"]     V6Props --> V6Grid   end", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled\nbun add @mui/icons-material", pitfalls = "The codemod is keyed on the import path. Run it after the rename or it skips the file.  Either un-wrap the inner `<Grid>` (rename one of them), or do the prop rename by hand. The codemod's source-matcher only catches direct `<Grid>` JSX.  Missing weights fall back to browser synthetic bold / light, which never matches real Roboto. The MUI docs flag the four weights explicitly.  `hyphens-auto` is language-aware and depends on a `lang` attribute somewhere up the tree (the standard place is on `<html>` or the section root). A `<Typography lang=\"en\">` works for that node, but if you put `lang` only on the `Typography` and not on the surrounding `ImageList` or `Box`, the rest of the tree still uses the document language.  The Tailwind-style \"inverse variant\" trap carries over to the MUI transition components. `disablePrefersReducedMotion` is on every transition component; you set it on the single component you want, and `theme.motion.reducedMotion` handles the rest. Building a custom CSS rule that starts unconditional then patches the inverse is fragile; build the rule with `motion.reducedMotion === 'system'` and the `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)` wrapper from the start." }
      },
    ]
  },

    videos = [

        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Composing dependent theme options (and a roundup: container queries, themable components, forced-colors, `Autocomplete`, CSS cascade layers, pickers migration, RTL, Tailwind v4 integration, v4-to-v9 upgrade path), examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Tailwind CSS 4 Essential Training",
        references = [
