        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 3 · **Prerequisites:** Material UI component catalog, MUI Timeline component, Chip, Dialog, Material UI MCP documentation server, Tailwind theme variables  This chapter opens with the `createTheme(options)` rule that catches people the first time they try to derive one theme option from another: build the base theme first, then derive the dependent values from it. Around that rule sits the rest of the MUI architecture vocabulary a real application leans on: `theme.containerQueries` (and the `@<size>` `sx` shorthand) for sizing against the parent container rather than the viewport, the `styled` / `ownerState` / `useThemeProps` pattern for building components that participate in the theme, `enhanceHighContrast` for Windows High Contrast Mode support, `Autocomplete` for searchable selects with separately tracked input text and selected value, CSS cascade layers (`enableCssLayer: true` plus a named `mui` layer or modular sublayers) for ordering framework and application styles, the `@material-ui/pickers` to `@mui/x-date-pickers` migration (with `Desktop*` / `Mobile*` / `Static*` exports and `LocalizationProvider`), right-to-left support (`dir` attribute, `theme.direction: 'rtl'`, RTL Stylis plugin), the Tailwind v4 CSS layers integration (`@layer mui` plus `@layer utilities` plus `@theme inline`), and the v4 → v5 → v6 → v7 → v9 upgrade ladder (codemods, Emotion peer deps, Grid rename, ESM-only, slots/slotProps, system-props codemod).", mental_model = "`createTheme(options)` is a normalising pass: it merges your `options` over the default theme, computes derived values (palette light/dark variants, typography rem sizes, breakpoint helpers), and returns the resolved theme. A dependent option is one whose value must read from another resolved option. The dependency has to be resolved at construction time, not at module load time, so the practical shape is \"build the base theme in one call, derive the dependent options from it in a second call\".  The same shape applies when you receive a theme as a prop (for instance from a `ThemeProvider` parent) and want to extend it: read the prop, derive the dependent values, return a new theme object. Forward-compatibility matters: an independently supplied options object should be merged into the *first* argument of the next `createTheme`, so the call is `(input, ...rest) => createTheme(deepmerge(base, input), ...rest)` rather than two separate calls.", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled\nbun add @mui/icons-material\nbun add @mui/lab\nbun add @mui/x-date-pickers", pitfalls = "`createTheme` is the normalising pass. Reading from raw options gives you the literal you wrote in, not the resolved value. The base theme is what you should read from.  The first argument is the resolved theme to extend; the second is the new options. Independent options objects should be merged into the first argument rather than chained through separate calls.  <Grid container>   <Grid2 size={6}>A</Grid2> </Grid> ```  <Grid container>   <Grid size={6}>A</Grid> </Grid> ```  Two grid systems in one tree produce inconsistent gutters and breakpoint behaviour. Pick one (matching the codemod run) and stick with it." }
      },

    local_chapter_file = "052-material-ui-with-tailwind-css-v4-css-layers.md",

        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/build-modern-web-apps-with-react-hooks-state-management-and-apis-using-vite-or-next-js/css-modules-local-scope-and-benefits-for-larger-projects",
        duration_text = "05m21s",
        duration_seconds = 321,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Material UI with Tailwind CSS v4 (CSS layers) (and a roundup: `Menubar` from Base UI, `NumberField` from Base UI, JSS to Emotion migration, MUI API design, `Autocomplete`, v9 upgrade highlights, `ClassNameGenerator`, MUI composition, composition with `component` and `slots`, themed custom components, MUI CSS cascade layers, MUI CSS theme variables, MUI `Drawer` variants, MUI extensible themes), examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",

          { label = "Material UI: Customization & Theming", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/customization/theming/", description = "Palette, typography, and createTheme configuration." },

        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2-3 · **Prerequisites:** Component Libraries: Material UI, Chip, Material UI component catalog, Material UI theme provider, MUI Timeline component  This chapter opens with the integration that lets Material UI and Tailwind v4 share the same DOM without `!important` wars: CSS cascade layers. MUI ships `experimental_extendTheme({ cssLayers: { ... } })` (and the corresponding `enableCssLayer` on the theme) to wrap every generated Emotion stylesheet in `@layer mui.global`, `@layer mui.components`, `@layer mui.theme`, `@layer mui.custom`, and `@layer mui.sx`; Tailwind v4 reads those layers and orders its own utilities last, so a `className=\"bg-red-500\"` always wins. Around the integration sits the rest of the MUI vocabulary the layered theme composes with: the documented `Menubar` recipe that composes Base UI's primitives with MUI components, the `NumberField` recipe that wires Base UI's `NumberField` to MUI form controls, the JSS to Emotion migration path for v4 to v5 codebases, the MUI API design conventions (low-level composition, consistent prop naming, root prop spreading, slot customisation, controlled state, root ref forwarding), the `Autocomplete` combobox (props like `options`, `value`/`onChange`, `inputValue`/`onInputChange`, `getOptionLabel`, `isOptionEqualToValue`, `multiple`, `freeSolo`, `groupBy`, `renderGroup`, `renderInput`, `renderOption`, `filterOptions`, `createFilterOptions`, plus the headless `useAutocomplete`), the v9 upgrade highlights (Enter/Space clicks now bubble as `MouseEvent`, the `nativeButton` prop for `<div>`-based custom buttons, the `AutocompleteValueOrFreeSoloValueMapping` typing for `freeSolo`, `Backdrop` no longer adding `aria-hidden` by default), the `unstable_ClassNameGenerator` build-time hook for rewriting the MUI class-name prefix, the composition guide (forwarded props, `muiName` preservation, merged slot props, polymorphic roots, forwarded refs), the composition patterns that use the `component` and `slots`/`slotProps` props plus `mergeSlotProps`, the themed custom components recipe (`styled(..., { name: 'MuiXxx', slot: 'root' })` so the theme can override the component via `components.MuiXxx.styleOverrides` and expose defaults via `defaultProps`/`useThemeProps`), the `cssVariables` mode that emits theme tokens as CSS custom properties (`--mui-palette-primary-main`, `theme.vars`, `colorSchemeSelector`, `useColorScheme`, `InitColorSchemeScript`), the `Drawer` variants (`temporary`, `persistent`, `mini`, `permanent`, `swipeable`), and the extensible-theme pattern that splits tokens and component defaults across separate exports so consuming apps can extend with object spread and preserve component variants with array syntax.", mental_model = "CSS cascade layers (`@layer a, b, c;`) define a strict order of precedence across the whole stylesheet. A rule inside `@layer a` always loses to a rule in `@layer b`, regardless of selector specificity. Tailwind v4 emits every utility class inside `@layer utilities;` so that any user-land `@layer theme` or `@layer components` overrides are guaranteed to lose to a Tailwind utility. The MUI integration simply wraps every Emotion-generated stylesheet inside `@layer mui.*` so it sits inside the cascade order too. With both sides agreeing on the layer order, you never need `!important`.  L1[\"@layer theme, base\"]:::base   L2[\"@layer mui.global\"]:::mui   L3[\"@layer mui.components\"]:::mui   L4[\"@layer mui.theme\"]:::mui   L5[\"@layer mui.custom\"]:::mui   L6[\"@layer mui.sx\"]:::mui   L7[\"@layer components\"]:::base   L8[\"@layer utilities\"]:::tw", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled", pitfalls = "// Right: inject the initial color scheme into the document before React hydrates <html>   <head>     <InitColorSchemeScript attribute=\"class\" />   </head>   <body>     <ThemeProvider theme={theme}>{children}</ThemeProvider>   </body> </html> ```  /* Right: declare the layer order at the top of the entry stylesheet */ @layer theme, base, mui, components, utilities;  @import \"tailwindcss\"; ```  // Right: also declare the order in CSS // @layer theme, base, mui, components, utilities; ```  // Right: pick one; component for simple element swaps, slots for slot-level swaps <Button component={RouterLink} to=\"/save\">Save</Button> // or <Button slots={{ root: MyRoot }}>Save</Button> ```" }
      },

The MUI integration simply wraps every Emotion-generated stylesheet inside `@layer mui.*` so it sits inside the cascade order too. With both sides agreeing on the layer order, you never need `!important`.  L1[\"@layer theme, base\"]:::base   L2[\"@layer mui.global\"]:::mui   L3[\"@layer mui.components\"]:::mui   L4[\"@layer mui.theme\"]:::mui   L5[\"@layer mui.custom\"]:::mui   L6[\"@layer mui.sx\"]:::mui   L7[\"@layer components\"]:::base   L8[\"@layer utilities\"]:::tw", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled", pitfalls = "// Right: inject the initial color scheme into the document before React hydrates <html>   <head>     <InitColorSchemeScript attribute=\"class\" />   </head>   <body>     <ThemeProvider theme={theme}>{children}</ThemeProvider>   </body> </html> ```  /* Right: declare the layer order at the top of the entry stylesheet */ @layer theme, base, mui, components, utilities;  @import \"tailwindcss\"; ```  // Right: also declare the order in CSS // @layer theme, base, mui, components, utilities; ```  // Right: pick one; component for simple element swaps, slots for slot-level swaps <Button component={RouterLink} to=\"/save\">Save</Button> // or <Button slots={{ root: MyRoot }}>Save</Button> ```" }

  {
    id = "05-053",
    number = 53,
    title = "Masonry (and friends: Modal, Drawer, Popover, Menu, Popper, Menubar, Native Color, Pigment CSS, Styled Engine)",
    local_chapter_file = "053-mui-masonry.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model", "`Masonry` and `MasonryItem`", "Modal layer (Modal, Drawer, Popover, Menu, Popper, Menubar)", "Theming layer", "Combining these in practice", "Common mistakes"],
    total_duration_seconds = 184,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzUzOlYxOjA1My1t",
        raw_key = "S5:C53:V1",
        title = "IQ: @layer and @apply: Combining utility classes",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/master-react-19-build-ecommerce-solutions-and-prepare-for-interviews-with-typescript-next-js-and-remix/iq-layer-apply-combining-utility-classes",
        duration_text = "01m37s",
        duration_seconds = 97,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Masonry (and friends: Modal, Drawer, Popover, Menu, Popper, Menubar, Native Color, Pigment CSS, Styled Engine), examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Master React 19, Build Ecommerce Solutions, and Prepare for Interviews with TypeScript, Next.js, and Remix",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2-3 · **Prerequisites:** Component Libraries: Material UI, Material UI component catalog, Dialog, Popper, MUI Timeline component  This chapter is a reference roundup built around the `Masonry` component, the variable-height, Pinterest-style column layout. Around `Masonry` sits the wider overlay ecosystem a `Masonry` host page usually pairs with: `Modal` (the foundation that `Dialog`, `Drawer`, `Menu`, and `Popover` all build on), `Drawer` for side navigation, `Popover` for anchored floating content, `Menu` and `Popper` for menus and tooltips, `Menubar` for top-level menus composed on top of Base UI's primitive, `Native Color` for opting the theme into CSS `color-mix()` and relative-color syntax, `Pigment CSS` for zero-runtime CSS-in-JS, and the styled-engine choice between Emotion and `@mui/styled-engine-sc`.", mental_model = "A `Masonry` packs variable-height blocks at a uniform width into N columns. Each new block goes into the shortest column, which avoids the \"ragged bottom\" you get with CSS grid when row heights differ. The component is not a CSS grid; it is a JS layout that measures every block and places it manually, which is what makes the no-vertical-gap packing possible.  Three details that drive every `Masonry` you write:", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled\nbun add @mui/icons-material\nbun add @mui/lab   # optional: Lab ships Masonry in some MUI versions", pitfalls = "`Masonry` measures and places each child in JavaScript. Wrapping the container in `display: grid` interferes with the column math. Use `Masonry`'s own column/spacing props, or use `Grid` (MUI 6 layout grid) when you want CSS grid behaviour.  Without it, the server renders an empty placeholder under each column and the page jumps on hydration. Set `defaultHeight` (and `defaultColumns`, `defaultSpacing`) to match the post-hydration layout.  `Modal` is the foundation; it does not bring visuals. Reach for the composite component that matches the user intent. Use `Modal` only when the surface is custom (a centred Card with bespoke padding and transitions).  `Popper` does not close on outside click. If you want a dropdown that dismisses itself, use `Popover` (or `Menu`). `Popper` is the right primitive for tooltips and non-modal hints.  // Right: uses theme-aware helper, produces color-mix(...) const hover = theme.alpha(theme.palette.primary.main, 0.08); ```" }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzUzOlYyOjA1My1t",
        raw_key = "S5:C53:V2",
        title = "Solution: Convert a basic button into a StyledButton",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/react-practice-for-beginners-build-and-modify-basic-components/solution-convert-a-basic-button-into-a-styledbutton",
        duration_text = "01m27s",
        duration_seconds = 87,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Masonry (and friends: Modal, Drawer, Popover, Menu, Popper, Menubar, Native Color, Pigment CSS, Styled Engine), examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "React Practice for Beginners: Build and Modify Basic Components",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2-3 · **Prerequisites:** Component Libraries: Material UI, Material UI component catalog, Dialog, Popper, MUI Timeline component  This chapter is a reference roundup built around the `Masonry` component, the variable-height, Pinterest-style column layout. Around `Masonry` sits the wider overlay ecosystem a `Masonry` host page usually pairs with: `Modal` (the foundation that `Dialog`, `Drawer`, `Menu`, and `Popover` all build on), `Drawer` for side navigation, `Popover` for anchored floating content, `Menu` and `Popper` for menus and tooltips, `Menubar` for top-level menus composed on top of Base UI's primitive, `Native Color` for opting the theme into CSS `color-mix()` and relative-color syntax, `Pigment CSS` for zero-runtime CSS-in-JS, and the styled-engine choice between Emotion and `@mui/styled-engine-sc`.", mental_model = "A `Masonry` packs variable-height blocks at a uniform width into N columns. Each new block goes into the shortest column, which avoids the \"ragged bottom\" you get with CSS grid when row heights differ. The component is not a CSS grid; it is a JS layout that measures every block and places it manually, which is what makes the no-vertical-gap packing possible.  Three details that drive every `Masonry` you write:", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled\nbun add @mui/icons-material\nbun add @mui/lab   # optional: Lab ships Masonry in some MUI versions", pitfalls = "`Masonry` measures and places each child in JavaScript. Wrapping the container in `display: grid` interferes with the column math. Use `Masonry`'s own column/spacing props, or use `Grid` (MUI 6 layout grid) when you want CSS grid behaviour.  Without it, the server renders an empty placeholder under each column and the page jumps on hydration. Set `defaultHeight` (and `defaultColumns`, `defaultSpacing`) to match the post-hydration layout.  `Modal` is the foundation; it does not bring visuals. Reach for the composite component that matches the user intent. Use `Modal` only when the surface is custom (a centred Card with bespoke padding and transitions).  `Popper` does not close on outside click. If you want a dropdown that dismisses itself, use `Popover` (or `Menu`). `Popper` is the right primitive for tooltips and non-modal hints.  // Right: uses theme-aware helper, produces color-mix(...) const hover = theme.alpha(theme.palette.primary.main, 0.08); ```" }
      },
    ]
  },

        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/master-react-19-build-ecommerce-solutions-and-prepare-for-interviews-with-typescript-next-js-and-remix/rendering-users-in-the-table",

        references = [

          { label = "Material UI: Customization & Theming", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/customization/theming/", description = "Palette, typography, and createTheme configuration." },

        notes = { definition = "**Prerequisites:** MUI Supported Components, Material UI component catalog, Material UI Color System, Popper, MUI Timeline component, Material UI Grid v2 migration  This chapter is a reference-heavy lesson. Its main subject is composing MUI's table parts into a usable data surface. The later sections collect related MUI API and migration patterns that often appear beside a table in a real application.", mental_model = "You meet this composition pattern in invoices, audit logs, product lists, and admin screens. It prevents three different bug classes: invalid table markup that confuses assistive technology, unstable row identity that makes reconciliation reuse the wrong DOM, and controls whose labels change while the displayed data stays unchanged.  Use a plain composed `Table` when you need a table-shaped view and you want control over markup. Move to MUI `DataGrid` when the feature set grows into a large data-management surface with virtualization, column menus, richer filtering, editing, or server-side models. `DataGrid` is a separate product with a different API. It is not a drop-in replacement for the composition tree above.", code_example = "import Table from \"@mui/material/Table\";\nimport TableBody from \"@mui/material/TableBody\";\nimport TableCell from \"@mui/material/TableCell\";\nimport TableHead from \"@mui/material/TableHead\";\nimport TableRow from \"@mui/material/TableRow\";\n\nexport function SmallTable() {\n  return (\n    <Table aria-label=\"two products\">\n      <TableHead>\n        <TableRow>\n          <TableCell component=\"th\" scope=\"col\">\n            Product\n          </TableCell>\n          <TableCell component=\"th\" scope=\"col\" align=\"right\">\n            Stock\n          </TableCell>\n        </TableRow>\n      </TableHead>\n      <TableBody>\n        <TableRow key=\"pen\">\n          <TableCell component=\"th\" scope=\"row\">\n            Pen\n          </TableCell>\n          <TableCell align=\"right\">18</TableCell>\n        </TableRow>\n        <TableRow key=\"notebook\">\n          <TableCell component=\"th\" scope=\"row\">\n            Notebook\n          </TableCell>\n          <TableCell align=\"right\">7</TableCell>\n        </TableRow>\n      </TableBody>\n    </Table>\n  );\n}", pitfalls = "This compiles, but sorting causes React to reuse the wrong row instance when a row contains local state or an input. React may log:  That warning appears when there is no key. Using `key={index}` silences it but still gives rows identities tied to their current positions. Use `key={row.id}` with an ID that does not change when the order changes.  This broken TypeScript is intentional:  With MUI's `TableCellProps`, TypeScript reports:  Use `align=\"center\"`, or use `sx` when you need a CSS alignment that is outside the component prop's contract." }
      },

    local_chapter_file = "055-merging-branded-and-application-themes.md",
    key_concepts = ["Hook: two paint boxes", "The concept", "Predict the render", "Component reference roundup", "Common mistakes", "Why this matters"],
    total_duration_seconds = 1304,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzU1OlYxOjA1NS1t",
        raw_key = "S5:C55:V1",
        title = "Client/server side rendering: SSG — ISR concepts and comparisons",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/master-react-19-and-next-js-16-with-hands-on-projects-and-real-world-applications/client-server-side-rendering-ssg-isr-concepts-and-comparisons",
        duration_text = "11m16s",
        duration_seconds = 676,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Merging branded and application themes, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Master React 19 and Next.js 16 with Hands-On Projects and Real-World Applications",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 3 · **Prerequisites:** MUI Table Composition, Component Libraries: Material UI, useActionState + React 19 Form Actions, MUI Masonry, Material UI theme provider", mental_model = "A shared brand package can serve several products while each product chooses its palette, navigation, and component behavior. Explicit merging makes that boundary visible in code. Ordered style arrays prevent a release from silently removing focus, hover, or disabled-state rules. Avoiding an eager deep merge also keeps theme construction out of the render path and removes avoidable startup work.  The component roundup gives the merged theme real consumers: a `Drawer` or `Menu` for navigation, a `Dialog` or `Backdrop` for attention, and controlled input components for preferences. Each consumer reads the final theme from `ThemeProvider`, so a change to brand tokens reaches the whole tree without prop drilling.", code_example = "import { createTheme } from '@mui/material/styles';\nimport type { ThemeOptions } from '@mui/material/styles';\n\nconst brand: ThemeOptions = {\n  palette: { primary: { main: '#111827' } },\n  shape: { borderRadius: 6 },\n};\n\nconst appTheme = createTheme({\n  ...brand,\n  palette: {\n    ...brand.palette,\n    primary: { ...brand.palette?.primary, main: '#2563eb' },\n  },\n});", pitfalls = "The code compiles, but the brand's `minWidth` and pseudo-class rules vanish. Spread the branded `MuiButton` entry and put the two roots in an array.  Spread `brand.palette.primary` before changing `main`, and repeat that pattern for nested groups you customize.  If you render `<Switch checked={enabled} />` without an `onChange` that updates `enabled`, the control looks stuck. React does not change the prop for you. Keep the source of truth in state.  `ToggleButtonGroup` calls its handler with `null` after the selected button is clicked again. Passing that value into a state typed as `View` produces `Type 'null' is not assignable to type 'View'`. Check `next !== null` before updating." }
      },
      {
        id = "UzU6QzU1OlYyOjA1NS1t",

        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Merging branded and application themes, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",

        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 3 · **Prerequisites:** MUI Table Composition, Component Libraries: Material UI, useActionState + React 19 Form Actions, MUI Masonry, Material UI theme provider", mental_model = "A shared brand package can serve several products while each product chooses its palette, navigation, and component behavior. Explicit merging makes that boundary visible in code. Ordered style arrays prevent a release from silently removing focus, hover, or disabled-state rules. Avoiding an eager deep merge also keeps theme construction out of the render path and removes avoidable startup work.  The component roundup gives the merged theme real consumers: a `Drawer` or `Menu` for navigation, a `Dialog` or `Backdrop` for attention, and controlled input components for preferences. Each consumer reads the final theme from `ThemeProvider`, so a change to brand tokens reaches the whole tree without prop drilling.", code_example = "import { createTheme } from '@mui/material/styles';\nimport type { ThemeOptions } from '@mui/material/styles';\n\nconst brand: ThemeOptions = {\n  palette: { primary: { main: '#111827' } },\n  shape: { borderRadius: 6 },\n};\n\nconst appTheme = createTheme({\n  ...brand,\n  palette: {\n    ...brand.palette,\n    primary: { ...brand.palette?.primary, main: '#2563eb' },\n  },\n});", pitfalls = "The code compiles, but the brand's `minWidth` and pseudo-class rules vanish. Spread the branded `MuiButton` entry and put the two roots in an array.  Spread `brand.palette.primary` before changing `main`, and repeat that pattern for nested groups you customize.  If you render `<Switch checked={enabled} />` without an `onChange` that updates `enabled`, the control looks stuck. React does not change the prop for you. Keep the source of truth in state.  `ToggleButtonGroup` calls its handler with `null` after the selected button is clicked again. Passing that value into a state typed as `View` produces `Type 'null' is not assignable to type 'View'`. Check `next !== null` before updating." }

    ]

    id = "05-056",

    title = "MUI Tabs and Tab components",
    local_chapter_file = "056-mui-tabs-and-tab-components.md",
    key_concepts = ["A settings binder", "The concept: a coordinated selection value", "Minimal example", "Predict the render", "Form-control reference", "Theme and migration reference"],

        title = "IQ: Rendering dynamic values from array and key attribute",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/master-react-19-build-ecommerce-solutions-and-prepare-for-interviews-with-typescript-next-js-and-remix/iq-rendering-dynamic-values-from-array-and-key-attribute",

        references = [
          { label = "Material UI Official Guide: Getting Started", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/getting-started/", description = "Production-ready component library implementing Material Design." },
          { label = "Material UI: Customization & Theming", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/customization/theming/", description = "Palette, typography, and createTheme configuration." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Prerequisites:** useActionState + React 19 Form Actions, useContext, Forms with React Hook Form, Component Libraries: Material UI, Material UI component catalog, MUI Table Composition, MUI Timeline component, Material UI MCP documentation server, Chip  This is a reference-heavy chapter. It starts with tabs, then collects MUI form controls and styling APIs that use the same two ideas: controlled values and context shared by a parent component.", mental_model = "You meet these components in settings screens, onboarding flows, search filters, review forms, and admin tools. A shared controlled-value model makes their behavior predictable, while the MUI parent handles focus and keyboard details that are easy to omit in hand-built widgets.  The context hooks and slot APIs also create supported customization boundaries. They prevent code from reaching into private markup or copying internal state. Migration work then becomes a change of package, styling engine, or extraction model without rewriting the form's domain state.", code_example = "import { useState } from \"react\";\nimport type { SyntheticEvent } from \"react\";\nimport Tab from \"@mui/material/Tab\";\nimport Tabs from \"@mui/material/Tabs\";\n\nexport function AccountTabs() {\n  const [active, setActive] = useState<\"profile\" | \"security\">(\"profile\");\n\n  const selectTab = (_event: SyntheticEvent, next: string): void => {\n    if (next === \"profile\" || next === \"security\") {\n      setActive(next);\n    }\n  };\n\n  return (\n    <section>\n      <Tabs value={active} onChange={selectTab} aria-label=\"Account sections\">\n        <Tab\n          value=\"profile\"\n          label=\"Profile\"\n          id=\"account-tab-profile\"\n          aria-controls=\"account-panel-profile\"\n        />\n        <Tab\n          value=\"security\"\n          label=\"Security\"\n          id=\"account-tab-security\"\n          aria-controls=\"account-panel-security\"\n        />\n      </Tabs>\n      <div\n        role=\"tabpanel\"\n        id={`account-panel-${active}`}\n        aria-labelledby={`account-tab-${active}`}\n      >\n        {active === \"profile\" ? \"Edit your name\" : \"Change your password\"}\n      </div>\n    </section>\n  );\n}", pitfalls = "A `Tab` with `aria-controls=\"security-panel\"` and a panel with `id=\"profile-panel\"` compiles and does not log a runtime error. The accessibility tree loses the relationship. Derive both IDs from the same tab value, or use the Lab components to wire the relationship.  This deliberately broken TypeScript drops the clear state:  Use `useState<number | null>(3)` or reject `null` deliberately when the product requires a rating.  An `Autocomplete` value loaded from one request may have the same fields as an option loaded from another request, but it is a different object reference. The component cannot match it by identity and may log:  Add `isOptionEqualToValue={(option, value) => option.id === value.id}`. Keep IDs stable and unique." }
      },
      {

    id = "05-057",
    number = 57,
    title = "Pigment CSS (zero-runtime CSS-in-JS) (and a roundup: the `css` / `globalCss` / `keyframes` / `styled` APIs, `variants` vs runtime callbacks, `withPigment` plugins for Vite and Next.js, `extendTheme` and `vars`, the `sx` prop, polymorphic MUI wrappers, MUI v7 and v9 upgrade steps, `prettier-plugin-tailwindcss` sorting and collapse, `@tailwindui/react` `<Transition>`, shadcn UI's copy-paste model, CSS Modules, component extraction, the component tree, `createRoot`, and one-way data flow)",
    local_chapter_file = "057-pigment-css-zero-runtime-css-in-js.md",
    key_concepts = ["Mental model", "Install and configure Pigment CSS", "The `css` API", "The `globalCss` API", "The `keyframes` API", "The `styled` API"],
    total_duration_seconds = 491,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzU3OlYxOjA1Ny1w",
        raw_key = "S5:C57:V1",
        title = "Data loader and products component",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/build-modern-web-apps-with-react-hooks-state-management-and-apis-using-vite-or-next-js/data-loader-and-products-component",
        duration_text = "06m00s",
        duration_seconds = 360,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Pigment CSS (zero-runtime CSS-in-JS) (and a roundup: the `css` / `globalCss` / `keyframes` / `styled` APIs, `variants` vs runtime callbacks, `withPigment` plugins for Vite and Next.js, `extendTheme` and `vars`, the `sx` prop, polymorphic MUI wrappers, MUI v7 and v9 upgrade steps, `prettier-plugin-tailwindcss` sorting and collapse, `@tailwindui/react` `<Transition>`, shadcn UI's copy-paste model, CSS Modules, component extraction, the component tree, `createRoot`, and one-way data flow), examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Build Modern Web Apps with React, Hooks, State Management, and APIs Using Vite or Next.js",
        references = [
          { label = "Material UI Official Guide: Getting Started", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/getting-started/", description = "Production-ready component library implementing Material Design." },
          { label = "Material UI: Customization & Theming", url = "https://mui.com/material-ui/customization/theming/", description = "Palette, typography, and createTheme configuration." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 1-4 · **Prerequisites:** Chip, Material UI Color System, MUI Table Composition, Components and Props, Domain-first data model planning, React Canary Release Channel  This chapter opens with **Pigment CSS**, MUI's zero-runtime CSS-in-JS engine: it pre-compiles styles at build time, emits real CSS, and is the path forward if you want a `styled`-like authoring experience while staying React Server Components compatible. Around that headline sits the rest of the styling vocabulary that the longer Styling section has been pulling together. The Pigment CSS APIs (`css`, `globalCss`, `keyframes`, `styled`, `variants`, runtime callbacks) are covered in depth, the Vite and Next.js plugins are explained as the entry points, the `extendTheme` / `vars` pattern is shown as the bridge between the build-time theme and runtime CSS variables, and the `sx` prop is described as the inline-style escape hatch that compiles down to `className` + `style` at build time. The roundup that follows covers the older Material UI `TypographyProps`-based polymorphic wrapper pattern, the v7 and v9 upgrade steps (package exports, slot standardisation, CSS layers, Grid renaming, accessibility improvements), the two halves of `prettier-plugin-tailwindcss` (fixed-order sorting and the v1.7+ collapse pass for whitespace and duplicates), the early `@tailwindui/react` `<Transition>` primitive, the shadcn UI copy-paste model that re-shapes how a team owns its component library, CSS Modules as a third styling option next to Pigment and Tailwind, the Wieruch component-tree / `createRoot` / one-way data flow foundations, and the extraction heuristic that keeps a component understandable.", mental_model = "A \"CSS-in-JS\" library writes a stylesheet that ships JavaScript to the browser, then inserts rules into the DOM on render. Emotion (the engine behind `@mui/material`) does this. The runtime cost is real: every render has to diff the styles, and the styles themselves have to live in the bundle. Pigment CSS inverts the relationship. The `css` and `styled` calls are still TypeScript, but a build plugin (Vite or Next.js) reads the source, emits a real CSS file, and rewrites the call sites so the runtime only does `className` lookup. The browser sees a `<link rel=\"stylesheet\">` and a class string, nothing more.  The build-time flavour has three consequences worth naming up front:", code_example = "bun add @mui/material @emotion/react @emotion/styled\nbun add @pigment-css/react\nbun add --save-dev @pigment-css/vite-plugin", pitfalls = "**1. Forgetting the stylesheet import.** The Pigment CSS build plugin writes the CSS file, but the bundler does not know to serve it. The browser shows a flash of unstyled content followed by the layout, because the CSS is loaded but its `<link>` is in the wrong place. Fix:  createRoot(document.getElementById(\"root\")!).render(<App />); ```  **2. Using `useTheme` from `@mui/material/styles` instead of `@mui/material-pigment-css`.** The first is the Emotion runtime hook and returns the resolved theme object; the second is the Pigment CSS hook and returns the `theme.vars` proxy. The types are similar but the values differ. The Emotion hook works in Pigment projects only because the Emotion engine is still installed; the values are the resolved objects, not the CSS variables. The Pigment hook is the one that matches the generated CSS.  // Right import { useTheme } from \"@mui/material-pigment-css\"; ```  **3. Using a callback for an enumerable prop.** A callback that reads `props.variant` and switches on a known list of strings is slower and less obvious than the `variants` array:" }
      },

    local_chapter_file = "058-shadcn-ui-base-ui-tailwind-component-library.md",

    total_duration_seconds = 1174,
    videos = [
      {
        id = "UzU6QzU4OlYxOjA1OC1z",
        raw_key = "S5:C58:V1",
        title = "Styled Components: Part 2",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/build-modern-web-apps-with-react-hooks-state-management-and-apis-using-vite-or-next-js/styled-components-part-2",
        duration_text = "05m56s",
        duration_seconds = 356,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Shadcn UI + Base UI + Tailwind component library (and a roundup: `class-variance-authority` + `tailwind-merge`, Base UI as the headless primitive layer, the Spotlight personal site template, stable keys for list identity, styled-components as a tagged-template CSS-in-JS option, `.attrs()` for non-class props, extending styled components, `babel-plugin-styled-components`, the Pocket template's Framer Motion animations, Thinking-in-React decomposition, Vite CSS Modules, `@tailwindplus/elements` custom elements, and the Wieruch component-tree / `createRoot` / one-way data flow foundations), examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",

        ],
        notes = { definition = "**Difficulty:** 2-3 · **Prerequisites:** JSX and Rendering, Components and Props, preconnect, Chip, React Major Version Policy, Vite modern web constraints, React Canary Release Channel  This chapter opens with the **Shadcn UI + Base UI + Tailwind component library** model: you don't `npm install` a styled component library anymore, you run an init command, the CLI copies the source for each primitive into `src/components/ui/`, and you own the result. Around that headline sits the rest of the styling vocabulary the Styling section has been collecting: `class-variance-authority` (`cva`) for typed variant strings, `tailwind-merge` to dedupe conflicting utilities, Base UI as the headless accessibility layer underneath, the Tailwind UI Spotlight personal-site template as the canonical \"put it all together\" example, stable keys for list identity in the project list, the styled-components library as the alternative CSS-in-JS authoring style (tagged-template literals, `.attrs()` for non-class props, extending via `styled(BaseComponent)`), the `babel-plugin-styled-components` companion that makes DevTools class names readable, the Pocket template's Framer Motion entrance animations, the Thinking-in-React decomposition heuristic, Vite CSS Modules as the third styling option (alongside Tailwind and CSS-in-JS), the framework-agnostic `@tailwindplus/elements` custom-element library, and the Wieruch component-tree / `createRoot` / one-way data flow foundations that hold the whole stack together.", mental_model = "A traditional component library ships as a black-box npm package. You import `Button` from `@mui/material`, you call `<Button>`, and the source for `Button` lives somewhere in `node_modules`. Customising it means either (a) wrapping it in your own component that overrides `sx` or `className` props, or (b) forking the package and shipping a private build.  The Shadcn UI model inverts the relationship. Instead of a black box, you get a folder of small files you wrote (or the CLI wrote for you), styled with `cva` variants, layered on top of unstyled accessibility primitives from Base UI, and rendered with Tailwind utility classes. The \"library\" lives in your repo. The dependency on `@base-ui-components/react` is the only third-party surface; everything else is your own source.", code_example = "// src/components/ui/card.tsx\nimport { forwardRef, type HTMLAttributes } from \"react\";\nimport { cn } from \"@/lib/utils\";\n\nexport const Card = forwardRef<HTMLDivElement, HTMLAttributes<HTMLDivElement>>(\n  ({ className, ...props }, ref) => (\n    <div\n      ref={ref}\n      className={cn(\n        \"rounded-lg border bg-card text-card-foreground shadow-sm\",\n        className,\n      )}\n      {...props}\n    />\n  ),\n);\nCard.displayName = \"Card\";", pitfalls = "If a caller passes `className=\"bg-red-500\"`, the rendered class is `\"... bg-primary ... bg-red-500\"`. The browser applies the **last** declaration in the stylesheet, not the last class in the string. With Tailwind, all utility classes live in the same generated layer at the same specificity, so the order in the class string *does* matter, but only when Tailwind's ordering rules are followed. For un-common pairs, you get the wrong winner, or you get both utilities emitted and the visual is whichever class the JIT put later in the sheet. `twMerge` removes the ambiguity by parsing the utilities and dropping the conflicting earlier one.  `cva` returns a *function* from a config. If the config object is recreated on every render, the returned function is a new identity on every render. Any consumer that memoizes against the `button` reference (`useMemo`, `useCallback`) sees a fresh dependency each render. The standard pattern is to declare the `cva` config at module scope and export the function (here, `buttonVariants`), then call it inside the component.  The string union is a copy. When you add a `link` variant to `cva`, the type does not follow, and the component silently accepts the new variant but the type still says `variant?: \"default\" | \"destructive\" | \"outline\"`. `VariantProps<typeof buttonVariants>` re-derives the union from the `cva` config, so the type stays in sync.  If you need a dialog that matches your bespoke design and you reach for `BaseDialog` directly, you have skipped the Shadcn layer that already wraps Base UI with the agreed tokens (`bg-background`, `text-foreground`, focus ring). The right move is to either (a) use the Shadcn `Dialog` (which is `BaseDialog` styled), or (b) if you need an unstyled primitive for a brand-new surface, reach for Base UI directly and add your own `cva` wrapper. Mixing the two without wrapping usually ends with two layers of styling fighting." }
      },

    key_concepts = ["The tagged template, briefly", "Typing the theme", "`ThemeProvider` injects the value through context", "`createGlobalStyle` reuses the same theme", "Transient `$` props keep style-only data off the DOM", "Linting the CSS inside the templates"],
    total_duration_seconds = 976,

        id = "UzU6QzU5OlYxOjA1OS10",
        raw_key = "S5:C59:V1",
        title = "Connecting mock external data into stories with Storybook for components",
        url = "https://www.linkedin.com/learning/building-ui-components-with-storybook-master-react-components-and-ui-design-with-storybook-for-better-efficien/connecting-mock-external-data-into-stories-with-storybook-for-components",
        duration_text = "14m44s",
        duration_seconds = 884,
        description = "In-depth walkthrough of Themed styled-components with TypeScript, examining real-world production implementations, rendering performance, and TypeScript contracts.",
        category_tag = "Building UI Components with Storybook: Master React Components and UI Design with Storybook for Better Efficiency",
        references = [
          { label = "Tailwind CSS Official Documentation", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/utility-first", description = "Utility-first CSS framework for rapid UI development." },
          { label = "Tailwind CSS: Responsive Design", url = "https://tailwindcss.com/docs/responsive-design", description = "Mobile-first breakpoint modifiers (sm, md, lg, xl, 2xl)." },
        ],
        notes = { definition = "A styled-component is a React component whose styles live in a tagged template literal right next to the component. You write CSS in the template, and the library returns a real component you render like any other:  const Button = styled.button`   padding: 8px 16px;   border-radius: 6px;   border: none;   background: #0a84ff;   color: white; `;", mental_model = "A themed component library is two systems working together. The theme plus typed templates give you styles that read from one design source and cannot silently misspell a token. The state architecture (minimal state, a clear owner, lifting, and the controlled-versus- uncontrolled choice) decides what those styles react to and keeps the values that drive them in exactly one place. Get the theme typed and the ownership right, and a tab strip, a paginator, a stepper, and a form all end up with the same predictable shape: value down, change up, styles derived from the theme.", code_example = "import styled from \"styled-components\";\n\nconst Button = styled.button`\n  padding: 8px 16px;\n  border-radius: 6px;\n  border: none;\n  background: #0a84ff;\n  color: white;\n`;\n\n// <Button>Save</Button>  ->  a <button> with those styles attached", pitfalls = "Without `ThemeProps<AppTheme>` (or a `DefaultTheme` augmentation), `props.theme` widens to `any` and `colours` (British misspelling) sails through the compiler. Type the theme once and the typo becomes an error at the call site.  Rename it `$filled`. The transient prefix keeps the prop out of the rendered attributes while still feeding the interpolation.  Keeping a `filteredList` in `useState` and updating it in an effect gives you two sources of truth that drift. Compute the filtered list from the source list and the query during render. State holds only the inputs; the output is derived.  If two siblings each keep their own copy of the \"same\" value, they will disagree the moment one updates without the other. Lift the value into their common parent and pass it down. One owner, one value.  Controlled state on a field that no other part of the UI reads adds a render per keystroke for no benefit. If only the final value matters, leave the field uncontrolled (a `defaultValue`, or React Hook Form's `register`) and read the value when you submit." }
      },

This section covers **Modern Styling: Tailwind CSS & Material UI** (Part 3: UI Systems & Server Communication), featuring **118 curated LinkedIn Learning video lessons**, comprehensive pop-up chapter study notes with **Ask AI**, and official React documentation references across all 59 core chapters.
